# Decisions

The author's decisions about the Open Moon (Terluna), with where each is recorded
and whether it stands. A later decision replaces an earlier one, and the
replaced entry names what replaced it. Modelling choices, reference values and
results appear here only where the author adopted them.

Sources:

- **July**: the recovered canon of the July 2026 knowledge bundle
  ([archive/july_2026_knowledge_bundle/](../archive/july_2026_knowledge_bundle/README.md);
  its file `01_RECOVERED_CANON_AND_CORRECTIONS.md` and `data/decision_log.csv`).
- **Sep 4**: the user requirements of the September feasibility report
  ([baselines/feasibility/report.md](baselines/feasibility/report.md), section 1).
- **Sep 9**: the September protection report
  ([protection/report.md](../protection/report.md), section 3).
- **Sep 19**: the 19 September 2026 industry discussion, known through a
  reconstruction written on 2026-09-26
  ([engineering/reference/industrial_architecture/](../engineering/reference/industrial_architecture/README.md),
  `RECOVERED_INDUSTRIAL_ARCHITECTURE.md`, section 8). The author confirmed its
  six traffic-safety rules on 2026-09-26.
- **Author**: decisions the author stated in working sessions, with the date.
- The conservation study keeps its own decisions, D1–D11, in
  [its README](studies/conservation/README.md#4-decisions); they are not repeated here.

## The world

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The world is Earth's own Moon, transformed in place, and the project is called Terluna. | July | Stands. The paper ensemble's working title is *Constructing and Sustaining an Open Moon*. |
| The Moon keeps its orbit, gravity, synchronous rotation and 29.53-day solar cycle. Life and culture adapt to the long day and night, storing resources across them. | July | Stands |
| Construction within 500 years of 2026, and operation for at least 10⁹ years. | Sep 4 | Stands |
| Plan on near horizons as well as the billion-year frame: the first thousand years, including the 500-year build, and the several thousand years after. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("Our sustainability plans measure themselves in the billions of years. We should probably start thinking more locally too. The first thousand years (including setup), and say the next... several thousands of years.") | Research direction; worked first in provisioning, resources and ecology on 2026-10-09 |
| An open atmosphere with no pressure dome over the Moon. | Sep 4 | Stands. Local enclosed places, such as the undersea Tranquility community (conservation D6), are separate. |
| Importing carbon, potassium and any other element is part of the plan. The design problem is the steady state: what carries each element downhill into the seas and sediments, and which local processes, biological, physical or engineered, return it. That is the return limb the Moon's missing geology leaves open, with carbon worked out together with the mineral and nutrient cycles. | Author, 2026-09-25 ("we would obviously ship enough in to accommodate the growing biosphere. But once it's grown and established and the cycle is in place it would be fairly sustained by local processes esp. with sustainability of other things like figuring out mineral cycle?"); 2026-09-27 (shipping in potassium like everything else "was never the issue") | Stands ([resources](../resources/README.md); the return limb in [research/studies/lunar_cycle_ecology](studies/lunar_cycle_ecology/README.md)) |
| A functioning phosphorus cycle is required across aerial life, surface waters, land and sediments, including nutrients exported in harvested food. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("We'll definitely need a real phosphorus cycle") and request to evaluate the cross-domain discussion | Research requirement; [resources/phosphorus.md](../resources/phosphorus.md) quantifies required retention and return with finite reservoirs. No tested recycling fraction, delivery architecture or population allocation is adopted. |

## Light and time

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Dawn and dusk follow the light on the ground under the solved clear sky. Practical dusk, while ordinary outdoor activity needs no artificial light, runs from sunset until the light falls to the level at which Earth's civil twilight ends: 2.98 lux in the same solver, with the Sun 6° down on Earth. The Moon reaches it with the Sun 41.4° below the horizon, 82 hours after sunset at the equator and 98 hours at 30° latitude; poleward of 48.6° a clear night never falls that low. Day vision for an 18% grey surface (CIE 191:2010) holds for the first 46 hours at the equator, to a depression of 23.6°. Dawn mirrors dusk. Where the Earth stands high, its light holds the night near the level: 2.3–2.9 lux at the darkest at three nearside coasts with the Earth 43–74° up, with the Earth's observed visual geometric albedo of 0.242 (Robinson et al. 2025). | July; revised 2026-10-04 at the author's request, from the sea-appearance study's [lighting calendar](studies/sea_appearance/README.md#the-lighting-calendar) | Stands. The July canon gave about five hours (2.54° of solar motion at the equator), replacing a 30-hour figure, and was held on 2026-09-26 against the ensemble plan's nominal 12.5-hour sunset pending a photometric definition for the actual atmosphere. On 2026-10-04 the author asked that this row follow the current understanding. The threshold carries civil twilight's meaning to the Moon through the solver's Earth control ([optical comfort](studies/optical_comfort/README.md)). Clear sky, from a horizontally uniform column without refraction; clouds come in the sea-appearance study's results by regime. |
| Earthlight follows the Earth's measured visual brightness: geometric albedo 0.242 (Robinson et al. 2025, a synthesis of ground and spacecraft photometry) with their analytic fit to its phase curve, across the shared constants, illumination, the immersion and the studies. It replaces the long-quoted 0.367, which rests on Danjon's extrapolated earthshine. A full Earth gives 7.8 lux above the air; nearside nights under a high Earth keep 2.3–2.9 lux at their darkest. | Author, 2026-10-04; [illumination/earthlight](../illumination/earthlight/README.md) | Stands. The Earth's turning continents and clouds move its light by about 10% within a day. |
| How the seas look is studied by day, through the long evening, under earthlight and in the far side's twilit night, in this order: a lighting calendar of six coasts with a map of where the Earth stands over the seas, the surface's reflection and the water's colour, results by regime, then renderings of four coasts (western Oceanus Procellarum by Russell, the eastern Smythii headland, southern Mare Nubium and the South Pole–Aitken coast by Mare Ingenii). The far-side rendering shows the twilit night first, as the more representative case. Cloud ice keeps the cloud scheme's own sizes, with 140 µm as a check. | Author, 2026-10-04; [research/studies/sea_appearance](studies/sea_appearance/README.md) | Stands. The calendar found the twilight wrapping round the Moon: at latitude φ the Sun sinks at most 90° − φ below the horizon, so only seas near the far side's equator grow dark around local midnight, and the author chose the twilit night for the far-side coast. The calendar also gave dawn and dusk their photometric definition (the row above). |

## Atmosphere

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Surface pressure 1.2 atm (121,590 Pa). | Sep 4 | Stands; the design pressure of the climate runs. The July bundle's 80 kPa was its own reference value. |
| An exobase near 250 K is preferred, with 260 K a candidate limit. | Sep 4 | Stands. The solved upper air behind the titania film gives 142–193 K before the sky's Lyman-alpha glow is counted ([middle atmosphere](../atmosphere/middle_atmosphere/README.md)). |
| Water, nitrogen and oxygen come from the Solar-System-wide resource operation. | Conservation D4 | Stands; replaced the September baseline's oxygen from lunar rock. |
| Plan the nitrogen's extraction in detail, so that it is feasible and sustainable and protects the major celestial bodies as the conservation study's principle 5 plans: water and gases from ordinary icy bodies, with Saturn's rings, Titan's atmosphere and the ocean worlds untouched. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("we still need to do more exact plan for extraction in a way that is feasible and sustainable and protects the major celestial bodies as planned") | Research direction, with the resources domain ([resources](../resources/README.md)). A rough count on 9 October: the Kuiper belt holds about 2% of Earth's mass (Pitjeva & Pitjev 2018), roughly 7×10²² kg of it outside the large dwarf planets; at a few tenths of a percent to a few percent nitrogen, bound in ammonia, ammonium salts and organics, that is 80–800 times the air's 2.5×10¹⁸ kg of N2. Supplying it means processing 10²⁰–10²¹ kg of small bodies, with the ammonia cracked to N2 at the source and its hydrogen kept there; the bodies mined for the water may bring much of it. |
| Consider how the array connects to industry in general and to the resource flows of the continuing Solar-System-wide extraction, on the near horizons; industry on platforms in orbit spares the Moon's heat budget. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("let's not lose the industry in general point... So we should also this round consider how the array interfaces with the general industry, resource flow from continued solar extraction, etc.") | Research direction; [industry](../resources/industry.md) takes it up |
| Atmospheric layers take descriptive names, never L1–L5, which stay reserved for Lagrange points. | July | Stands |
| "Gravity wave" means an atmospheric buoyancy wave; write "atmospheric gravity wave" on first use. | July | Stands |
| A band at 35–45 km was placed as the top of the main troposphere, provisionally, pending a climate model. | July | Settled on 2026-09-26: the band stays as a named flight band, and its physics comes from the models. The 1-D column puts the tropopause near 86 km (288 K surface); in the 5% climate the band holds air at −10 to −20 °C and 0.58–0.48 atm ([climate/gcm](../climate/gcm/README.md)). |

## Water and climate

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Standing water covers 28% of the surface. | [shared/scenarios/water.json](../shared/scenarios/water.json); conservation D10 | Stands for the seas: selected on 2026-09-25 within the author's 25–35% range, at one common level. The rain-fed lakes above that level, added on 29 September, cover a further 10.3% (the GCM's surface carried 12.0% until 19 October), so standing water is about 38%; on 19 October the author kept the seas at 28% and counted the lakes on top ([below](#the-design-case-and-the-models-earth-limits-1920-october-2026)). |
| The Open Moon has a mix of climates and biomes, with the hottest days pleasantly warm. | Author, 2026-09-25 ("I still want summer days at the hottest to be nice and warm, but definitely more of a mix of climates and biomes.") | Stands as an objective. The corrected design case's land annual means lie within about 3–5 K and no 3-day mean anywhere falls below 14 °C, so the mix comes mostly from water and light ([biosphere/ecology](../biosphere/ecology/README.md)). Near 307 K, where the GCM settles with its corrected longwave and ice optics, the land's surface spans 32–35 °C with the poles warmest ([climate/gcm](../climate/gcm/README.md#where-the-corrected-moon-settles)) |
| The shield passes 5% less sunlight than the titania stack at every wavelength, and the Moon settles near 295 K (294.9 K with the GCM's Sun, tilt and sunlight split corrected; 294 K before). | [climate/gcm](../climate/gcm/README.md) | Stands; chosen on 2026-09-26 and kept on 2026-09-29 after the GCM's correction. |
| The corrected climate's drier land and smaller lakes are accepted for the comfort it brings. Rain-fed lakes cover 10.3% of the Moon (11.6% before the GCM's correction), 42% of the land gets under 0.5 mm of rain a day, and the GCM's comfortable hours over land rise, most of all poleward of 45°. | Author, 2026-09-29; [climate/gcm](../climate/gcm/README.md), [geography](../geography/README.md) | Stands. CM1's check disagrees: its three flat rings (2026-09-30) find no low-lying land comfortable, and corrected for height 42 comfortable hours per lunar day on their land against the GCM's 387 at the same places, with comfort only on high ground poleward of 30°. Which model is right about the air near the ground over land is open ([climate/crm](../climate/crm/README.md)); comfort is carried as the range between them while the climate work pauses (Research practice). |
| The climate investigated so far is the planned climate: the corrected GCM design case near 295 K (the rows above), around which the project has built its designs and experiences. The project works to keep it: what the shield and the array add to the Moon's heat is answered so that the Moon keeps the climate as decided. The ring fleet's own infrared glow is answered with added solar protection, by redirecting the glow away from the Moon, or both. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("we should try to consider the investigated climate as the planned climate going forward and work to ensure that we can manage the climate as decided. So we have to deal with that warming either through added solar protection and redirecting that infrared glow") | Its temperature superseded on 19 October: the resort at 301 K is the design climate ([below](#the-design-climate-and-the-waters-19-october-2026)), and the added heat is still answered so that the Moon keeps the climate as decided. Until then it stood, the climate work paused (Research practice). The glow and the means of answering it belong to the solar-shield work ([its row](#solar-shield-and-habitat-array)). Since 17 October, with the GCM's longwave refitted and its cold cloud given ice optics, the design sunlight takes the Moon near 307 K, and the author weighs keeping a warmer Moon ([the target climate](#the-target-climate-17-october-2026)). |
| The CM1 rings run flat and are read for low-lying land; the highlands' climate comes from their results corrected for height (about 1 °C cooler per kilometre with lower dewpoints, from the terrain ring's first eleven days, checked against the GCM's highland cells). Rings with terrain are not used: in two dimensions air forced over 8-km mountains turned into gales along the whole ring. The highlands' own weather (winds up and down slopes, rain on the slopes facing the wind, cold air pooling in valleys) waits for a possible 3-D box, 385 km square, over high ground on `ring_70_45e`'s path, forced by that ring and decided after its results. | Author, 2026-09-29; [climate/crm](../climate/crm/README.md) | Stands. The flat rings and the height correction were done on 2026-09-30. The 3-D box ran on 2026-10-01 at the author's go-ahead, at 44.7° S, 246.1° E, and again on 2026-10-02 with each column held and forced at its own height, the first run having taken its forcing from one corner column at 2.9 km: dry and sunny, with 183 comfortable hours per lunar day rising with height from 82 below 1 km to 262 at 5-6 km, muggy mornings and some cloud at sunrise on the low ground, slope winds and mild cold pooling at night ([climate/crm](../climate/crm/README.md), "A box over the highlands"). |
| Study the Open Moon's atmospheric electricity to a depth comparable with the project's other subjects of similar importance: storm charge and fields, lightning (rate, kinds, size, energy, nitrogen fixed), the air's conductivity, the global circuit and transient luminous events. In stages: (1) the CM1 storms already run and the fall-speed pair, with an ionization and conductivity column, setting storm generator currents against what the air conducts at breakdown; (2) an electrified CM1 storm, with the NSSL microphysics patched for lunar gravity and the electrical physics following WRF-ELEC, benchmarked on Earth; (3) the global circuit and transient luminous events. ROCKE-3D stays a separate background-climate check. | Author, 2026-10-02; [atmospheric-electricity plan](studies/atmospheric_electricity/README.md) | Stands. Revised the same day to this order, with the goal set by the author. Stage 1 done on 2026-10-03: some storm columns reach the breakdown field under every charging law tried, because lunar cloud, ionized only by muons, conducts about 10⁻¹⁶ S/m and holds charge for a day; how often turns on the charge slow graupel separates at 1–1.5 m/s, from most storms within minutes (Takahashi; measurements at 1.2 m/s) to the strongest after hours (Saunders and Peck). Stage 2's electrified CM1 was built and benchmarked on an Earth supercell on 2026-10-03, and on 2026-10-04 took WRF-ELEC's sedimentation sub-steps and branched lightning, in cloud and to ground; the electrified equatorial box (`box_0e_elec`, two lunar days) runs with them from 2026-10-04 at the author's go-ahead. The coarse 6-km box is followed by a fine one over a smaller area (`box_0e_elec_fine`, 2 km over 128 km, from the coarse run's averaged air before a stormy window), set up on 2026-10-04 and started, at the author's direction, once the coarse run showed it worthwhile: it runs from the coarse run's day 10.75, four hours before its first flashes (day 10.92). The radiation reads cloud ice sizes capped at 140 µm, as the Morrison runs do, so that the NSSL and Morrison boxes share their radiation; the ice above the cap is logged for the radiation and cloud-optics work (author, 2026-10-04). The fine box ran two model days without a flash, and the author extended it toward the coarse run's busiest lightning; its deep convection collapsed after coarse day 13.25, and the author stopped it at coarse day 13.75 (2026-10-04). The lunar runs lift WRF-ELEC's 180-kV/m cap on the breakdown field, which on Earth applies only below about 4.5 km but in the denser lunar air below about 36 km, where half the first lunar day's flashes started; the density scaling holds at every height and the 50-kV/m floor stays, above about 100 km (author, 2026-10-04). The coarse run takes the lifted cap from day 18, and its first lunar day's storms run again with it from the day-10.5 restart (`box_0e_elec_uncapped`). The author left point discharge from the ground, which WRF-ELEC lacks, to the agent: the model carries it as a setting (Standler and Winn 1979, its onset 3 kV/m over dense vegetation scaled by density), and the first lunar day's storms run again with it (`box_0e_elec_uncapped_corona`) to show what it changes before any other run takes it. The lunar runs take WRF-ELEC's own ground-strike rule, a downward channel reaching air warmer than −7 °C in matching charge, as an upper bound on ground strikes until a rule for a leader's crossing of the air below the storm replaces it (author, 2026-10-05); the height rule they first took could allow no ground strike. Charge leaks through stage 1's conductivity in the lunar runs from now on (author, 2026-10-05): without it the charge evaporating cloud leaves on the small ions stayed for weeks and held the field at the ground near 20 kV/m all over the box, where leakage clears it within the hour and leaves the lightning as it was. A ground strike in the lunar runs needs the leader to cross the air below the storm, its channel's internal field 1 kV/m at sea-level density, the literature's central value for a thermalized leader (author, 2026-10-05): its tip must keep the potential its streamer zone needs beyond the air's all the way down, which at that field passes 82–100 % of the strikes WRF-ELEC's rule alone counts. The conductivity column keeps its continuum scaling of the ions' attachment to aerosol with pressure, which makes its clear-air values aloft the low end (5–12 % low at 34 km, 23–45 % at 70 km); the transition correction comes with stage 3's rebuild of the column with the regional aerosol (author, 2026-10-06). A review of the electrified build found NSSL's rain and cloud-ice ventilation left at Earth's fall speeds (lunar rain evaporating 1.3–1.5 times too fast); the build now scales them, and the main run `box_0e_elec_corrected` restarts from day 0 with the fix, its start held for the author (author, 2026-10-06). |
| The wave work turns to the main seas and their water levels: next come the nearside sea's waves through the 60-day global atmospheric record, a tide product in geography, and one synthesis of the wave studies. The nearside sea runs at a 300 s timestep, checked against 150 s over its stormiest days. | Author, 2026-10-03; [climate/waves/review.md](../climate/waves/review.md) | Done on 2026-10-03. Through the second lunar cycle the nearside sea's mean Hs is 1.39 m (Smythii–Marginis 0.88 m), Hs of at least 1 m covers 68% of its area and time, and its largest Hs is 4.9 m; the seas travel toward the equator, and the most exposed coasts take up to 731 W/m on average ([climate/waves/nearside.md](../climate/waves/nearside.md)). The tide product is in [geography](../geography/README.md#the-monthly-tide) and the synthesis in [climate/waves/README.md](../climate/waves/README.md). |
| The review of the wave studies stands as their assessment. SWAN's and SWASH's lunar handling holds, and the runs forced by the GCM carry lunar air–sea momentum transfer: ExoPlaSim's open-sea roughness follows Charnock's law at lunar gravity. The studies leave out the seas beyond Smythii–Marginis (which holds 2.7% of the water), the monthly tide (a typical monthly range of 3.7 m over the nearside sea, 2.1 m in the South Pole–Aitken sea and 0.55 m in Smythii–Marginis, from the geography tide product), coasts of regolith whose sediment moves far more readily at lunar gravity, and how the waves look (lunar seas move at 0.41 times the speed of Earth seas of the same wavelength). Their largest uncertainties are the 1° basin grid, the forcing and the single lunar cycle. | Author, 2026-10-03 (asked for the analysis to be locked in); [climate/waves/review.md](../climate/waves/review.md), [results/review_checks.json](../climate/waves/results/review_checks.json) | Stands. The geography tide product (2026-10-03) replaced the review's first tide estimate; it also finds Mare Fecunditatis exchanging water with the nearside sea through a narrow strait every 9.5 days, whose geometry and friction set its fortnightly tide: twice the equilibrium with bed friction for slow flows, 0.8–1.05 times it with the friction of the metre-per-second flows the strait would carry. The review's other recommendations stay open: a Janssen drag-and-growth run, a 0.5° basin parent, further cycles, a wider frequency band, the seas' composition, sediment and beach evolution, and figures drawn over relief. |

## Protection

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The protection architecture: a solar-filter complex with an industrial hub; magnetic protection; protection that grows around occupied destinations and Earth–Moon traffic corridors, secondary to protecting the lunar atmosphere and surface; Earth–Sun L1/L2 hubs for freight, power and industry. | Sep 9, carrying the original 2025 architecture | Stands |
| No superconducting planetary ring. | Sep 4 | Stands |
| The protection is designed across a range of total loss budgets, 1–100 kg/s, until the designs show which rate works best. Each budget is stated with its atmospheric cycle time, the time the loss and the resupply that matches it take to replace the whole atmosphere: about 100 billion years at 1 kg/s, 10 billion at 10 kg/s and 1 billion at 100 kg/s. | Author, 2026-09-26 | Stands |
| A surface radiation dose of at most 0.027 mSv/day. | Sep 4 | Stands |
| Protection hardware is a formation of replaceable units, never a single megastructure. Ageing and damage are handled by replacing units, supplied over the long term. | Author, 2026-09-26 | Stands |
| The protection design covers the Moon and its atmosphere. Destinations and corridors come later, and the older Earth–Moon–L1–L2 megastructure concept stays outside it. | Author, 2026-09-26 | Stands |

## Transport and safety

The author confirmed these rules on 2026-09-26 and regards them as set, to be reopened only for good reasons.

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Passive failures miss planets: nominal transfer paths and their failure dispersions avoid inhabited bodies unless capture has been verified. | Sep 19 | Stands |
| Earth never lies behind the lunar catcher, so a missed lunar capture cannot become an Earth-impact trajectory. | Sep 19 | Stands |
| Major braking happens remotely: high-energy packets approach the inhabited Moon only after capture and deceleration. | Sep 19 | Stands |
| Packet energy is capped. Mature dense traffic uses packets of roughly 10⁶–10⁸ kg in preference to 10¹²–10¹⁵ kg units, so each failure's consequences stay bounded; the exact cap awaits risk optimization. | Sep 19 | Stands |
| Planetary-scale surveillance, tracking, interception and tug capability, and assigned arrival corridors are part of the infrastructure. | Sep 19 | Stands |
| No civilization-threatening kinetic payload enters a planetary intercept corridor before verified capture. | Sep 19 | Stands |

## Solar shield and habitat array

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Develop the combined titania/PV shield, optical-routing alternatives, regional power grid and toroidal habitat fleet; create a branch from main and investigate holding with a full ephemeris. | Author, 2026-10-04; [design brief](studies/solar_shield_array/design.md) | Stands as a research direction. [DE440 study](studies/solar_shield_array/report.md) completed for bounded trajectory families; final materials, holding architecture and inhabited capacity remain open. |
| Consider photogravitational tacking as a route to lower holding cost at the existing mass per area. | Author, 2026-10-04; [tacking review](studies/solar_shield_array/tacking.md) | Research direction. [Numerical cycling study](studies/solar_shield_array/cycling.md) now supplies a replayed return arc and a three-year bounded point-tile candidate at 50 g/m². Measured useful projected area is about 15.7%; spatial fleet coverage, practical control and architecture selection remain open. The relative-orbit plan of 2026-10-06 carries the direction forward. |
| The formation work moves to relative-orbit structure. Coasts run at equal energy, with burns that manage it each cycle; e/i-vector separation carries the pattern through the fold; attitude schemes are compared. Designs are built in the linear relative-orbit model and verified in the coupled finite-square model. The shield's protection stays the acceptance test throughout: finite-Sun coverage, overlap, and UV transmission within the atmosphere's allowance. | Author, 2026-10-06; [relative-orbit plan](studies/solar_shield_array/relative_orbits.md) | Stands as the working plan. It replaces the SQP campaign of the expanded-cycle package, which stays preserved. |
| Findings adopted from the review of the expanded cycle. The cycle's ~345 km return error is along-track drift from the depth stack's orbital-energy spread, which one burn of 0.42 m/s rms per tile cuts to 70 m along-track in a gravity-only replay. The hour-13.8 clearance failures are the pattern folding through its orbital plane. The installed actuators have six-fold headroom, and attitude turns dominate the energy. | Author, 2026-10-06; [relative-orbit plan](studies/solar_shield_array/relative_orbits.md#findings) | Adopted. Stage 1 publishes the reproducing product. |
| Decision gate. A 361-tile cycle that closes every coverage, clearance and recurrence gate within about 1.3 m/s per tile per cycle, with attitude energy inside the 23.835 TW target, takes the orbiting fleet to a global ring screen near the Sun-synchronous radius. Otherwise the held screen with a zoned aperture becomes the baseline. | Author, 2026-10-06; [relative-orbit plan](studies/solar_shield_array/relative_orbits.md#plan) | Replaced the same day by the integrated comparison below |
| Stage 2's local unit is a bundle of ring segments. Each ring is one natural orbit of shingled tiles; the rings step outward in radius and slide past one another without reassembling. Each tile holds its own radial-facing attitude. The decision gate applies to the bundle's recurrence cost per tile per cycle. The rings fly natural orbits, unlike the rejected prescribed circular family, which paid continuous thrust to hold Sun-following circles. | Author, 2026-10-06; [layout screen](studies/solar_shield_array/relative_orbits.md#layout-screen-finite-patches-meet-ring-bundles-stay-clear) | Stands |
| Investigate a zoned aperture and collection sized to demand. The climate stack sits only in the central window; the annulus is a light film that blocks the UV and passes visible light, so the shield casts no deep shadow on Earth. Collection follows demand, with the 5% dimmer as semitransparent PV for the 100 TW case and small local collectors for holding. | Author, 2026-10-06; [relative-orbit plan](studies/solar_shield_array/relative_orbits.md#areal-mass-and-collection) | Research direction. Areal-mass targets and the dimmer as PV stay open choices. |
| The shield system meets the shield requirements, gives reasonable net-positive electricity for habitat, balances overall resource requirements, and is stable and safe. Mass flows count by direction as well as size: the propellant a design expels could threaten the cold, dense exobase. | Author, 2026-10-06 | Stands |
| Candidates are compared in one integrated ledger. Each must pass the shield requirements (UV transmission counting gaps, pinholes and outages; the protected radius; the window's spectrum; coverage of the full solar disk; Earth's shadow; where rejected light goes), stability, safety (every outflow kept out of the escape region, failed parts traced) and net-positive electricity for habitat after the system's own loads. Candidates that pass are weighed on resources: hardware, fresh feedstock, recycled throughput and delivery. The 23.835 TW figure and the 100 TW civilization case serve as comparison references, with higher demand as sensitivities. | Author, 2026-10-06; [integrated comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md) | Stands. Replaces the decision gate above |
| Findings adopted from the review of the ring-bundle checkpoint (02e36d6). The kept bundle as a whole first comes within 150 m at day 8.1, at its top edge pair, and that pair touches at day 15.7; samples after the first breach are diagnostic. Its 213 m interior minimum comes from one propagated tile per ring and two-body copies, whose radial error reaches 67 m at three lags within five days, so independently propagated tiles and receiver-ray coverage have to confirm it. A start-up defect had advanced every ring 0.489° along its orbit; it is fixed and the products regenerated. The zoned held screen's 32.8 TW is mean electrical holding demand (75.7 TW installed) for continuous area under an idealized optical bound. Its 26 and 5 g/m² are conditional; the 5 g/m² annulus needs its own transmission calculation, because the 24.8–120 nm opacity proof rests on 22 g/m² of silica. The dimmer's 586 TW of optical power is one possible source. The 17.29 million tiles belong to the cycling capacity relaxation, and the ring architecture needs its own inventory. | Author, 2026-10-06; [relative-orbit plan](studies/solar_shield_array/relative_orbits.md#coupled-ring-bundles-free-and-kept) | Adopted |
| A screen held upstream of the Moon sends all its reaction mass into the Moon's hemisphere: the zoned held screen expels 51 t/s carrying 23 TW of jet power. Without the planned magnetosphere the solar wind carries the charged exhaust downstream over the Moon; the magnetosphere would hold off the ions but pass the fast atoms and the unionized gas. Its exhaust has to stay out of the escape region to a few parts per million of that energy or better (proposed requirement S7). The ring fleet keeps station with photon forces: attitude offsets that tilt the sail force, and reflectivity trim. | Author, 2026-10-06; [protection/report.md](../protection/report.md), section 8; [integrated comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md) | Adopted as working choices. The exhaust results are in the integrated comparison. Checked on 2026-10-06 and 07: photon keeping reaches the kept run's demand on translation; on attitude it holds with a filter centred on each tile, reflectivity trim and a momentum store, while edge-on turns collide with the next ring ([integrated comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md#what-holds-the-ring-fleet-the-attitude)) |
| The ring fleet is the lead candidate. The held screen stays a documented fallback, and for it only two literature checks continue: how fully thrusters ionize their propellant, neutralizer flow included, and a bound on its exhaust's plasma path with and without the magnets. The reasons: at its lightest design the held screen burns 51,000 kg/s, more than the atmosphere loses unshielded across most of the 300–80,000 kg/s range (S6), and meeting S7 would need gridded ion thrusters and capture of most of its unionized gas, while the ring fleet fails no gate and releases nothing. | Author, 2026-10-07; [integrated comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md) | Stands |
| Requirement S7 is adopted as written: everything the protection systems release or redirect stays out of the escape region, within a stated share of R1. | Author, 2026-10-07; [requirements](studies/protection_architecture/requirements.md) | Stands; the share of R1 left to outflows is open |
| The ring fleet's form. Tiles stay radial-facing; each tile's redirecting filter covers only its middle along the ring (the 8.5 km pitch plus about 400 m), with 25% reflectivity trim and a momentum store near 2×10⁹ N·m·s per tile. The rings fly near 20,000 km, tilted from the Moon's orbital plane. They form one nested bundle with one sail loading, so annulus tiles come to about 13 g/m², carrying 0.1 µm of titania on 4 µm of silica with the stack's coating layers (10.2 g/m²). The reasons: edge-on turns collide with the next ring, at 15,000 km the strips cross within weeks, and rings of different sail loading are forced to eccentricities that cross. | Author, 2026-10-07; [integrated comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md#what-holds-the-ring-fleet-the-attitude) | Stands |
| Next work: check in the atmosphere domain where solar X-rays that pass a light annulus film deposit their energy along slant paths above the limb; then study the outer rings' drift as a radius profile that keeps each tilted ring turning with the Sun, before steering or yearly oversizing. | Author, 2026-10-07 | Research direction; the X-ray check was done the same day (below) |
| O1 counts the light that passes gaps by the heat it leaves in the air along its slant paths above the limb, traced over the whole aperture, in place of a quarter of the light that reaches the disk. Traced, a gap heats the thermosphere 2.5–5 times the disk count, so for the same loss allowance gaps may pass about a quarter to a third of what that count allows in the cooler titania cases. The loss response takes the traced heat from the middle atmosphere's limb tracing, and O1's levels and the design point are rederived with it. | Author, 2026-10-07; [the X-ray check](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md#the-x-ray-check), [limb tracing](../atmosphere/middle_atmosphere/README.md#slant-paths-above-the-limb-2026-10-07) | Stands; rederived on 2026-10-07 at the ring fleet's 4-radius shadow ([requirements](studies/protection_architecture/requirements.md)) |
| The X-rays' rise from quiet Sun to solar maximum comes from FISM2's measured daily spectra, the year around each of the last three maxima over the WHI 2008 quiet week: 4.6–6.9 for the whole band below 10 nm (6.0 for their mean), which sets the X-rays a gap passes, and 11–24 for the light the annulus films and the window stack pass. It replaces the escape model's convention of 2.5 for the band a filter passes and 100 for a film's X-rays, and the products that use the convention are rerun with it. On this count the 4 µm annulus film adds a tenth to a fifth of the sky glow's heat at solar maximum. | Author, 2026-10-07; [the X-ray check](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md#the-x-ray-check) | Stands for the escape model's own film and leak heats; the products that used the earlier convention were rerun on 2026-10-07. Since the same day the loss chain's solar maximum takes its X-rays from cycle 19's year with the rest of its light (row below), and the escape model's mean of the last three maxima rides with its 2.5 in the stress case |
| Next work, before the outer rings: how warm the upper air gets, which the loss budget, the protected radius and the magnets all turn on. First the solar-cycle average R1 reads, then the thermal column's heating shape taken from the limb tracing, rerunning the loss chain after each; then the near-infrared heating of CO2 above a few pascals or the outer rings, by what the first two show. For the cycle average each band of the ultraviolet takes its own measured rise from FISM2's daily record, in place of the escape model's single factor of 2.5 at maximum. | Author, 2026-10-07 | Research direction. The solar-cycle mean and the traced heating shape were carried through the loss chain the same day ([requirements](studies/protection_architecture/requirements.md#retention)); with them every titania case stays within 10 kg/s at the standard level without magnets and the solar wind decides 1 kg/s, so the CO2 step decides little and the outer rings come next |
| Before the outer rings, answer how much atomic oxygen escapes: an oxygen atom escapes far more readily than the molecules, and no magnet holds a neutral. | Author, 2026-10-07 | Research direction. Answered the same day ([oxygen.py](../atmosphere/loss_response/oxygen.py); [R2](studies/protection_architecture/requirements.md)): behind the titania stack at the standard level, with the middle atmosphere's eddy mixing scaled for the Moon, the atoms add 0.002–0.006 kg/s in the cooler cases and 0.12 kg/s with all near-infrared heating over the solar cycle (0.06 with the September magnets, bringing that case to 0.81–1.0 kg/s); Earth's weaker mixing, a bound, gives that case 2.1 kg/s (1.0 with the magnets). Hot atoms from near the exobase are 0.001–0.002 kg/s of these and hydrogen adds 0.001–0.003 kg/s. How strongly the upper air mixes above the base decides the question; the gravity-wave estimate that followed narrows it (below) With the thermal column's infrared cooling (2026-10-08) the warmest case's atoms lose about a sixth as much: 0.021 kg/s over the cycle with the Moon-scaled mixing, 0.15 with Titan's and 0.46 with Earth's. |
| The products' solar-maximum columns take FISM2's measured maxima, with the record taken back to 1947 so that cycle 19 is in it, and keep the escape model's 2.5 as a labelled stress case. | Author, 2026-10-07 | Stands. Done the same day ([cycle.py](../atmosphere/loss_response/cycle.py)): the solar maximum is the year around the strongest maximum on record, cycle 19's of 1957–58, taken whole, its X-rays and glow from the same year as the rest of its light. Its far ultraviolet is weaker than the stress case's and its glow (1.88 against 1.5), light below 50 nm and X-rays (9.6 against 6.0) stronger, so the cooler titania cases lose about what the stress case gives while the warmest titania case runs away at the standard level and the 200-nm edge at the tight level ([requirements](studies/protection_architecture/requirements.md#retention)). Before 1978 FISM2 rests on the 10.7 cm radio flux alone. The author then took the strongest maximum since 1978 as the column and cycle 19 as a further stress case (row below) |
| The solar-maximum column takes the strongest maximum since 1978, when FISM2 adds the MgII and Lyman-α proxies to the 10.7 cm radio flux: the year around cycle 21's maximum of 1979–80, taken whole. Cycle 19's year, the strongest on record, is a further stress case beside the escape model's 2.5. | Author, 2026-10-07 | Stands; carried through the loss chain the same day. At cycle 21's maximum the warmest titania case loses 8.4 kg/s at the standard level (3.5 with the September magnets) against the 2.5's 26 (15), and the cooler cases 0.73–1.1 kg/s; the 200-nm edge runs away at the standard level at every maximum. With the thermal column's infrared cooling (2026-10-08) the warmest titania case loses 1.5 kg/s there (0.090 with the magnets) and the cooler cases 0.62–0.82 kg/s, and no case runs away at the standard level |
| Noted for later study: the ring fleet could extend its protected radius by more active control as the solar weather requires, spending more energy and propellant while it does; it need not hold its maximum extent all the time, and its formation is already dynamic. | Author, 2026-10-07 | Noted, not yet studied. A first look at the same tiles: holding the stack's half-monthly breathing by keeping, which the bundle's design allows in place of the room left for it in the strips' overlap, would extend the shadow to about 4.1–4.2 lunar radii; the rings' spare 23% covers the pattern's annual shift ([the comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md#next-steps)) |
| The dynamic option kept in view for solar maxima: cover failed cells faster through the maximum years (the swarm's tight level, within a day in place of a week), with spare tiles and servicing ready across the fleet; changes of the protected radius are set aside. | Author, 2026-10-08 | Noted, not yet studied. With the thermal column's infrared cooling (2026-10-08) the standard level already keeps every maximum on record clear of the self-sustaining swollen state, and at cycle 21's maximum the tight level brings the warmest titania case from 1.5 to 1.4 kg/s (0.090 to 0.042 with the September magnets); before the cooling it brought that case from 8.4 to 2.5 kg/s (3.5 to 0.45) ([the loss response](../atmosphere/loss_response/README.md)) |
| Estimate the Moon's upper-air eddy mixing from breaking gravity waves, which decides how much atomic oxygen escapes. | Author, 2026-10-07 | Research direction. Done the same day ([gravity_waves.py](../atmosphere/loss_response/gravity_waves.py)): Earth's gravity-wave spectrum, calibrated so that it gives Earth's measured eddy diffusion (Swenson et al. 2021) and homopause, mixes the lunar upper air about as strongly as the Moon-scaled profile, since long waves rise almost undamped through the cold thermosphere; the warmest titania case's atoms then lose 0.10 kg/s over the cycle (0.05 with the September magnets). Titan's measured mixing carried to the Moon gives 0.77 kg/s (0.37), and the waves at the low end of Earth's measured mixing 0.45 kg/s; Earth's own unscaled mixing (2.1 kg/s) lies outside both. The lunar troposphere's own wave sources (the CM1 runs could supply them) and the day-night circulation are what remain With the thermal column's infrared cooling (2026-10-08) the warmest case's atoms lose 0.017 kg/s with the waves' mixing and 0.15 with Titan's. |
| Before the outer rings, see whether the runaway is a real latch: give the thermal column the infrared cooling of NO, CO2 and atomic oxygen. | Author, 2026-10-08 | Research direction. Done the same day ([infrared.py](../atmosphere/loss_response/infrared.py); [the loss response](../atmosphere/loss_response/README.md)). CO2 at the middle atmosphere's 400 ppm radiates most of the heat the upper air takes, so the air needs about three and a half times the heat to swell past the shadow's edge. The latch stays: past a threshold, 1.9–8.4×10⁻⁵ W/m² at quiet Sun with the exobase at 4.5–5.8 lunar radii, the unfiltered light beyond the aperture still outgrows the heat that swells the air, by the geometry of the shadow's edge. Every maximum on record settles at the standard level, a tenth to three-fifths of the way to its threshold, and the allowed transmissions rise two to eight times. Over the solar cycle at the standard level the warmest titania case loses 1.4 kg/s without magnets (0.044 kg/s with the September magnets) and the cooler cases 0.61–0.80 kg/s. The oxygen atoms made above the base, and in the swollen state those the unfiltered light would split from the outer air's O2, are left out and could move the threshold either way. |
| Close the solar-shield work after the outer rings, the last question that could still change the ring fleet's form or tile count: fly their radius profile for a year against steering by roll with the larger store, then write one consolidated summary into the integrated comparison and the research status, and park the rest (the keeping in the regressing frame with the edge rings treated and the along-track keeping; the momentum store and trim as hardware and their power; and what waits on inputs this machine lacks: a plasma model of the wake and of a weak dipole, the closure trajectories for the magnetic comparison's rerun, the held screen's two literature checks). The branch's merge into main waits until the other branches are ready, for a proper integration and joint merge. | Author, 2026-10-08 | Research direction. The outer rings were done the same day as the stack's radius layout (the row below), and the consolidated summary is in the [integrated comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md#where-the-comparison-stands-8-october) and the research status; the parked items are listed there |
| The ring stack's radii are laid out two-sided: every ring has a radius of its own (the rings share one line of nodes), set near the radius at which its plane turns with the Sun, so the radius grows toward both edges of the stack, the two sides interleaving. | Author, 2026-10-08 ("The two-sided layout makes sense") | Stands; flown the same day ([ring_layout.py](studies/solar_shield_array/ring_layout.py), [integrated comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/integrated_comparison.md#the-ring-planes)) |
| Keep thermodynamic computing as a research direction for the solar shield and habitat array, for the array's own control and for scientific and AI services, and find out whether the heat flows, temperature differences and thermal equipment the array already has could support it without adding much to its burden. | Author, 2026-10-08 ("Lock this idea in, do some initial investigation") | Research direction. The first investigation, with a screen of where the array's heat arises, is in [array_industry.md](studies/solar_shield_array/array_industry.md); it continues in the planned branch of the row below |
| End the solar-shield branch with the heat, computing and industry investigation, and plan one later branch, `research/array-industry`, for heat reuse, general computing and industry of the array, with the shield's reflectivity trim and momentum store and their power in its plan. No new branch opens yet, with many branches still open off a stale main. | Author, 2026-10-08 ("make this the cap of this branch with one planned future branch... research/array-industry and reasonable to include the shield's trim and store power in the plan") | Stands. The plan is in [array_industry.md](studies/solar_shield_array/array_industry.md#the-planned-branch); the momentum store and trim as hardware and their power move there from the comparison's parked items |
| The ring fleet avoids lighting the Moon's night zones. The light its night half would send there is redirected away from the Moon and, where it can be, put to use, for computing for instance. Lighting over cities might be acceptable if fairly fine spotlights are possible. | Author, 2026-10-08 ("Best is to avoid lighting the night zones. If we can redirect the light somehow, use it for compute instead for instance, that would be preferable"; earlier the same day: "if we can redirect it away from the Moon at least that would probably be ideal rather than eliminating even more 'night'") | Stands as the direction of the Moon's night requirement, whose value comes from the biosphere and human work. The [joint synthesis](studies/joint_synthesis/README.md#1-the-night-with-the-ring-fleet) found the fleet as designed lighting the night at 25–66 lux; a roll of the night half near 80° with a Moon-facing scatter near 10⁻⁵ brings a far-side midnight to about 0.02 lux, and each tile's glint is an image of the Sun about 170 km across. The night-side attitude law, the films' scatter and collectors for the turned light belong to the planned `research/array-industry` branch |
| The ring fleet's own infrared glow is kept from warming the Moon past the planned climate, with added solar protection, by redirecting the glow away from the Moon, or both. | Author, 2026-10-09 (the planned climate, [Water and climate](#water-and-climate)) | Research direction for the shield work. First estimate ([the provisioning first screen](../provisioning/README.md#heat-and-energy), 9 October): the films absorb 3–4.5% of the sunlight they pass, 65–83 PW over the fleet, warm to about 175 K and radiate from both faces; the Moon, about 10° across from 20,000 km, takes 0.7–0.8% of the inward half, about 6–10 W/m² over the Moon (5.7–9.9), 1.9–3.4% of the design sunlight and 3.7–6.4 K if it warms as sunlight does. The means to size: less light absorbed in the films, for instance the ultraviolet reflected before the titania absorbs it, which cuts the glow however the stack lies; a heat mirror on Moon-facing faces, clear to sunlight and reflective in the thermal infrared, adding little absorption because the films absorb so little now (a sparse metal mesh or metasurface a few micrometres in pitch is the first candidate); and added dimming for what remains. The stack weakens the mirror: about two layers overlap in the Moon's view, and a ring hidden behind another passes much of its heat on inward. By a first two-layer estimate (the same screen), a mirror of emissivity 0.03–0.1 on every tile cuts the Moon's share only 1.5–2 times and heats the hidden layers to 280–380 K; on the layer nearest the Moon alone, with strong emitters on the outer faces, it cuts it 3–9 times. Computing on collected light keeps its own heat off the Moon with radiators that face outward or stand edge-on to it. Screened on 12 October on research/array-industry ([glow](studies/solar_shield_array/glow.md)): as built the stack puts 14.1 W/m² on the Moon, its own glow of 9.4 W/m² with each ring's night half at perilune and 4.7 W/m² of the Moon's infrared and sunlight that the films image back onto it. Heat mirrors on the Moon-facing faces raise that total. Rolled 80° outside the service arc on both halves, inside the clearance along the kept orbits, the fleet leaves 6.0 W/m² on the layout re-flown for its push (13 October, 350–500 km further in, inside the photon roll's steering reach), which added dimming of 2.0% of the design sunlight answers at the GCM's infrared efficacy (2.7% at an efficacy of 1), for a momentum store 5–12 times the chosen one; the night half rolled alone takes a store 14–48 times the chosen one and leaves 8.7 W/m². The light the roll leaves on the night's edge is 0.3–0.6% of the tall sky's own twilight there; a tilt toward the Sun through the nodes, which would cut it, breaks the clearance |
| Weigh every industrial and computing use of the array by what it does to the Moon's heat and the fleet's glow: what it puts on the films, how it dims, and where its heat goes. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("note the importance of considering how industrial use impacts heat/glow") | Research direction, with the planned `research/array-industry` branch. First rules in [the provisioning screen](../provisioning/README.md#heat-and-energy): computing and industry do little against the glow, since keeping a watt of it off the Moon takes 221–303 W out of the films, and they can use the light the fleet turns away or collects, limited by demand; each extra percent of sunlight the films absorb adds 1.7–2.9 W/m² of glow; a dimmer that absorbs on the window rings gives 15–26% of its dimming back as glow, and one that reflects gives none back; a terawatt used on the Moon adds 0.026 W/m², and one released in orbit 5×10⁻⁵ W/m². |
| Computing is the leading use of the energy the array collects and would otherwise waste: the array's own control, the Moon's management and its simulations, and services beyond them. Heavy industry on the array weighs on its mass. Whether the array also serves Earth, or prototypes an array of Earth's own, is open. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("Compute seems to be the thing screaming 'valuable resource that turns the otherwise wasted energy into use.'"; "honestly for quite some time this array makes sense as a prototype for Earth's") | Research direction; [computing](../provisioning/computing.md) and [population](../provisioning/population.md) take it up |
| The array's feasibility is worked out in stages on `research/array-industry`: the tile hardware with its power, momentum stores and replacement, then a broader comparison of materials and support architectures, then a cheap screen of geometries and materials with the coupled flexible model implemented, verified and benchmarked. Running the coupled design search comes at a later date. | Author, 2026-10-14, in a Codex session ("Let's do that. Work in array-industry.", on the proposal to size the tile hardware; "Commit this work and then do a broader materials and support-architecture comparison."; then, asked whether the coupled search would follow the solver benchmarks and the cheap screening, "Alright let's do the cheap screening at least and solver benchmarking and implementing and varifying the coupled model. Actually running the coupled search can be done at a later date.") | Done the same day ([tile design](studies/solar_shield_array/tile_design.md), [support comparison](studies/solar_shield_array/support_comparison.md), [flexible coupling](studies/solar_shield_array/flexible_coupling.md)) and reviewed in [the constraints review](studies/solar_shield_array/constraints_review.md): no tested tile fits the inherited areal-mass allocation, and the tiles' mass and the photons' steering authority bind together. The coupled search waits. |

## Research practice

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Simulation work fits the author's laptop (8 cores and 31 GB shared with the author's own work, runs of hours with rests between); there is no budget for rented compute. | Author, 2026-09-29 | Stands |
| Before any further CM1 run, CM1 is rebuilt for speed and tested: gfortran at `-O3` for this laptop's processor (`-march=native`), and a milder variant without fused multiply-adds or glibc's vector maths that may reproduce the current build bit for bit. A rebuild is used for every later run if it runs faster and matches the current build, byte for byte or within the current build's own spread after a one-bit nudge. The flat rings running on 2026-09-29 (`ring_70_45e`, `ring_equator`, `ring_70_135e`) finish on the current build. | Author, 2026-09-29; [climate/crm](../climate/crm/README.md) | Stands. Tested on 2026-09-30: neither rebuild matched the current build byte for byte, and both stayed within the spread of two nudges of it (the last bit of every value of its scalar restart file, since a single value's nudge could not reach the whole ring in a day). `-O3 -march=native`, 11–15% faster, is now every OpenMP build's setting; the MPI builds keep `-O2`, untested. |
| The climate work pauses. CM1 and the GCM disagree on how warm and humid the air over land is near the ground, and no affordable option settles which is right: the cheaper runs refine CM1's answer without an independent check, and a second GCM (ROCKE-3D) would take weeks of laptop sessions. Comfort is carried as the range between the two models (on the CM1 rings' land, 42 comfortable hours per lunar day in CM1 corrected for height against 387 in the GCM), and both put the most comfortable land poleward of 30°. The corrected GCM design case and the products built from it stand. The work reopens with an independent GCM or new evidence on daytime cloud over a slowly rotating world. | Author, 2026-09-30; [climate/crm](../climate/crm/README.md) | Stands. On 2026-10-01, at the author's go-ahead, two GCM runs tested its cloud: convective cloud thinned toward CM1's warmed the whole Moon by 1.7 K, and low cloud added toward CM1's foggy nights cooled it by 5.0 K, each about alike from the ground to 18 km, so the GCM's cloud does not explain the gap. What remains is how each model treats the lowest few kilometres. The runs also put the GCM's own climate between about 289 and 297 K across those cloud settings, and its comfortable hours on the rings' land between 121 and 387. |
| Data products record the shared constants their code reads by name and value instead of pinning `shared/constants.json` or `constants.py` by hash, and their tests compare those values with the current ones. Adding a constant then leaves every product current, while a change to a value a product used shows up. Workflow tools such as DVC and Snakemake track a step's parameters the same way. | Author, 2026-10-04; [shared/provenance.py](../shared/provenance.py), [shared/README.md](../shared/README.md#data-products) | Stands. The optical, sky and cloud-twilight producers follow it; regenerated on 2026-10-04, every data value reproduced and the sky's embedded numerical checks were refreshed with the current check code. The wave, tide and coastal products still record the constants file's hash as a record of their run, without a test of it. |

## Life and people

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Aerial and gliding life is a major feature of the world. | July | Stands |
| A working population scale for the frontier of thousands to tens of thousands of years: about 20–30 billion people across the Solar System, affluent by modern standards and that the norm for the population of the future, most of them on Earth, on the Moon and in the array. People who work on the array often commute; those who work a great deal at its industry may live where it is, so the array has habitats. Whether the array and the Terluna system sustain that many people affluently, and how they would live and meet, are the questions. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("I'm thinking a rather 'affluent by modern standards but norm for the population of the future' with a figure of... maybe... 20 - 30B total spread across the solar system") | Research direction; the scale is the author's working figure. [Population](../provisioning/population.md) takes it up |
| Reopen the population distribution and evaluate the Moon's inhabited volume, including aerial and supported settlements, before selecting an allocation. Retain the 20–30 billion Solar System working scale. Historical domain scenarios do not determine capacity. | Author, 2026-10-09, cross-domain population discussion and request to evaluate and record the findings | Research direction. [Shared cases](../shared/scenarios/population.json) are comparison inputs only. The [inhabited-volume screen](studies/inhabited_volume/README.md) evaluates conditional mass, lift, area, holding power and services; no allocation, architecture or ultimate capacity is adopted. The [ways-of-living study](studies/ways_of_living/README.md) (2026-10-09) gives eighteen settings with their costs per billion residents and the shared cases' totals by mix; every mix carries every case at its own split, and the distribution stays the author's to choose. |
| Allow much floating habitation to roam, with collision and storm risks managed. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("I'd let a lot of the floating habitation roam just being careful of collision storm etc.") | Working habitation direction. Shared population scenarios carry it; the inhabited-volume study treats continuous fixed-position holding as a comparison and screens occasional powered navigation/repositioning, with storm routing, rendezvous and manoeuvre reserves still to design. The design winds ([climate/gcm/zonal_winds.md](../climate/gcm/zonal_winds.md), 2026-10-09) give the days drifting towns live by height and latitude, where they can hold the hour, and how passive routes gather: onto one track near the ground within months, and over the poles at 40–60 km within years. |
| High-altitude life is sparse: microbes or engineered radiation-hard films. | July | Stands |
| Consider the aerial biomes almost first: the skies are a large set of biomes of their own and connect the others, with cheap lift, room for larger organisms, wide space and moist air. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("while the land is important, the skies are a massive potential set of biomes in and of themselves in addition to be interconnected to others... we really should consider the aerial biomes almost first") | Research direction; [people, land and sky](../biosphere/ecology/people_and_land.md) leads with them |
| Long-endurance high-altitude platforms serve farside astronomy and nearside earthshine photometry. | July | Stands |
| The seas are living: optical studies use stated best guesses with ranges for plankton pigments, dissolved organic matter and bioluminescence, drawn from the biosphere and lunar-cycle ecology work and labelled as design guesses, beside the regolith fines. | Author, 2026-10-04; [research/studies/sea_appearance](studies/sea_appearance/README.md) | Stands |
| Open Moon organisms are engineered. Earth species' traits are reference points and design variables; the hard limits are light, carbon and energy supply, temperature, water, transport and the 708.7-hour cycle. | Author, 2026-09-27, on the day fruit | Stands ([biosphere/canopy](../biosphere/canopy/README.md); [biosphere/ecology](../biosphere/ecology/README.md)) |
| The fully terraformed surface is developed, weathered and biologically worked soil; raw regolith describes the start of the transformation. | Author, 2026-10-05 | Stands. The aerosol study's dust follows it; the living waters' fines still take raw Apollo soils ([biosphere/ecology](../biosphere/ecology/state.md)) |

## The summit port and metropolis

The author set these on 2026-09-27. The [summit tower study](studies/summit_tower/README.md#the-central-port)
sizes the port, and the [metropolis brief](../habitation/summit_metropolis/README.md) collects the tower's form,
the metropolis and the first estimates behind them.

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The Moon's largest sky port stands on the far side's summit (5.4° N, 158.6° W). It is commercial: trade, travel, hospitality and short stays, with no homes and no industry. It holds about 1.5 million people on a regular basis. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Stands |
| Long-haul sky ships dock in the flight band, so the tower rises 24 km, to 35.6 km above sea level. Regional docks sit at 10–15 km, and every block of floors has berths. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Stands |
| The frame is a round diagrid that gathers into six splayed legs, as on the Eiffel Tower, through a deep transfer ring a kilometre or two up. The ground between the legs stays open. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Stands; the legs and the transfer ring are sized by hand in the chosen form ([form.py](studies/summit_tower/form.py), aac8c64), and its structural analysis is open |
| The floors are rings in the lower tower and disks round a central core from about 12 km up, as in the author's picture of the port, each a block of a few storeys with a park and open-air shops on its roof. They replace the full rings of 2026-09-27. The lower rings, where the open air is mildest, are wide (at 150 m, ring 0's park would be about 3 km²), with fewer storeys under the park and no terraces. Their decks cantilever from the frame, chosen for their look; cables from the frame above would be lighter. | Author, 2026-09-28 | Stands; the rings and their cantilevers, the disks and the core are sized by hand in the chosen form ([form.py](studies/summit_tower/form.py), aac8c64), and its structural analysis is open |
| Open-air parks follow the air: for everyone up to 3 km above the summit, for residents and acclimatised visitors up to 10 km, and a few short-visit high gardens, winter parks among them, from 10 to 15 km. Above 15 km the blocks are sealed. Every interior is kept at a comfortable pressure and temperature. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Stands |
| The disks' open decks are all cold, about 1 °C at 12 km and colder above, and give the Moon its winter: the open winter parks sit just below freezing at about 13.5–15 km, and it is colder still above 15 km, where the blocks are sealed. The mild open-air parks are the ground and the lower rings. | Author, 2026-09-28 | Stands |
| A beautiful, sustainable mega-metropolis surrounds the port and centres on it, planned for about 100 million people at about 40,000 per km², within about 32 km of the tower. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Stands, as a rough planning scale |
| The metropolis uses the third dimension: small sky boats serve much as cars do, gliders fly throughout, and glider and parkour zones are designed in. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Stands |
| A high-capacity backbone of metro, rail and large sky ferries carries the metropolis, with sky boats and gliders alongside. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Stands |
| Fusion supplies most of the Moon's local power, supplemented by regional solar and wind. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Planning assumption; fusion is a conditional technology here ([engineering](../engineering/README.md)). Since 12 October food and the sky towns take beamed power from the ring fleet ([the domain synthesis's decisions](#the-domain-synthesiss-decisions-12-october-2026)) |
| Regional agriculture and foraging feed the metropolis. | Author, 2026-09-27 | Planning assumption; the land per person has not been computed |

## Sky ships and flyers

The author adopted these findings on 2026-09-28 as the planning basis for the Open Moon's flyers. The
[sky-ship study](studies/sky_ships/README.md) and the [sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md) work them out
at first order, and the [metropolis brief](../habitation/summit_metropolis/README.md#flyers) collects what they mean
for the summit. No flyer has been designed. The long-haul class for the crown, whether winged liners join it, and
the sky boats' share of trips, ownership and traffic system stay open.

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The rule of similarity: under a sixth of Earth's gravity, a flyer six times larger in every length, flying at the same speed in air of the same density, carries the same stresses from its weight and from the same gusts. From 9 km up the Moon's air has the density of Earth's at a sixth of the height above 9 km, so the flight band (35–45 km) holds the air of Earth's 4.3–5.8 km. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-ship study](studies/sky_ships/README.md)) | Stands |
| Rigid hydrogen ships are reasonable up to about 3 km in the flight band and at their best near 750–900 m; their structure would take all their lift near 7 km with 1930s materials and near 30 km with modern ones. Pressure hulls reach 280–350 m there, and wings carry their weight with the An-225's share at about 710 m of span. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-ship study](studies/sky_ships/README.md)) | Stands; the hull model is fitted to four airships' weight statements. With the corrected climate (8 October) pressure hulls reach 260–330 m in the band |
| Wings and rotors are cheap under lunar gravity: hovering takes a fifteenth of Earth's power and a kilometre on a wing a sixth of the energy, while buoyant lift per cubic metre is Earth's. The small flyers are winged or rotor-borne; from ferries up, the flyers are buoyant ships, which hold still at their berths for free. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md)) | Stands |
| People fly on their own power. A pedalled wing of about 11 m span flies level on about 63 W, within what an untrained adult holds for three hours; a foot-launched micro glider of about 9 m and 4.5 m² glides 15 to 1 at 8 m/s; a round canopy about 4.8 m across laid flat lands a person at 4 m/s. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md)) | Stands |
| A four-seat sky boat is an electric flyer that lifts off on rotors and cruises on a wing: about 700 kg, 11 kW to hover and about 0.74 kWh for a 10 km trip, a twenty-fourth of the same trip on Earth. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md)) | Stands; sized with the Joby S4's technology |
| Large sky ferries are rigid hydrogen ships of about 250 m carrying about 1,200 people at 25 m/s; a line every two or three minutes carries 24,000–36,000 people an hour each way. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md)) | Stands |
| At a car-like tenth of the metropolis's trips, sky boats fly more densely than NASA's urban air mobility spacing allows, which holds 7–8%; owned at the rate cars are owned in Tokyo, their berths would cover three-fifths of the metropolis's land, while a shared fleet of about 1.1 million fits in 65 km². | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md)) | Stands as a finding; the share of trips, ownership and the traffic system are open |
| The flyers stack by height: wings, gliders and canopies below about 300 m above the ground, sky boats and air taxis to 1.5 km, sky ferries to 2.5 km; gliders soar to about 10 km by day; regional ships at the regional docks, 22–27 km above sea level; long-haul liners and freighters in the flight band; winged liners near 55 km; high platforms near 70 km. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md)) | Stands, as a first arrangement; with the corrected climate (8 October) the day's mixed layer reaches about 10 km and its cloud base about 7 km |
| Winged liners that take off and land on tilting rotors cross the Moon fast for little energy: a 300-seat liner reaches a mean destination in about 5 hours for about the energy per passenger-km of the largest airships. | Author, 2026-09-28 ([sky-fleet study](studies/sky_fleet/README.md)) | Stands as a finding; whether they serve the crown is open |
| The Open Moon is a sky-ship world. Under a sixth of Earth's gravity in dense, tall air, lift is cheap, and people and goods use the third dimension far more than on Earth: in construction, access, evacuation and transport. Flight does not settle everything; how fire and smoke behave at 0.16 g still has to be worked out. | Author, 2026-09-27 ("This world is a sky ship world. Lift is cheap. We have considerably more access to the third dimension than on Earth. Not a PERFECT solution to everything but the very thing that makes fire safety a 'well how does this work' also makes construction and accessibility much easier.") | Stands; the flyer findings above, the [port fire study](studies/port_fire/README.md) and [provisioning](../provisioning/README.md) follow it |

| Decision | Source | Status |

No new spectral window or exobase result adopted. |
| Evaluate the Moon's surface and aerial habitation before choosing population distribution; arbitrary domain allocations do not establish capacity. | Author, 2026-10-09 | [Shared comparison cases](../shared/scenarios/population.json); 20–30 billion Solar System working scale retained, no distribution selected. |
| Investigate true aeroplankton and large photosynthetic aerial organisms together, their consumers, and food harvest with a functioning phosphorus cycle. | Author, 2026-10-09 | [First requirements evaluation](studies/aerial_ecology/README.md) completed; ecology and resources must close nutrient return including harvest, with no assumed safe yield or species count. |

Main and each domain branch recorded these directions on 9 October; the integration of 12 October keeps one
copy. The roaming row is also recorded, with the author's words, under Life and people. Main's summaries of the follow-up are [aerial_biosphere_population.md](integration/aerial_biosphere_population.md)
and [floater_viability.md](integration/floater_viability.md).

Work out what the first arithmetic leaves out: fittings, seams, gusts and damage, then water and storms. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("Love that honestly if we can do that"; "Then let's figure that out") | Research direction; the [coupled aerophyte study](studies/aerophytes/README.md) (schema 2, 9 October 2026) gives size limits, ages, water, storms and sailing as screen values for the author's review |
| The large aerial organisms gather their water from the air around them, in preference to descending to drink. | Author, 2026-10-09 ("Pulling from the air would be preferable") | Working direction; the aerophyte study maps where rain, vapour and humid layers meet each trait set's need ([water](studies/aerophytes/water.md)): rain-drinking giants bank storm rain within their trim, and polar aerophytes drink vapour |

## The aerophytes' names, 10 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The large photosynthetic aerial organisms, held aloft by their own lifting gas, are called aerophytes, replacing the working name "floaters". | Author, 2026-10-10 ("we need to stop calling them 'floaters.' It just sounds dumb for a few reasons"; "Aerophytes.") | Adopted. The study is [research/studies/aerophytes](studies/aerophytes/README.md), and the taxonomy's G01 carries the name |
| The larger colony types take the common name "sky reefs". | Author, 2026-10-10 ("I like 'sky reef' does work for a common name for the larger colony types") | Adopted. The taxonomy's G03 gives the colony form the name; the round giants have no common name yet |
| Names that mean "cloud" can serve the scientific layer, and the everyday names describe what people see. | Author, 2026-10-10 ("most just translate as 'cloud' but that could work in the taxonomic sense, not the regular everyday name") | Direction.
