| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Welkinia is the scientific name of the giant aerophytes, the kind that get really large; smaller kinds take names suited to their size and how they live. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("We'll go with Welkinia for the kind that get really really large. Smaller varieties can have other names that are more aligned with their relatively smaller size and how they live.") | Adopted. The taxonomy's G03 carries it; the smaller kinds' names are open |
| Designed taxa may be strange by Earth's standards and need not align with plant, animal or the other Earth categories. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("Also remember that the taxa may get really weird by our standards because we need not algin with plant, animal, etc.") | Direction for the taxonomy |
| Rely more heavily on bioluminescence for light at night. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("We can also rely more heavily on bioluminsecence.") | Research direction; the [living-light study](studies/living_light/README.md) (2026-10-11) finds a beetle-class glow lighting a path at walking level for 2–10% of a plant's night store, the natural night serving walking on 48% of the dry land, and the land under a light-polluted sky falling to 0.9–40% at 4–8 billion lunar residents |
| The taxonomy's open choices follow from what kind of organisms the aerophytes become and what synthetic biology builds them; until those are known, the taxonomy works each choice out in general terms, conditioned on the path taken. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("Hmm I think we should tie that to what kind of organisms they end up being, what kind of synthetic biology we use, etc.  We can't do the full taxonomy until we know things like that, but you consider in general terms conditioned on what path is taken in the future."), answering the taxonomy's open choices O1–O6 and the class's scientific name; then, the same day, ("Record taxonomy as hold for later and continue other work.") | Direction for the taxonomy. On hold for later at the author's request the same day; the sources checked for it are kept on the research drive (ecology-runs/taxonomy_paths) |

## Phosphorus from the seas, 11 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Extract phosphorus from the seas for human use, as a distribution system for it, while keeping a decent natural cycle beside it. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("Regular extraction from the seas for human use totally makes sense and shouldn't be hard. And that would act as a major useful distribution system for phosphorus, though I still want to make sure we have a decent "natural" cycle as well.") | Direction: [resources/sea_phosphorus.md](../resources/sea_phosphorus.md) takes it up (2026-10-11). The rain-fed lakes catch 96% of the land's phosphorus; dredging their deltas can supply people's 0.18–1.44 Tg a year while the waters' dissolved phosphorus stays with their food webs and carriers |

## People on the Moon and beyond, 11 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Work with 4–8 billion people on the Moon itself, with plenty of it kept wild, the aerial life above all. Lighting can depart from Earth's where the darkest night is twilight, and rely more on bioluminescence. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("I'd say the world should be able to handle around 4  - 8 billion people itself, while still having plenty of space "wild" esp. with aerial life"; "Reasonable but be careful. Lighting does not have to work the same way as on Earth for many areas because many areas get twilight at darkest. We can also rely more heavily on bioluminsecence.") | Working range for step six. Studied on 2026-10-11: the [distributions study](studies/distributions/README.md) finds 60–94% of the dry land wild outside a kept 30% and 31–92% outside a kept half on the food study's power-heavy mix (61–94% before the refreshes of 12 October); at 8 billion outside half three placements together fit every corner, the dry highland towns poleward of 45° first among them; reviewed and corrected the same day. The choice among its four distributions is the author's |
| 2–4 billion of the Moon's people live aloft; sky towns move under active power, with tracking and air traffic control. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("Towns can have more active transport means however so that's not AS much of an issue. But yes tracking and air traffic control is very important still. 2 - 4 billion aloft seems reasonable.") | Working case for step six. Studied on 2026-10-11 (distributions): 348,000–696,000 sky towns 6.2–8.7 km apart outside a kept 30%, with 0.066–5.3 million conflicts a day across the fleet for tracking and traffic control; at the high-cost end the hydrogen remade gives the largest share of the Moon's heat |
| On land, people live mostly in the drier highlands, the summit metropolis round its tower above all, with many communities scattered elsewhere; cities can be taller at lunar gravity. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("For land, I think a lot of the plan was to have them more on the dryer highland areas, esp. that mega city with the grand tower or whatever we were going to call it.Though of course many other communities scattered around. Cities can also be taller on the Moon in general so that's a higher population density allowed which helps.") | Direction for step six. The [land-cities study](studies/land_cities/README.md) (2026-10-11) gives the high ground: 3.90 million km² of dry land 4 km or more above the sea, 91% on the far side, 1.35 million km² of it poleward of 30° and dry; all 6 billion of the ground's people at 10,000 per km² would settle 15% of it, at the metropolis's density 3.8%; towers rise three to four times as high for the same frame steel per floor, with heat and daylight capping density; water lifted to the highlands costs 0.9–2.7 GW per billion from the nearest lakes and 7–21 GW from the sea The distributions study (2026-10-11) puts the metropolis's 100 million and 1.1–4.4 billion in the dry highland cities poleward of 30°, settling 6.5–33% of the dry high ground outside a kept 30%. |
| The array and the Solar System's budding colonies carry a decent share of the people; Earth at 10–16 billion is a bit much. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("I would expect the array + people scattered through the solar system's budding colonies to carry a decent load. Earth's 10 - 16 billion is doable but a bit much.") | Direction for step six. Studied on 2026-10-11 (distributions): four distributions of 25–30 billion with Earth at 9, the array at 5–8 and the colonies at 3–5 billion; per billion the array needs 248–990 Gt of shield and 1,005 Gt of habitat, and the colonies 3.39–14.4 TW of primary energy |
| Count current and emerging agriculture, hydroponics and aquaponics among them, and the fog deserts with water extraction, in the food system. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("You seem to however be forgetting about current and emerging agriculture. You didn't seem to even consider hydroponics and aquaponics."; "there's also fog deserts and extraction that you don't seem to be considering") | Research direction, studied on 2026-10-11: the [food-systems study](studies/food_systems/README.md) prices eight systems per billion residents, their fit to the lunar cycle and seven mixes for 4, 6 and 8 billion with their feasible ranges, reviewed and corrected the same day; the choice of mix is the author's |
| Evaluate the large lava tubes as the first phase's habitats and as lasting cities, protected from water percolation. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("I think they'd have to be protected from water perculation, etc. but it's likely the first phase of Moon terraforming would be in those massive lava tubes as many plan. Some of those could easily remain large scale mega cities themselves.") | Research direction; first study done on 2026-10-11 ([land cities](studies/land_cities/README.md)). The 28% sea covers 13 of 17 mare pit sites, 12 of the 19 roofed-rille places and all three measured collapse chains; on land the rain lifts the water table over every pit floor within years to four centuries unless the basalt drains freely. Sealing to Earth's practice holds far deeper at lunar gravity, for 0.03–245 MW of pumping for a 1 km by 50 km tube. The mapped tubes hold 2–52 million people (5–129 million with the GRAIL candidates), and they matter most in the build's first steps, before the air's column matches Earth's at 16.8 kPa |
| Evaluate the division of labour, activities and synergies among places, so that regional weaknesses become strengths. | Author, 2026-10-11, added in the author's own words to a continuation note ("There is also an additional author request to evaluate division of labor and activities and synergies to help turn potential regional weaknesses into strengths."), with an example: "if (and maybe this is totally off base) the membrane system for the undersea cities ... and lava tubes deal with a lot of sea locked mineralsm etc. then maybe they can become good sources for agriculture, and support nearby agriculture" | Research direction for step six; studied on 2026-10-11 in the distributions study: the tubes' seepage watering coastal fields, the sea floor's and the lake deltas' sediment for phosphorus, the fog desert's glass drinking piped sea water, the dark belt's dark nights and the array's power behind Earth's food |
| Connect the undersea cities with the lava-tube habitation. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("the undersea cities were a few days back and I forgot to say that they should probably be connected/associated with the lava tube habitation") | Direction for step six and the lava-tube study. Studied on 2026-10-11 (distributions): six of the thirteen undersea communities lie within 250 km of a mapped submerged tube, and joined they hold 1.07–28.6 million people |
| The author's distribution lies closest to C: Earth 9 billion, the Moon 8 (4 on the ground and 4 aloft), the array 5 and the colonies 3. In the middle latitudes, about 30–49°, homestead communities live among the farms, and the rest of that belt's population goes where it is better sustained. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("Hmm I think let's drop the population there and distribute it where it's better sustained. C′ keeps C's heat, energy, water lift and wild land; outside a kept half its high-cost end fits with the dry highland towns poleward of 45° first and the scattered towns gathered on the twilit homes, once the homesteads' homes count within the fields they work. Reviewed the same day |
| At least half of the land and of the dark sky kept wild is preferred. It can come down to 30% wild with another 20% hybrid, a compromise between keeping 30% and keeping half: hybrid land is fairly wild, more heavily managed and carries more human activity, people's daily life at low density. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("Preferably at least 50%. Thus acting as a compromise between the two options."), answering how much land and dark sky to keep wild; then, on the earlier reading, ("You could have still kept (and you can restore if you can) the other run just as comparison.") | Working rule for step six. Applied on 2026-10-11 in the [food-systems](studies/food_systems/README.md) and [distributions](studies/distributions/README.md) studies. With at least half wild, the preference, C′ is the study's kept-half case: every corner fits through the poleward and twilit placements once the homesteads' homes count within their fields. Under the compromise the hybrid fifth is the author's megaparks and great gardens, farms and towns keep to the same half, and C′ fits every corner the same way, at the kept half's costs. While more dense populated areas need parks, etc. these would be more like megaparks and mega gardens and could even include food gardens. Their purpose however would be more liesure, entertainment, etc. the things that large public gardnes and parks are for."; then "Don't assume that other residents as well as visitors wouldn't use the parks and gardens too.", "We would rely on heavy automation." and "Small shops, restrooms of course, boutique, some places to rest, etc. would be in this region too, after all that's a lot of land, so it does need accomodations.") | Working rule for step six. Applied on 2026-10-11 in the [food-systems](studies/food_systems/README.md) and [distributions](studies/distributions/README.md) studies: the hybrid fifth is parkland, 4.68 million km² under the compromise, kept from farms, grazing, the homesteads' homes and towns, and C′ fits every corner as under half wild; the comparison of half wild with 20% hybrid takes the same parks. The parks' water, automation energy, amenities and night light remain to be sized |
| Every kind of land is heavily managed, the wild included, for inspection, evaluation and preservation; wild land need not serve regular human use. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("Indeed, even the fully "wild" lands would be heavily managed in terms of inspection, evaluation, preservation, etc. just managed as not necessarily for human regular use.") | Direction |
| Parks and gardens are built as far as possible around the lower water. In the fog deserts, where evaporation is slower, more reservoirs can feed manicured water features; much of the beauty comes from plants suited to dry conditions, in the highlands above all. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Parks and gardens should as much as possible be built around the lower water availability. In the fog deserts since evaporation would be slower we could have more resevoirs, etc. feeding artifiical manicured water features. But a lot of the beauty should come from plants, etc. that are well suited for the dryer conditions, esp. in the highlands.") | Direction for the parks' design; their water is sized under it |
| Parks and gardens are designed for richness within the dry land: water placed sparingly where it shows (still mirror pools and rills in the fog desert, stone and gravel rivers in the highlands, mulches that slow evaporation and take in the storms' rain, and living soil crusts, the parks' own treated water); dry-adapted planting after the richest dry floras, with silver foliage for the long night, regional palettes and dryland food gardens; the lunar cycle as the show, with bloom at each dawn and dusk and living light and night scent through the night; and shade structures and planting fitted to each slope's microclimate. Each garden is a monthly performance. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Seems good. Alright let's lock that in."), adopting the suggestions for rich gardens in dry land; the mulches' wording updated at the author's direction, 2026-11-03 ("Update the row wording") | Direction for the parks' design, set out in [habitation](../habitation/README.md#parks-and-gardens). At the resort's 301 K the deserts' nights run 57–60% humid, with the dew point 8.6–9.5 K below the air, so no mulch gathers dew; the mulches save water by slowing evaporation (session-tools/agents/stale_premises/stale_premises.md; [coast water](studies/coast_water/README.md)). |

## The Moon's regional variation, 11 October 2026

| Direction | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The Moon's organisms are told apart by regional variation across a connected world: the way round is short, no great oceans divide its land, and its skies join most of it. The parks and gardens stand apart from the wild by their intensive manicuring. | Author, 2026-10-11 ("We should assume that regional variation is what separates organisms of this world. It's a shorter distance around, no massive oceans to really separate land masses, and the skies really connect most of the world.The difference is extreme manicuring of the park and garden regions."), as the hybrid land became megaparks and great gardens (provisioning register) | Direction for ecology |

## Automation, the network and food, 12 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The Open Moon is heavily automated, as is the whole network it belongs to, the resource operation across the Solar System included, and the array supplies ample computing. Heavy automation keeps the cities functional, in good shape and clean. The megaparks and great gardens are almost purely automated, their amenities and food-service shops included. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("This world is HEAVILY automated, as is the whole network it belongs to, the resource extraction across the solar system would have been laregly automated, and we will have tons of compute available from the array. Habitation would be supported by heavy automation keeping cities functional, in good shape, and clean. The park/garden/recreation mega zones would be almost purely automated. That's nearly a given."; then "They would also largely manage the automated ammenities including food service shops there.") | Direction; it settles how much of the work is automated ([questions](../provisioning/questions.md), item 10). At Earth's staffing the parks alone would need 50 million to 1.5 billion gardeners ([habitation](../habitation/README.md#parks-and-gardens)); the machines' energy is still to size. The wild's monitoring and the machines in the living world are in the next section |
| The whole system is engineered for extreme uptime. Its machines going offline would mean the array was down, a catastrophe in its own right. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("How could it? If the system goes offline that would mean the array is down, and that's catostrophic. The whole system must be engineered for extreme uptime."), asked whether the living world should be able to survive a long outage of the machines | Requirement for every system. Millisecond control stays on the Moon ([computing](../provisioning/computing.md)), and the conservation study's [continuity rule](studies/conservation/README.md#8-stewardship-and-decisions) keeps more than one keeper of every life-support function |
| However fully automated the systems are, every consensus decision is made with the near-continual input of a well-educated, involved population. The decision-making system itself waits. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("I also want to add a small footnote. I don't want to deal with the whole decision making system right now. What I will say is that while "fully automated" every consensus decision would be made with the near continual input of a well educated involved population.") | Direction; the decision-making system belongs to the human paper and comes later |
| A continual network covers the whole Moon and connects to the array. A mesh of aerostats drifting near 30 km is the base layer. A small fleet of fixed posts at the same height serves places that need steady capacity or a long watch: the shared sky tracks, the edges of the wild, and storms and fires while they last. Tall structures such as the summit port's crown are fixed posts too; fibre and small towers serve settled ground and the parks; and the sky towns carry their own networks. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("I guess this set of rounds can also cover things like global monitoring, as we'll need a continual network across the whole world. For Earth that largely comes from satellites connecting local regions. On the Moon I'm guessing weather balloons could suffice? They can just acting as floating cell "towers" right? Though I'm not sure if the thick atmosphere would impact distance. Still, "line of sight" is pretty easy. Then it would just connect to the array."; then, offered a drifting mesh at 30 km, fixed nodes near 20 km or both, "Unsure of pros/cons.", and after the comparison "Going with your suggestion.") | Design direction; [network.md](../provisioning/network.md) holds the estimates. The far side's radio quiet passes to the conservation study's replacement, a radio array in deep space ([D3](studies/conservation/README.md#4-decisions)) |
| On the shared sky tracks, towns give way to sky reefs. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("I think towns give right of way to reefs."), asked whether towns give way or shepherds move reefs off the towns' tracks | Rule for the traffic on the shared tracks ([questions](../provisioning/questions.md), item 2): the towns' steering reserves are sized for meeting reefs, and the network's radar follows the reefs; refined on 2026-10-12 by the domain synthesis's decisions: reefs keep the preference if they become part of the useful balancing system, and towns otherwise get a bit more precedence |
| Food comes from a blend of the three mixes that fit under the author's wild share, power-heavy, power-made and fully lamp-lit, weighted to the optimum the food study sets. Small amounts of meat come from the homesteads and hobby farms as a luxury, from animals raised for it: homestead dairy and vaca vieja, some wagyu, heritage pork and chicken. Most "beef" is grown. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("For food, I would do a blend of the three that currently work to give a good distribution. Small amounts of meat can also come from the hobby farms/homesteading. Such would be a luxury and animals for such would be raised as. So as one minor example, when it comes to beef you would have homestead dairy and vaca vieja, you might have some wagyu, etc. while most of the "beef" would be "grown." Similar with heritage pork, chicken, etc."; then "Okay to close that last question you can set optimal blend weights.") | The author's choice of mix. The weights are set to the optimum on 2026-10-12 ([food study](studies/food_systems/README.md#the-authors-blend)): power-heavy 40%, power-made 50% and fully lamp-lit 10%, the blend that takes the least sunlight from wild life at the high-cost end with each mix between a tenth and a half of the diet, the limits setting the weights. At 8 billion its fields take 0.68–1.86 million km² since the refreshes of 12 October, none in the middle latitudes, and its heat is 15–171 TW with fusion. Heritage meat at a kilogram a person a year takes 0.30 million km², a fifth of the homestead region, and its cattle bring back the ruminants the lunar design left out (ecology, finding 25), with 1.6–1.7 Mt of methane a year that only soils remove. The mixes grow their protein as microbes and fungi; cultivated animal cells are not priced |
| The comparison run, half wild with another 20% hybrid, keeps the parks reading: its hybrid fifth is the same megaparks and great gardens. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Agreed."), to the recommendation to keep the parks reading over the earlier one, which read hybrid land as working countryside open to grazing and the homesteads' homes | Stands; the food and distributions studies already run it this way |

That can even go as far as having specialized automatons that use special signaling, and simply behavior, to direct and track wildlife, keep separation between wildlife and habitation, etc.") | Direction, building on the row of 11 October that every kind of land is heavily managed (People on the Moon and beyond); [machines.md](../biosphere/ecology/machines.md) sets out the machines' work. The section before this one holds the rest of the round: the whole system engineered for extreme uptime, the network, and towns giving way to sky reefs on the shared tracks |
| The machines join the living world in every way offered: automatons that behave as members of animal groups or as their predators; signals and reporting built into the organisms, a shared signal vocabulary, stress reporters and DNA watermarks among them; and machines holding ecological roles, such as carrying minerals back uphill beside the sea-to-forest migrants, controlled disturbance and shepherding in the sky. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("All of them."), offered behaviour only, signals and reporting built into the organisms, ecological roles, or all of them | Direction; [machines.md](../biosphere/ecology/machines.md) |

| Field machines and sensors are built to compost when lost. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Compost when lost is a good idea.") | Design rule; [machines.md](../biosphere/ecology/machines.md) |
| What may live wild follows general constraints that work on this open world, with no containment: the biomes are shaped by climate and the parks by intensive manicuring. The starting constraints: each design fits the climate where it is placed and has no advantage everywhere; it is harmless at the Moon's scale; its N2O, methane and ethylene stay within what the air's sinks remove; it returns what it takes, phosphorus above all; it carries the shared signals, reporting traits and a DNA watermark; and its safety rests on nothing evolution can switch off. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("And I don't think we can do much with containment nor do we need to. ## The domain synthesis's decisions, 12 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The levels at which methane and N2O are held wait for the gases' warming in the deep column, which is the next work. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Fair. Let's do that next then."), on the [domain synthesis](studies/domain_synthesis/README.md)'s first decision | Computed on 2026-10-12 ([reduced gases](../atmosphere/radiative_convective/README.md)): Earth's present levels force 4.7 W/m² all-sky in the Moon's deep column and the screen's central steady state 7.9–8.3 W/m², 4.9–8.3 K; the author's rule for the levels is the next row |
| Methane and N2O are held at whichever levels keep things closest to the already planned world, meaning a balanced choice that limits how far the set knobs move. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Whichever keeps things closest to the already planned world."), asked which levels to compute; then, on the levels first applied, "When I meant closest to already planned world, I did not necessarily mean moving from no motion of N2O constraints to meaning "none" but rather that we should find a balanced approach that  limits how much we have to adjust already set important knobs." | First applied the same day as the lowest levels the planned means reach, 0.3 ppm of methane and 0.03 ppm of N2O, which pushed biology's cuts and removal from open air hard; the author's explanation set that reading aside, and three pairs were weighed ([domain synthesis](studies/domain_synthesis/README.md#where-they-are-held), `gases.pairs_weighed`). Settled by the choice of 13 October (below) |
| Food and the sky towns run on power from the array, given a good recommendation for distributing it. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Array power if you have a good power distribution recommendation.") | Met on 12 October: the author took the recommendation as reported (next row) |
| Food and the sky towns take beamed power from the ring fleet on the recommended design, which sizes the whole Moon's intake: beams at 2.45 GHz straight to the sky towns' crowns; closed receivers at the ground's loads, with planting beneath the mesh, joined by a grid; several beams for every receiver, switched to another part of the ring before a sky reef crosses a column. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Beamed energy as you suggested then I suppose."), on the recommendation reported in full that day | Chosen. The [power screen](../provisioning/power.md) sized the [design](studies/domain_synthesis/README.md#power-from-the-array-the-chosen-distribution) the same day: the deep air takes 6.3–7.8% of the beams, so each watt delivered leaves 1.47–1.49 W of heat; the tiles make 47–224 TW, which the dimmer's light covers at the low-cost end; the ground's receivers take land equal to 36–59% of C′'s settlements; no polar cap is blind; and a fault across the ring leaves 2.5–7.5 TW of critical loads for a week's stores |
| Added dimming can be had by extracting more of the sunlight; the fleet's glow is the harder problem, and working it out is the task. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Well if I knew the answer to that... Dimming we can probably just extract more. Glow is harder to deal with. That's what we need to figure out..."), on how the added dimming is supplied beside the glow's answer | Direction; the glow's answer is in the fleet's night work ([plan](plan.md), next work item 7) |
| Heritage meat is 1–2 kg a person a year. High-efficiency feed production can cut its land, and wild culls and fishing can supply part of it if parasite risk is kept low. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("1 - 2kg a year. We can probably reduce that by using high efficiency production for feed. Wild cull, fishing, etc. if we can keep parasite risk low,  can also be used as part of that.") | Working range. At 1–2 kg the blend prices 0.30–0.60 million km² of pasture, 21–43% of the homestead belt, before feed and wild sources reduce it |
| Sky towns fly in the reefs' layer. If sky reefs become part of the useful balancing system they get more preference; otherwise they are an interesting feature, and sky towns get a bit more precedence. A somewhat higher sky-reef density is acceptable. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("It would take a decent density of sky reefs for that to become a problem. I'd suggest that if sky reefs can become part of the useful balancing system, then give them more preference, otherwise they're an interesting feature and sky towns get a bit more precedence. I'd be okay leaning towards a bit higher sky reef density though.") | Rule for the traffic on the shared tracks, refining the row of 12 October that towns give way to reefs; at 1% cover a town passes 0.04–1.2 reefs a day ([synthesis](studies/domain_synthesis/README.md#the-skys-layers)) |
| The network's stability decides whether its nodes ride through the storm tops that reach 30 km or climb round them. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Stability of the network should decide.") | Rule for the network's constellation model (the synthesis's next work, item 7) |
| The taxonomy stays on hold. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Indeed. We hold that.") | On hold, as recorded on 11 October |
| The carbon cycle is evaluated next, with the project's present parameters. | Author, 2026-10-12 ("Also another thing we need to evaluate coming up is the carbon cycle under the updated parameters.") | Done the same day ([carbon cycle](studies/carbon_cycle/README.md)). Left alone once the biosphere is grown, the air falls below the plants' comfortable 43 Pa in 135–376 years. Holding 400 ppm takes 0.14–0.39 Gt C a year of return or import at year 10,000: kilns of 19–58 GW return the carbonate as it forms, and the lakes' organic burial, the largest sink, leaves an import of 0.09–0.27 Gt C a year. The build brings 985–1,354 Gt C by the year the biosphere is grown, with methane held at the author's choice of 1.9 ppm. Revised on 14 October with the groundwater ([springs](studies/springs/README.md)): the air falls to 43 Pa in 207–447 years; kilns of 3.9–14 GW; an import of 0.12–0.33 Gt C a year at year 10,000; a build of 951–1,251 Gt C |

## The gas pair, 13 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Methane and N2O are held at the middle of the three pairs weighed: 1.9 ppm of methane, Earth's present level, and 0.1 ppm of N2O, a third of Earth's. Claude keeps the pair while it continues to make sense. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("Middle pair it is most likely then. And you can choose that if it continues to make sense."), after the pros and cons of the three pairs | Applied the same day ([domain synthesis](studies/domain_synthesis/README.md#where-they-are-held), `gases.held`): 3.58 W/m² all-sky in the deep column, answered by 1.69% added dimming over the sunlight the Moon absorbs, against 0.83% for the low pair and 2.23% for Earth's 2019 air. With biology at the sources cutting 90% of the methane, contactors pass 0.03–0.09% of the air a year once methane reaches 1.9 ppm; N2O's decomposition takes 0.29–4.4 TW across the denitrification bracket once N2O reaches 0.1 ppm, 365–5,610 years from none. The light study checks the pair against what the dimming costs the biosphere |
| Where and when light limits growth, if anywhere, is worked out: it bears on the gas pair, and it is worth knowing in general. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("One thing to consider: when/where if at all IS light the limiting growth factor. It's good to know in general anyway.") | Done the same day ([light limitation](studies/light_limitation/README.md)). Water holds most of the land: the rain supports 55–57% of the canopy's light-limited growth within 15° of the equator and less poleward. Light limits the wet margins, the lakes, the river-fed coasts and the sky, 10–40% of the living Moon's growth, and matters most at dawn, dusk and in the twilight; each 1% of dimming costs 0.07–0.30% of the growth through light, and the plan's 4.6–6.4% costs 0.3–1.9%. In Earth's models the same dimming takes about 1% of the rain for each 1%, which the Moon's GCM would size; the middle pair stays the balanced one |
| The GCM runs again with the gases, the glow and the dimming, since the gas pair and the dimming's cost wait on how the rain answers them. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("I guess I really can't make any good decision until we rerun the GCM. Let's do it."), after the light study found that the rain's response to the dimming could cost the biosphere more than the light | Done the same day on domain/ecology ([gases, glow and dimming](studies/gases_glow_dimming/README.md)): three 20-year branches of the design case. The middle pair's 1.69% holds the design temperature and takes 0.9% of the rain, about half Earth's G1 response, costing 0.5–0.7% of the living Moon's growth through the rain-fed land besides 0.1–0.5% through light; the low pair would save 0.3–0.6% of the growth and Earth's present air would cost 0.2–0.4% more, so the middle pair stays the balanced one. The glow's infrared heats the upper air and about 4% reaches the ground: matched watt for watt it cools the Moon 1.3 K, and the dimming that holds the design temperature is 0.71 of watt for watt, 1.80% for its infrared, about 2.0% of the design sunlight with the returned sunlight against the glow study's 2.7%. Held there, the gases and the glow take 3.4% of the rain (4.0% over land) and 2.3–3.4% of the growth through it; the glow's part, 2.4% of the rain and 1.8–2.6% of the growth, is the larger |
| The glow's further answers, the annulus absorber and the ultraviolet mirror, are set aside: the rain the glow takes is made up by the plants' water design. The glow's dimming carries the GCM's 0.71 of its infrared's watts. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("If we can do other things to compensate like funky ecology then I don't think it's worth it."; then, on the recording of it, "Go ahead.") | Applied the same day ([gases, glow and dimming](studies/gases_glow_dimming/README.md#what-it-finds)): plants that fix 3.2% more carbon per litre of water, or pass a 3.2% larger share of what the land evaporates, restore the glow's part of the rain-fed growth, and 4.1% the whole plan's; the dry bands need more, 2.0% within 15° of the equator, 4.0% at 15–30°, 5.6% at 30–45° and 11% at 45–60°. Ecology's plants carry it next. The 0.71 went into the glow study at the integration of 17 October ([glow](studies/solar_shield_array/glow.md)): 2.0% of the design sunlight for the recommended answer with its returned sunlight, about 1% below the domain synthesis's convention |

## The seas through time, 13 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The seas' make-up is worked out from the water's delivery through the world's billion-year life, with fresh, salted and phosphorus-added water, so that the state the seas are to hold, stable or dynamic, can be chosen. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("Might as well just so we have it. While we're focusing on the first thousands of years we also have been considering long term across billion years lifespan of this world. So it's something to consider what kind of stable or dynamic state we want the seas/oceans to be."), on resources' question 9 | Done the same day ([sea chemistry](studies/sea_chemistry/README.md)). Left alone, the seas turn alkaline: the Moon's rock gives the rivers carbonate with almost no chloride or sulphur, so fresh seas reach pH 9 in 13,000–34,000 years, a soda water of Earth's salinity in 24 million and a saturated soda brine in 200 million; delivered salt keeps its chloride but turns alkaline the same way. Sediment and the seas' own deposits fill the lakes in 0.2–6.8 million years and the seas in 0.7–12 million. Every lasting state is a held one: calcifiers' calcite, magnesium made into magnesite or clays, soda works and silica-building life hold fresh, brackish or salted seas where the delivery sets them for the billion years, with the land's sediment returned and the deposits taken out. Phosphorus follows the lakes' trap: 0.4–23 mg/m³ in fresh seas while it holds, 7–350 once the lakes are full. Revised on 14 October with the groundwater and the floors ([springs](studies/springs/README.md)): pH 9 in 30,000–60,000 years, a soda water of Earth's salinity in 27–38 million and a saturated brine in 220–300 million; the seas fill in 0.8–16 million years |
| The seas are held fresh, as hard fresh water near 0.14 g/kg and pH 8.3, on the condition that they are rich in life. Ecology designs their communities for high biodiversity, as Earth's old, clear fresh lakes hold hundreds of endemic species through variety of habitat and time: rocky shores, shallows, deltas, reefs and deeps. Phosphorus from the dredged deltas can go to the open seas if they need more production. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("Keeping fresh is fine as long as they're rich in biodiversity and not life poor."; then "Proceed.") | Direction. The held state takes the four flows and the sediment upkeep the [sea chemistry](studies/sea_chemistry/README.md) sets out; the seas' communities are ecology's design ([question 10](../biosphere/ecology/questions.md)) |

## The terrain through time, 13 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| How long the young Moon's terrain lasts and what renews it is worked out: which lakes, ponds and landforms fill or wear down without upkeep, and when; and what each way of rebuilding costs. The ways are the returned sediment and the seas' deposits as blocks and terraces for built land, machines carrying sediment back uphill for the wild, dredged deltas to keep lakes open, and living builders, dam builders and travertine, for relief at small scales. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("How much of that do we have planned kept vs eventually allowed to degrade away? We've only got certain planned preserved sites so far. I need to figure out general terrain. The question is what would reasonably work to rebuild that terrain? I guess automation, as I'm not sure we can trust biological processes to rebuild entire terrain. Obviously we could take the sediment return and build blocks and build massive buildings and terraces, and for the planned gardens and parks, that would be a great option. But not sure about the wild parts."; then "Proceed.") | Done the same day ([terrain through time](studies/terrain_through_time/README.md)). Only conservation's heritage sites are kept so far. Left alone, the small waters go first: at the central erosion 43% of the 28,480 rain-fed lakes are filled within 10,000 years, mostly small ones, while 98% of the lake area stays open; lakes under 10 km² last a median 4,600 years and the largest 1.5 million, and craters of 10–100 m below the grid lose half their depth in 57–44,000 years. The relief halves in 4–170 million years. Keeping lakes and relief is one loop, the deltas dredged and the rock carried back uphill as a dust fall of 1–42 mm a century, for 0.2–11 GW; the seas' 0.39 km³ a year of calcite, magnesite and opal is stone for the built land (0.23 since the springs study of 14 October, when the seas fill in 0.9–15 million years). Travertine builds on most rivers for the first 300–10,000 years while the young soils weather fast, and dam builders work the small streams below the 1.9 km grid |
| The young-Moon landforms to keep are chosen as a set, like the heritage register but for terrain, in the next round, which builds the atlas out in detail down to districts. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("And yes, I agree. I was planning on the next round after we work these details out to be a significant round of atlas building, even going as far as mapping out districts."), on choosing the landforms to keep | Direction for the atlas round |
| Delta growth joins the terrain study for the atlas round: where each delta forms, how fast it advances and the land and marsh it makes, from each lake's depths. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("Okay. Do so."), on adding delta growth per inflow | Done the same day ([terrain through time](studies/terrain_through_time/README.md#deltas)). Deltas move the shorelines first: at the central erosion, delta plains cover 54,000 km² of the lakes within 1,000 years and 256,000 km² within 10,000, 6.5% of their area (1.3–18% across the cases), against the 87,000 km² the lakes taken as whole boxes lose; the common-level waters gain 16,000 and 104,000 km² of new land. The largest deltas reach 9–13 km from their mouths in 10,000 years, and some fill their whole lake |
| Water delivery continues after the build so that the seas stay balanced against what the crust takes up; the water table is still to be worked out. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("We would continue to transport water I suppose so that it balances. So that at least deals with some of the issue in that regard. Still, water table is important to know.") | Working direction. Sized the same day by the groundwater model's first stage ([geography](../geography/README.md#groundwater-how-fast-the-crust-takes-the-water)): the crust takes 1,500–4,100 km³ a year through the first thousand years at the central values and 1,700 to 10,000, falling to 240 by 100,000; without the top-up the seas would fall 370 m within a thousand years |
| Groundwater is modelled: the crust's uptake through time, the water table on land with its springs, baseflow and lake seepage, and the springs' load; its preparation runs beside the delta work without going far. | Author, 2026-10-13 ("I've been meaning to do groundwater modeling, because that's a big left open issue."; then "I would do prep work in parallel (don't go overboard) to save time."; then "Proceed."; then "Continue"; then, on 14 October, "Proceed."; then "Then run a general analysis and ideation on the side while you work on carrying the weathering correction and the filling crust's carbon into the carbon cycle, and the sea floors' share into the sea chemistry..") | Stage 1 done the same day ([geography/groundwater_uptake.py](../geography/README.md#groundwater-how-fast-the-crust-takes-the-water)). The airless crust is cold near the surface and hot below its insulating megaregolith, so rock below freezing reaches 240 m down at the equator and 1.5–5 km near the poles; the planned climate thaws it in 530 years at the equator to 56,000–250,000 near the poles. Once thawed the crust takes water fast: a global layer of 103 m within 1,000 years (14–296 across the cases), 514 m within 10,000 (198–840) and 1,075 m within 100,000, 0.36, 1.8 and 3.8 times the seas. On land it drinks most of the rain that soaks in for thousands of years. Stage 2 followed ([the water table](../geography/README.md#groundwater-the-water-table-on-land)): in the mature Moon groundwater comes out at the ground across a third of the land (three quarters of the tropics, little poleward of 45°) and lies a median 364 m down elsewhere; it carries a quarter of the rivers' flow; nearly all the lake area takes it in, and the lakes losing fast lie perched near the poles; and the young Moon is drier, the middle latitudes most of all while their crust fills. Stage 3, the springs' load, followed on 14 October ([springs](studies/springs/README.md)): the springs' water is young, a median 76 years in the mature Moon, and carries about the soil water's load, since below the soil it has only the CO2 it took up there; the rock lays the rest of the calcium as calcite underground, so the springs are moderately hard (65–80 mg/L), alkaline magnesium waters in the young Moon, and build almost no travertine. The land's weathering reaching the waters is 59% of the carbon cycle's count in the mature Moon and 20–49% in the young Moon, chiefly because nearly half of the rivers' flow is rain on the lakes; the filling crust takes 0.05–0.08 Gt C a year down with its water over the first 10,000 years. At the author's word the same day the carbon cycle and the sea chemistry took them in ([carbon cycle](studies/carbon_cycle/README.md), [sea chemistry](studies/sea_chemistry/README.md)): the seas reach three times calcite saturation two to four thousand years later, the kilns return a fifth to two fifths of what they did, and holding 400 ppm imports 0.12–0.33 Gt C a year at year 10,000 against 0.09–0.27; the filling crust holds 191–676 Gt C by then; left alone, the seas reach pH 9 in 30,000–60,000 years |
| The deserts get a general analysis and ideation beside the groundwater work, from the author's Codex conversation on their climate: what they are, their water, living with them and ideas for using and shaping them. | Author, 2026-10-14 ("So you would consider it related. Then run a general analysis and ideation on the side"), on the Codex thread Explore desert climate potential | Working note on the research drive (deserts/desert_analysis.md, with its sources), for the author's review |

## The curved air's light, 14 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The GCM and CM1 take the Moon's curved air into account: an energy ledger of the spherical air against flat columns of the same air, by ground, height and Sun angle, for the sunlight and the infrared; terms from it with the Moon's own horizon slant path; the GCM tested over 20 years with them and the 45° rings rerun with them on the design forcing and ground. | Author, 2026-10-14 ("I don't think either CM1 or the GCM considered scattering quite enough, though it's possible it makes little difference.", on another session's finding that neither model lights the air below each column's horizon; then "then proceed with the proposed plan") | Done on domain/ecology. The ledger is done ([the curved air's light](../climate/gcm/README.md#the-curved-airs-light)): at the design column's temperatures the curved geometry takes in 10.3 W/m² more sunlight than flat columns and lets out 16.2 W/m² more infrared, 5.8 W/m² net, nearly all of it from the shells' widening; its curved light and escape alone give +0.7 W/m², and its twilight puts 1.4 W/m² on the ground with the Sun below the horizon (the corrected ledger, whose channels keep the heights of the absorbed sunlight as well as its totals). Over 20 years the GCM with the first ledger's terms settles 0.65 K cooler than the design case, its clouds giving back most of the geometry's loss, and its rain moves a little poleward. The 45° rings ran again on the corrected terms and the new design case's forcing (two rows below). |
| PlaSim's misplaced sunlight is checked where it matters most, at the exobase and on the ground: the middle atmosphere's design cases again with the curved light's heating, with the thermal column from the new base; its column with the sunlight shared among the GCM's layers as PlaSim shares it; and, the ground moving more than about 0.3 K, CO₂ and O₂ bands for PlaSim fitted to the line-by-line model, its water multiplier refitted and another 20-year GCM test. | Author, 2026-10-14 ("The main things I'm concerned with right now is exobase stability and ground conditions. Though I still want to understand what the issue is either way.", on finding that PlaSim puts about 10 W/m² of its sunlight too low; then "Agreed with your proposed checks.") | Done the same day on domain/ecology. The twilight warms the 0.3 Pa base 3.6–5.6 K and the exobase with it; behind the film alone the exobase stays at 146–198 K, 50–100 K under the 250 K target, and the losses stay negligible ([middle atmosphere](../atmosphere/middle_atmosphere/README.md#the-curved-airs-light-2026-10-14)). PlaSim's sharing is worth 0.88 K of ground in the column. Its bands held no CO₂ or O₂, whose lines the deep column takes high up; with them 0.19 K is left, water vapour's, just under the tropopause, where Lacis and Hansen's curve of growth takes the sunlight too low. In the GCM the bands clear the high cirrus and the Moon settles near 292.0 K, 2.9 K under the design case, with 7.6% less rain and 43.4% of the land under 0.5 mm/day against 42.2% ([the curved air's light](../climate/gcm/README.md#the-curved-airs-light)); holding the design temperature would take about 1.5–1.7% less dimming. |
| The GCM's corrected radiation becomes its standard from now on: the curved air's light, the CO₂ and O₂ bands, and water vapour's high lines, PlaSim's water path given a pressure exponent fitted to the line-by-line model. The cooling it finds is answered with less dimming, holding the design temperature, which gives back some of the rain. | Author, 2026-10-14 ("I would answer the cooling with less dimming. That should give us back some lushness too. Let's fix the water and let the bands become the GCM's standard from now on as suggested."), on the bands' 20-year test | Applied on domain/ecology. The curved air and the bands are the runner's standard, with the multipliers refitted with them. A pressure exponent on PlaSim's water path does not move its sunlight up (every exponent below 1 fits the layers worse), so the water keeps Lacis and Hansen's form for now; what remains is worth 0.19 K of ground in the column. The design temperature needs a sunlight scale near 0.970, about 3.0% dimming in place of 5%: at 0.9685 the Moon settles at 294.76 K with 2.5% more rain than the design case ([the GCM's account](../climate/gcm/README.md#the-curved-airs-light)). The gases' and the glow's added dimming wait to be measured again under the new standard. |
| The design case under the corrected radiation is a 20-year run at the sunlight scale of 0.970 that lands on the design temperature. It becomes the design case for the climatology and the CM1 forcing, and the four rings along the 45° parallels, with and without the curved air's terms, run again on it; the oblique rings can follow later. The machine rests an hour before the run, half an hour after it, and half an hour in every couple of hours of the rings. | Author, 2026-10-14 ("Accepted. But let the computer rest for another hour and then another half hour after.", on the proposed design case; then, on rerunning the four rings, "After that half hour rest, and rest a half hour every couple of hours."; on 2026-10-15, told they are the parallels' rings, "Sounds like these rings will give us the most detail on what leaving out twilight missed. We can always run the obliques later."; on 2026-10-16, on the proposal to keep the southern twilight ring and skip its control, "Drop the control.") | Done on domain/ecology. `A28_corrected_970`, from the 0.9685 run's year-19 restart, settles at 295.02 K at the ground and 294.27 K at 2 m with 2.31 mm/day of rain, 3.3% more than the old design case ([the GCM's account](../climate/gcm/README.md#the-curved-airs-light)). The rings `ring_45{n,s}_corrected` and `ring_45{n,s}_corrected_twilight`, set up from it and smoke-tested, run from the evening of 14 October in two-hour blocks with half-hour rests ([the CRM's account](../climate/crm/README.md#the-45-rings-on-the-corrected-radiations-design-case)). The north pair is done: the curved air's light changes its ground by hundredths of a kelvin, and on the design ground the ring is a desert, with no rain or fog and afternoons near 38 °C. The south pair paused at day 7 of its twilight ring for the Jura Island box, on its own branch `research/jura-island`, and its twilight ring resumed from its restart on 16 October and ran to its end. Its control is dropped: the north pair settled the twilight's local effect at hundredths of a kelvin, and the twilight ring alone gives the southern climate on the design ground, whose land is about three times as wet as the north's. |

The work has its own branch off domain/ecology. | Author, 2026-10-15 ("Yeah I agree. ## Salt water and the fresh seas, 15 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The water table by the shorelines is worked out further, with the seas' freshness weighed: the ground under every shore is fresh to its base and fed by the sea, with no salt wedge and no salt in the spray, and lunar gravity lifts water higher in the soil. The seas stay fresh or near fresh. | Author, 2026-10-15 ("We considered the water table a little bit but we need to consider a little more by shorelines, and also we didn't consider enough the fact that the seas/oceans of this world are freshwater and probably will be maintained as such or near freshwater except for maybe a few intentionally maintained for saltwater life.") | Direction. Screened the same day ([geography](../geography/README.md#shores-by-the-fresh-seas)): plants draw on the water table from 15–25 m above the sea against Earth's 6–10, flat shores' belts are 0.6–2.5 km wide, low islands up to about a kilometre across stay wooded on groundwater alone, and the sea's load settles where the draw ends, mostly as a hardpan. The shore study, groundwater stage 4, is [question 16](../resources/questions.md), proposed and not started. |
| Saltwater life: the author leans to keeping it in aquariums over maintaining ocean-like salt seas, which would need their chlorine brought, since the Moon's own closed lakes turn to soda. | Author, 2026-10-15 ("Okay so we'd still need some maintained Earth ocean-like seas or just do aquariums (might just be better todo that and leave the soda lakes as their own local ecosystem)") | Leaning, open. The salt each separate sea would need, 2,600 Gt for Moscoviense to 174,000 for South Pole–Aitken, the chlorine's sources and the soda path are in [the sea chemistry](studies/sea_chemistry/README.md#closed-lakes-soda-lakes-and-salt-seas). The soda lakes are ecology's ([Soda lakes](#soda-lakes-15-october-2026)). Whether the delivered water's own chlorine is cleaned out is [question 17](../resources/questions.md). |

## Soda lakes, 15 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Soda lakes, which the Moon's own water makes in closed basins in the dry belts, are left as their own local ecosystems: a biome that may be fairly common on the Moon. | Author, 2026-10-15 ("(might just be better todo that and leave the soda lakes as their own local ecosystem). Actually good to know about the soda lakes. It brings up a very interesting biome that may be somewhat common in this world."), on whether the load gathering by the fresh seas' shores could make salt lakes | Direction. The Moon's water carries calcium, magnesium and a little sodium with carbonate and almost no chloride, so a closed lake keeps sodium carbonate at a pH near 10, reaching the ocean's salinity in about 100,000 years at 10 m deep ([the sea chemistry](studies/sea_chemistry/README.md#closed-lakes-soda-lakes-and-salt-seas)). Their communities are [question 10](../biosphere/ecology/questions.md) and where they lie belongs to the biome map, question 9 ([ecology](../biosphere/ecology/README.md#fresh-shores-and-soda-lakes-15-october-2026)). |

## The rings' missing transport, 16 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The heat budget of the 45° N ring's lower air is worked out alongside the current work, and a ring with the GCM's sideways heat and vapour imposed by height tests whether that transport explains the warm drift of CM1's forced rings and boxes; then a short GCM run measures the eddies its 3-day means miss. | Author, 2026-10-16 ("Let's do the heat budget alongside current work then."; on the proposed confirming ring, "Do it."); 2026-10-17, on the eddy measurement and the glow pair, "Let's proceed."; on checking CM1's radiation, "Proceed."; on the GCM's heating by process, "Do it." | Done on domain/ecology. Below 8 km at 45° N the GCM's circulation carries about 15 W/m² of heat sideways out of the air, which a ring cannot do. With it imposed (`ring_45n_corrected_sideways`), the warm offset over the second lunar day falls by about a third, from 5.9 to 3.7 K in the lowest 1.8 km and from 5.8 to 4.0 K at 1.8–6.5 km, and the lowest layer still warms 0.05 K a day. Two more years of the design case writing every 6.5 hours (`A28_corrected_970_eddies`, 17 October) show that 3-day means miss only 2% of the eddies' transport, so the rest lies in how the two models shed the ground's heat. The ring's own cloud-free columns through the atmosphere domain's correlated-k model show CM1's radiation cooling its lower air 4.5–10 W/m² a layer more than the line-by-line model, and its sunlight was within 2.5 points of it, so the warmth comes from elsewhere. A year of the design case writing PlaSim's heating by process shows its longwave cooling its lowest 1.8 km 10.6 W/m² more than the correlated-k model, and the next layer 11.4 W/m² more, against RRTMG's 4.5 and 8.5, so part of the gap is the GCM running cool, about 1.5 K of it, and against the line-by-line model both over-cool their lower air ([the CRM's account](../climate/crm/README.md#what-else-divides-cm1-and-the-gcm-at-the-crossing)). |

## The GCM's high cloud, 17 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Before any long run with the longwave correction, PlaSim's high cloud is looked into: its humidity thresholds, how coarse its top levels are, and CM1 as a check that may itself be tuned to Earth. | Author, 2026-10-17, choosing to look into the high cloud first ("If I had a computer to run endless simulations on it would be one thing, because it would also give us a good before and after if we ran it again, but each simulation taxes this machine. So at least take a look into PlaSim's high cloud. It is comforting knowing that CM1 tends to agree there, but still worth a look. CM1 also may be too tuned towards Earth so it's a good check.") | Done on domain/ecology the same morning: PlaSim's cloud starts near where ice appears by itself in clean air; its top layers are Earth's in pressure, each spanning about 25 K; CM1's ice above 107 km came from its start and its air below is held to the GCM's vapour; RRTMG and correlated-k agree on the correction's upper cooling; PlaSim gave the cold cloud liquid water's optics ([the GCM's account](../climate/gcm/README.md#the-high-cloud-and-its-ice)). |
| PlaSim's cloud colder than −38 °C takes ice optics, CAM3's as RRTMG carries them, with the crystal size set by temperature from Kristjánsson and Mitchell's table, keeping its thresholds and its cloud water; the patch is tested offline and in a year beside the longwave check year, and the 20-year test of the correction then runs with it. The high cloud's amount gets a range from its water and its crystals' size, and a CM1 ring that makes its own upper vapour waits for later. The CM1 runner's sounding above the GCM's top keeps that level's share of ice saturation, and the glow pair on the current radiation is analysed. | Author, 2026-10-17, on the look's findings ("Alright. I concur with everything here. Proceed.") | Done on domain/ecology, except the high cloud's range and the ring with its own upper vapour: the patch's Fortran matches its Python copy to nine figures and, switched off, repeats the longwave check year exactly; in a year from the design case ice halves the new high cloud's net warming, 5.7 W/m² in place of 12.4, with the top of the air still gaining 13 W/m². The 20-year test (`A28_corrected_970_lw_ice`) settles near 307 K, 306.5 K over its years 10–19 ([where the corrected Moon settles](../climate/gcm/README.md#where-the-corrected-moon-settles)); the sounding is fixed for new rings; the glow pair is analysed (row above). Below 180 K the crystal size follows the table's own trend, and below 190 K the optics follow Ebert and Curry's formulas past the 10 µm they were fitted down to. |

## The target climate, 17 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Before any more runs, beyond the 20-year test then running, the target climate is worked out: whether the Moon is brought back to the design temperature, with more dimming tested, or kept hotter and wetter provided it stays comfortable, its relief found in the cooler and wintry weather aloft, in the summer heat some people seek and along its long coasts, perhaps chiefly as a world for recreation. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("I'm not convinced we need to cool the world back down. I need to think about whether it's worth more dimming and testing on more dimming, etc. We still get cooler weather in the skies, including winter weather so I'm not convinced that we have to cool the world back down. A very warm lush Moon might be fine as long as there's still plenty of comfort. Plus some people love very warm summer weather and there's a lot of beachfront, right? So... might be fun as a largely recreation world. So I think before we run more stuff (except for what's running now) we need to figure that out..."), after the 20-year test's first years | Answered on 19 October: the resort at 301 K is the design climate ([below](#the-design-climate-and-the-waters-19-october-2026)), and the runs that follow it wait for the author's go-ahead with their machine time. The 20-year test settles near 307 K ([climate/gcm](../climate/gcm/README.md#where-the-corrected-moon-settles)): over its years 10–19, on the model's own land and weighted by area, the land's air at 2 m has a median of 33.6 °C and its wet-bulb temperature a median of 26.9 °C, above 28 °C for 31% of the land's time (22.3% at the lowest level, about 0.9 km up, which the first readout of 17 October, counting cells alike with the output's humidity as written, put at 17.6%); the seas' surface has a median of 34.5 °C; the land under 0.5 mm of rain a day falls from 37.6% to 18.9% counting every cell's land, and from 42.3% to 20.1% on the model's own land cells, the rain still heaviest near the equator; the median of the land's columns crosses freezing 36 km above the ground, against 23 km in the design case. [What each target climate takes](studies/climate_targets/README.md) sets the three targets side by side. The design case for the chosen target, the glow pair from it, and geography's lakes and the climate's other readers follow the choice |
| The three targets are the hot world, where the corrected Moon settles near 307 K; the lock, the design temperature of 295 K the plan has held since 9 October; and between them the resort, a tropical resort Moon in keeping with the 20% of the land kept as hybrid land, the manicured megaparks and gardens of 11 October. The author leans towards the resort and finds the choice hard. The lock takes much more of the array, where a dimmer of little energy and propellant is already hard to find. The hot world stays possible, since its fresh seas hold more dissolved oxygen at saturation than salt seas would at the same temperature, but it remains harsh, and its extreme heat reaches more of the land than first counted. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("'Resort' is the middle target. I believe that is the term we decided on when discussing it earlier. It's  possible the context was lost. I was saying something like possibly being okay with a sort of tropical resort world, esp. considering we plan to devote 20% of the surface to basically park, garden, and ammenities. But I'm still not sure which of the three is really the best and it's hard to decide. I'm leaning towards the 'resort' world only because it'll require a lot of extra array work and it's already difficult enough finding a low energy/propollent option there. The hot world could work because oxygen saturation is less of an issue with fresh water but it's still rough, and now with the fact that the area where we are dealing with extreme heat is worse, that leans more towards resort or original lock, with lock more  being considered weakly because it would require so much extra screen work."), after the area-weighted heat figures | Named; the choice stays open. The resort is the study's 300 K: the land's wet-bulb temperature at 2 m has a median of 21.1 °C and a 99th percentile of 26.0 °C, the seas' surface a median of 28.6 °C, and it takes 5.2–6.5% of the sunlight beyond the plan's additions ([what each target climate takes](studies/climate_targets/README.md)) |
| Winter should probably reach low enough that at least the tallest sky towers experience it; the tallest tower already stands near the upper limit for sky towers, with little room to grow. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("One design consideration I do want to bring up is that it would probably be good to have 'winter' low enough that at least the tallest sky towers can experience it.  And I yjink our tallest already kind of gets close to the upper limit of sky towers I think. And with not too much room to spare.") | Worked out the same day ([winter on the tower](studies/climate_targets/README.md#winter-on-the-tower)), reading winter as mean air at or below 0 °C over the summit's column. The tower's top, 24 km above the summit, keeps a mean below freezing up to 303.8 K, and its warmest 6.5-hour mean stays below freezing up to 302.6 K, on two design-case years. Keeping 2 km of the tower below freezing in the mean puts the limit at 302.25 K, where the top's warmest 6.5-hour mean is −0.4 °C and the air 2 km below the top has a 0 °C mean with its warmest weather near +1.2 °C. The freezing level rises about 1.2 km a kelvin across 298–303 K. Above about 296 K the open winter parks at 13.5–15 km lose their winter, which moves up to the sealed disks and the crown. A design case at the chosen target settles the figures |
| The resort may vary a little near 300 K, to whatever value suits the other constraints best. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("Resort can vary a little bit near 300 K whichever is most ideal against our other constraints."); then, given the study's recommendation of 301 K, 2026-10-18 ("301K it is.") | Set at 301 K by the author on 18 October, the study's recommendation within 300–302 K ([the resort's best value](studies/climate_targets/README.md#the-resorts-best-value)). The tower's winter caps the band near 302 K; the dimming, the rain and the deserts favour its warm end, comfort at the ground, the waters' oxygen and the margin under a saturated column its cool end. Against 300 K, 301 K takes 0.7–1.0 points off the dimming and raises the ground's 2 m wet-bulb 99th percentile from 26.0 to 26.8 °C |
| The glow study and the domain synthesis keep the glow's efficacy at 0.71 until the glow pair reruns on the final radiation; [what each target climate takes](studies/climate_targets/README.md) carries the range 0.645–0.71 meanwhile. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("I think you suggested at the glow pair's rerun and that seemed right."), asked whether the glow study and the domain synthesis should take the efficacy range now or at the glow pair's rerun | Waits on the glow pair, which reruns from the design case for the chosen target |
| How well the fresh seas and lakes water their coasts and islands is tested at the resort's 301 K: the groundwater within plants' reach under the deserts by the seas, islands and lakes, then the night's dew and fog on the shores against inland, as light compute beside the lake work and by one agent that starts no agents of its own; a two-dimensional CM1 slice from sea to dry land is planned and costed only. | Author, 2026-10-18 ("I don't think we tested how well the freshwater seas and lakes water their coasts and islands under this world's physics, right? If not is that heavy compute or light compute and more just setup and analysis?"); then, given the groundwater reach and the dew and fog first with the slice after, "Alright set that up with a new subagent. Make sure it doesn't spawn any subagents of its own." and "Light compute for the groundwater reach research can occur alongside existing lake work." | Done 18 October on `domain/climate` ([coast water](studies/coast_water/README.md)), independently reviewed and corrected: 0.4–1.5% of the desert land within plants' reach (0.40–1.07% at the central crust, with the 100 m check's ratios from four non-desert coasts taken to carry over to the deserts); 394–575 small islands green throughout by the strips, 189–387 as discs; the night's dew and fog add almost nothing (desert nights 57–60% humid where dew needs about 90%); the deserts at 301 K are 22.5–28% of the land as the interpolation's form leaves them; the slice recommended at 500 m once a design case at 301 K exists and the coasts are designed, 3.4–4.2 hours a slice |
| The comparison gives the resort's deserts at 301 K as a range, 22.5–28.1% of the land (24.8–31.5% on the model's own cells), between carrying each cell's rain between the two runs by a fixed amount and by a fixed factor a kelvin, until a design case at 301 K settles it. | Author, 2026-10-19, asked whether the comparison should show the range in place of 27.9% ("I think so unless there's any good reason not to.") | Done on 19 October ([the comparison](studies/climate_targets/comparison.md)). No reason against was found: the hot world and the lock are the GCM's own runs, and the deserts, a threshold on each cell's rain, are the figure the interpolation's form moves most; the per-kelvin desert rate is given as 0.9–1.8 points by the same forms |

## The ChatGPT work of 15 October, confirmed 17 October 2026

The author confirmed on 17 October that the decisions recorded in their ChatGPT work stand where work done here has
not superseded them. These three, from the discussion of 15 October, were each checked against the repository first.

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Architecture shows the Moon's gravity: its guards and circulation make room for the added spring in a step at a sixth of Earth's gravity, in forms distinct from Earth's. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("As long as they weren't superceded in time and importance by work we've done locally."), confirming the decisions recorded in the ChatGPT work of 15 October | Stands as a design rule; no later work here replaces it. The [low-gravity design note](../habitation/low_gravity/README.md) (17 October) works it out from the daily-life study's falls, jump and braking. Its guard bands, guard form, stairs, handholds, ramps and halls for moving are design choices that await the author's review |
| The low-gravity architecture of that discussion suits the semi-arid parks, gardens and their amenities better than a city; where it stands stays provisional while the climate and the ecology are worked out. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("As long as they weren't superceded in time and importance by work we've done locally."), confirming the decisions recorded in the ChatGPT work of 15 October | Provisional, as the author left it. It agrees with the parks rows of 11 and 12 October (megaparks and great gardens, built round the lower water and rich in dry-adapted planting) and with Jura Island's layout of 15 October, whose bay-facing slopes are one of the great gardens built to the dry belt's rules; the ChatGPT work set its scene on those lower slopes above the flooded Sinus Iridum. Jura's climate box found the island dry. Its rerun waits ([the row of 17 October](#the-gcms-longwave-by-height-17-october-2026)), and the target climate ([17 October](#the-target-climate-17-october-2026)) settles how dry the setting stays |
| The district wind study turns to denser layouts than its sparse pilot, whose towers stood 200 m wide with gaps of 645 m. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("As long as they weren't superceded in time and importance by work we've done locally."), confirming the decisions recorded in the ChatGPT work of 15 October | Stands as a direction for a later study; the work of 15 October is unrecovered. ChatGPT's follow-up tested gaps of 200, 100 and 50 m, podium and infill layouts and a diagonal wind, and its commits and source stayed in ChatGPT's sandbox. Two of its images survive on the research drive (integration-history-review-2026-10-17/originals/4-district-wind/), and the review's assessment (integration-history-review-2026-10-17/assessment.md, its finding F01) describes the method: empty domains as matched controls, the sweep of gaps, podium and infill layouts, neutral and stable air, and grid checks. Its reported wind speeds have no surviving source and stay out of the repository, and its forcing, the fog desert's winds near the ground, predates the corrected climate. The [plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order) names the study for later, and the author set its city the same day (next row) |
| The district wind study is wanted, and its denser layouts are studied for the summit metropolis, the largest megacity, round the central sky tower. | Author, 2026-10-17 ("Probably the largest mega city that gets to central sky tower."), asked whether the district wind study is wanted and for which city | Direction for a later study of the summit metropolis's districts, forced by the climate of the target the author chooses ([the target climate](#the-target-climate-17-october-2026)); the [plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order) names it (item 10) |

| The waters' trophic design: the open water of the lakes and seas is held nutrient-poor, with productive habitats where nutrients naturally gather, at the deltas, shelves and lake margins and in farms, under the seas' condition of high biodiversity ([the seas through time](#the-seas-through-time-13-october-2026)). Earth's most species-rich lakes, Baikal, Tanganyika and Malawi, are nutrient-poor, their diversity coming from habitat and age. | Author, 2026-10-19, asked whether nutrient-poor open water with productive habitats where nutrients naturally gather read their thoughts ("I believe so as long as "nutrient-poor open water" still allows rich biodiversity and I think we've had that converastion. Nutrient-poor does not mean low biodiversity.") | Set. Nutrient-poor waters hold the air's methane under the held pair at the methane share's centre and keep the deep lakes' floors oxic ([the resort at 301 K across the domains](studies/resort_301k/README.md)); the aquatic programme designs the habitats ([its plan](studies/aquatic_biogeochemistry/plan.md)) |
| Living things take on much of the waters' mineral and methane work, after much selective breeding: reef builders and other calcifiers take the calcium as calcite and, in alkaline basins, the magnesium, their carbonate also serving as material; shellfish lock minerals in their shells; and methanotrophs eat much of the methane. The row on engineered organisms stands ([life and people](#life-and-people)). | Author, 2026-10-19 ("It would take a lot of selective breeding, etc. but I think it would be a good option to use reef builders and methanotrophes to handle a lot of the issues. The reef builders for calcium and magnesium (and also for crafting material, etc.), also shell fish to lock in minerals, and methanotrophes to deal with a lot of the methane problem.") | Direction; the aquatic programme works it out ([its plan](studies/aquatic_biogeochemistry/plan.md)). Biology makes magnesium carbonate readily only in alkaline, magnesium-rich water, so the magnesium reefs belong in alkaline basins; phosphorus limits the shellfish; and in nutrient-poor water most of the methane left is the shallow floors' bubbles, which pass the water's methanotrophs, so the floors' make-up is the methane lever ([the waters' methane](studies/lake_methane/README.md)) |

|---|---|---|

| The exobase is checked on the states at hand before any push of the design case to 301 K with the cold cloud, and the design air's methane is carried into the upper air: its water at the two bounds of its oxidation for the exobase, and its hydrogen's escape through the homopause with cycle times. | Author, 2026-10-21 ("Important then.", of the hydrogen's escape, then "Proceed.") | Done on domain/atmosphere (c4469bd): the ground and the cold cloud leave the exobase as it was; methane's water at its bound warms the 0.3 Pa base 1.2–2.0 K, and behind the film alone the exobase stays at 147–200 K, 50–100 K under the 250 K target. The hydrogen escapes at 0.0085 kg/s (0.005–0.021 at methane's bounds), 3.3 times H2 and water alone: about 2,400 m³ of water a year, a billionth of the delivery, and the air's hydrogen in H2 and methane in about 3 million years. The same water at its bound would add 6.1 W/m² at the top of the column, about 3.8 K, so where methane is broken in the middle atmosphere is a climate question as well |
