A source of ultraviolet at the ground lights only the air near it, so it would act as a sink there with no reach into the middle atmosphere; a UV-B window through the shield would light the whole column, and the column's methane chemistry is built if one is opened. | Author, 2026-10-21 ("Reasonable and I don't think that surface UV if we add a source will impact things that far up, right?"), then ("Record it.") | Set (domain/climate 05b1384) |

## The CM1 boxes' rain and PlaSim's convection, 21 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Why the CM1 boxes rain far less than the GCM is found from a test or a literature result, or both, before anything more is run. | Author, 2026-10-21 ("There HAS to be a way to get an idea of why... SOMETHING either a test or a literature result or both...") | Done on domain/climate (13e7123). The Near-side Sea box's water and heat budgets, set against the GCM's own budget at the site, account for its 0.06 mm/day of rain against the GCM's 2.72; its water budget closes to 0.01 mm/day. Its nudging from 8 km supplied 89 of the 107 W/m² its radiation removed, in place of the deep convection that forms the GCM's rain at 18–72 km. Its forcing stopped at 16 km and left out the 35.5 W/m² the GCM's circulation takes from the air above, and the interpolation of the GCM's layers dried its column by 0.55 mm/day. CM1's simple boundary layer (`ipbl` 2; the same code in the cm1r22.0 release and NCAR's main branch) gave the air rho times the surface's heat, water and drag, 1.32–1.51 times. It runs in 51 of the 59 CM1 runs, every earlier ring and box among them, and biases them until a fixed rerun sizes it; it is a candidate for much of the rings' warm, humid air near the ground. The earlier land box rained 63 mm a lunar day against 180 in the GCM that forced it. Randall and Cripe (1999) and Derbyshire and others (2004) frame the forcing and the convection |
| The pair runs again with three changes: CM1's simple boundary layer per unit mass; the GCM's forcing through the whole column, each layer's total kept on CM1's levels; and the box's temperature and vapour held to the GCM's only above the top of the GCM's convection, the wind still nudged. | Author, 2026-10-21 ("Proceed."), to the three changes and their trials | Done on domain/climate (13e7123, f07ad8d). The build `moon_omp_rain_law_pbl` brought the boundary layer to 0.995–1.000 of the evaporation in the trials. The GCM resolves no tropopause, its temperature falling to its top level (166 K at 129 km), so the air is held from its first level above its convection's top, 93 km. The sea box now convects to about 70 km and rains 0.85 mm/day over its second lunar day, 31% of the GCM's. From noon to midnight it rains 34–58% of the GCM's rain. From midnight to noon it rains almost nothing: the GCM's circulation sinks over the site then, while PlaSim's Kuo scheme keeps raining the sea's evaporation, 2.9 mm/day after midnight. 61% of its falling water evaporates, gamma about 0.027 by PlaSim's pooled shares, provisional while its upper air still cools 0.1–0.2 K a day. The land box (domain/climate 30e7f83) rains 92% of the GCM's rain on the same cells and at the same hours, mostly in the afternoon and barely after midnight, because over land the ground's evaporation follows the Sun; the night-time gap is the sea's own. It also evaporates 61% of its falling water, which PlaSim's land does not reach even at gamma 0.04 (58.2%; about 0.046 extended), so no single constant fits both places. With the boundary layer fixed, the boxes' air at 2 m runs 0.23 K warmer than the GCM's over the sea and 1.15 K over land, against the September rings' 1.8–4.7 K ([plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order), item 5) |
| The climate stays on ExoPlaSim, and PlaSim's Kuo convection gets its replacement there. A simplified Betts–Miller scheme (Frierson 2007) is written in Fortran and patched into `rainmod.f90` behind PlaSim's own switch: `nprc` 1 keeps Kuo exactly, 2 selects the new scheme, at about Kuo's speed. Two lunar options come off by default, a reference lifted with some mixing of the surrounding air and an inhibition limit. The scheme is tested against a Python copy, on speed, on design-climate branches and against CM1 at both sites, with the energy and exobase checks. The design climate takes it at the author's call once those are in. | Author, 2026-10-21 ("The Kuo convection scheme is no longer considered reasonable for modern Numerical Weather Prediction (NWP) or climate modeling. Right? Why is PlaSim still using it?"), then ("I'm not going to move to ROCKE-3D! You know that! Can you implement a better scheme in Fotran that runs close to the same speed as the existing scheme and patch it in? Is a simplified Betts-Miller scheme likely good enough?"), then ("Lock that in on main so we don't lose it."), then ("Almost out of usage limit so not sure you'd get through it but you can try.") | Written and tested on domain/climate (2542b57, 30e7f83). The Fortran routine, compiled alone, matches its Python copy to 10⁻¹⁰ on five columns, and the patched module compiles with ExoPlaSim's own compiler and flags. A year of Kuo's scheme through the patched model matches the design branch's first year bit for bit, and the new scheme runs at Kuo's speed. Settled (row below) ([plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order), item 5) |
| The new scheme's settled GCM results come first: everything up to the CM1 boxes forced by the new GCM is done before them. | Author, 2026-10-22 ("Hmm... alright so clearly it's functioning differently at least. I want to see what the actual settled GCM results are with the new scheme first.") | Done on domain/climate (5578b55). Two 15-year branches from the design climate's year-19 restart at its sunlight settle within 0.1 K: 299.6 K at Frierson's reference humidity of 0.7 and 300.1 K at 0.6, against Kuo's 298.4 K. The ground warms 1.0–1.5 K, the air over land 1.5–2.3 K and the air poleward of 70° 2.0–2.7 K, with 6–12% more rain and 6–10% more water in the column. Over the Near-side Sea at 0.7 the rain after midnight halves (2.93 to 1.45 mm/day) and the morning's falls by two-thirds, toward CM1's pattern, while the nights still rain about eight times CM1's. Over land both settings sharpen the afternoon peak (15.1 and 12.7 mm/day against Kuo's 11.1 and CM1's 8.6). Against the corrected CM1 boxes, forced by Kuo's GCM, the warming near the ground has the right sign and runs larger. The 301 K longwave refit leaves 3.38 W/m² against the rule's 2.5, so the scheme's climate takes a refit of its own. On CM1's own columns no setting of the scheme matches CM1's timing: it would rain tens to hundreds of mm/day at every hour, because CM1's night columns hold 9.9 kg/m² of vapour above its reference without raining it. Mixing raises its rain until the parcel loses buoyancy, and an inhibition limit holds back the mornings and afternoons before the nights. The scheme's rate on CM1's air cannot predict the GCM's timing: over land it would convect on none of CM1's afternoons, while the GCM rains at CM1's hours. The sea box's third lunar day (days 59–88.5) has settled, its upper air changing by 0.5 K or less between sunrises, and rains 1.33 mm/day against 0.85 on the second: 0.00, 2.23, 2.97 and 0.14 mm/day by quarter, 67% of the new GCM's rain, with gamma still 0.027. The attempt that followed is the next row ([plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order), item 5) |

After midnight the sea box holds a 6.1 km boundary layer that reaches the free-convection level in 59% of its columns, 1,415 J/kg of CAPE for a parcel mixed over 0.5 km and 0.4 J/kg of inhibition, yet rains 0.14 mm/day with resolved updrafts of 1.0–1.25 m/s. The land box's nights are quiet for a physical reason: its boundary layer collapses to about 100 m under 136–157 J/kg of inhibition. The circulation's rate of building CAPE is negative through the sea's rainy evening and its quiet night alike, so a dynamic-CAPE trigger (Xie and Zhang 2000) cannot tell them apart, and a parcel diluted by mixing (as in Neale and others 2008) finds almost no CAPE on the GCM's raining columns. A CAPE threshold, built as an option off by default with its own optional dilution, quiets the sea's nights at about 100 J/kg in its first response on the GCM's columns (0.21 mm/day against CM1's 0.14) and brings the land's afternoon to CM1's (8.2 against 8.6), but takes the sea's afternoon (0.7 against 2.2) and the land's evening (0.04 against 1.5); only a branch would show how CAPE builds in a running model. The 6 km grid is as wide as the sea's night boundary layer is deep, so its eddies go unresolved (Petch and others 2002), and whether CM1's quiet sea nights are physical is open: a finer box decides it, then the threshold's branch if they are ([plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order), item 5) |

## The snow work's course, 24 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The snow work returns to the lunar question. The single-crystal growth model is frozen as it stands, its remaining Earth errors carried as stated uncertainty. Its check against Earth's laboratory crystals takes a rule sized to the data, each measured crystal at its measured age within the scatter between repeat crystals, on mass, axes and fall speed, in place of the 1,320 pointwise limits against fitted curves. The work then moves to the lunar deliverable: flake sizes as a range over the moisture supply and the number of crystals, the flakes' joining checked against Earth's snowflakes with measured crystals as input, and their survival in turbulence. A Claude agent carries it on domain/atmosphere. | Author, 2026-10-24 ("Also have a subagent take a quick lookat the 30 commits on terluna-atmosphere related to the snow work. Try to figure out why it's having so much trouble."), then ("Alright that was my fault. I'm still not confident it doesn't matter, but maybe I am trying too hard to make sure the model is consistent with Earth results at base level first."), then ("Alright have one subagent work on the climate work and the existing subagent lock in the suggestions and get to work on making progress.") | Done on domain/atmosphere (37c154b, 094d1cf, c54aede), with a first answer. High in the snow zone the flakes outgrow any span they can survive, so breakup sets their size: about 5 cm for ordinary flakes and 8–15 cm for the largest, scaled from Earth's 2 cm flakes and 4–6 cm storm aggregates by the load their junctions bear. Under the same turbulence a lunar flake bears the junction load of an Earth flake 2.2–2.6 times smaller, its weight dominating and turbulence adding 1–36%. The aggregation is McSnow's, rebuilt from its hash-checked source, with measured crystals and Connolly and others' (2012) sticking: its median melted diameters come to 0.87–1.16 of Gunn and Marshall (1958) and Sekhon and Srivastava (1970) at 0.3–9 mm/h, their coefficients taken from secondary statements because the primary pages refused access. Without breakup its Earth tail reaches 60 cm against the observed 4–6 cm, so a load analogy stands in for a breakup law. On the Moon at 3 mm/h the largest tenth of the flakes pass 10–14 cm within 0.5–2.5 km of the cloud top under every sticking law tried, three crystal numbers and the Earth error band. Under the data-sized rule the frozen calibration passes on mass and axes (5 of 93 outside, about 4 by chance), and the component every lunar run uses fails (83 of 332; its fall speed mostly 1.1–2.0 times too fast). Carried through the lunar column, the component's Earth errors move water-limited crystal sizes 1.4–1.6-fold, against 9.3-fold for a hundredfold change in the number of crystals. The review of the 30 commits (16cde54–800a8c9) had found the lunar sizes moving 50- to 100-fold with the moisture supply, the number of crystals and the solver, and a rule of 1,320 limits tighter than the laboratory's repeat crystals, which differ in mass by up to 2.2–2.4 times. Next, at the author's word: a breakup law, growth and aggregation together, a storm-coupled water supply and the storms' optics |
| The snow's next steps: a breakup law with Earth support in place of the load analogy, growth and aggregation together through a storm's water supply, and whether light snow reaches the size limit before it melts. | Author, 2026-10-25 ("Alright that doesn't look too hard drive heavy so let's get some work done then.") | Done on domain/atmosphere (c8e6a38, bff6981, 6e91689). No law for whole snowflakes breaking has Earth support, and collisions in free fall (0.5 m/s or less, about 10⁻⁸ J) fall below every measured breakage, so the load analogy stays as the cap with its four untested premises recorded. Grown and aggregated together without breakup, and converged under four times the superparticles, flakes reach the freezing level at a 90th percentile of 4.2–4.5 cm in light snow from a −15 °C top, 9.7–12.7 cm in light snow forming near −30 °C (its tail still sensitive to resolution) and 10.5–10.9 cm, with a 99th percentile of 14–19 cm, in heavy snow with a hundredth of the crystals. Heavy snow at the reference crystal number and saturated columns run away to metres and are not results. As crystals gather into flakes they take up less vapour; at 3 mm/h only 40% of the supply arrived as snow. Every lunar McSnow run of 24–25 October diffused vapour at 0.651 of its formula, because our patch set the surface-pressure constant, which the formula also uses as its reference, to the freezing level's pressure. Fixed for the lunar build only (6e91689), single reruns move light snow by under 5% and bring heavier snow 11–26% larger with 24–37% more vapour taken up; the README carries the corrected values and lists the remaining reruns for the author |
| How far the large flakes fall below the freezing level before fully melting, and their water before fully evaporating, with sublimation carried all the way down, and where the snow's water and latent heat go. | Author, 2026-10-25 ("Independently, have the snow work estimate how far down the unusually large snow can reach before fully melting and then before fully evaporating. Not only does it impact how far down we get winter like weather, it can impact water and heat transfer. We might end up with rain further down where we would have only had snow further up."), then ("I would assume it already will properly taken into account but just in case make sure the snow continues to take into account sublimation in addition to taking into account melting the rest of the way down.") | Done on domain/atmosphere (e6ca655, corrected in 6e91689). No snow reaches any ground: below a freezing level at 22–26 km the flakes sublimate, then melt, and fall the last 15–25 km as rain. Melting begins 0.3–0.55 km down, where the ice-bulb temperature passes 0 °C in air 93–97% humid; 2 cm flakes finish about 1.6 km down and 10–20 cm flakes 3.7–5.2 km down, three to four times the 20 October small-flake depths. Over the sea dry snow falls above 21.75 km and wet snow and sleet reach 18.3–19.9 km, the bands standing about 3.6 km higher over land. The large flakes' 9–15 mm drops, below the lunar stability limit of 16.6 mm, bring 81–93% of their water down, while drops from flakes up to 2 cm evaporate before any ground. Light snow delivers 11–13% of its water to the ground and snow of flakes near 10 cm 55–60%, 61–65% with the corrected diffusivity, against CM1's 39–40% for its storms. Sublimation takes 2–8% of the snow over land and 10–23% over the sea, where its heat equals the melting's in heavy snow and doubles it in light. Per mm/h of snowfall, melting draws 71–90 W/m² in a layer 2–3.5 km deep and evaporation 230–550 W/m² over the whole fall. The Earth check puts melting's onset within 3–5% of Matsuo and Sasyo's depths and within 0.17 K of Heymsfield's fits; Mitra (1990) was unreadable, and the model follows Frick and others (2013). Wet flakes sticking together below the freezing level are not modelled |
| The rain code takes Massman's measured vapour diffusivity in place of its Chapman–Enskog value, 17.6% low; products the current snow answer rests on are rebuilt, and the older ones keep the pre-fix code as a byte-pinned baseline. | Author, 2026-10-25 ("Do so if you have not done so already.") | Done on domain/atmosphere (e14e587). Settle rebuilt the rain products and the 20 October snow products. For the hot world, the resort and the lock, the smallest drop reaching the ground grows from 2.43, 2.03 and 1.58 mm to 2.47, 2.07 and 1.62 mm, and the water reaching the ground falls from 1.7, 5.3 and 16.5% to 1.5, 4.7 and 15.0%. The 33 earlier snow products keep the pre-fix code as a byte-pinned baseline (atmosphere/rain/baseline/drops_2026-10-24.py, in research/provenance.json), each noted. McSnow's own formula stands 3.4–4.6% below Massman's, recorded as uncertainty |
| The snow reruns on the corrected lunar McSnow build wait while the climate work uses the compute, and they come first when the snow work resumes. | Author, 2026-10-26 ("Hold off on snow reruns for now because we want this compute for this, but make sure they're noted as needed when we start snow work again.") | Done on domain/atmosphere (aceddfa, 29 October). The eighteen lunar runs still carrying the old diffusivity factor ran again on the corrected build, and the growth-and-aggregation and melting products were rebuilt from them. Light snow at the reference crystal flux arrives with a 90th percentile of 4.1–4.9 cm and heavy snow with a hundredth of the crystals with 12.6–12.7 cm, both converged, while light snow forming near −30 °C parts by 21% at the median between its two superparticle numbers; the heavier snow melts 0.2–0.6 km deeper and sends 56–65% of its water to the surface, light snow 6–15%. The single-crystal runs, the Earth error band and the size range never carried the factor |

## The design run, 31 October 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The design run starts at once under a watch that logs each year and pauses it if, over the latest four years, the land's ground falls faster than 0.08 K a year while its clouds' shortwave effect strengthens by more than 0.15 W/m² a year, the sunlight run's drift, or if from its year 3 the ground lies more than 0.75 K from 301.25 K; meanwhile the drift's mechanism is traced from the sunlight run's saved budgets. | Author, 2026-10-31 ("If we start the run now, can we keep eye on things to see whether the drift begins to be explained as it runs, and would there likely be good early stopping signal if the land drift causes further problems?"), then chose the watch when offered it ("Start with the watch") | Done (`watch_design.py` on the research drive, session-tools/snow_sublimation). The land rule never fired in either design run, and the sunlight run's drift does not show at the design state; the guard paused the first run, at 0.949, 0.85 K above the target (next row). Over the sunlight run's years 1–5 the imbalance falls 0.99 W/m², almost all of it in the clouds: the land's reflect 0.74 W/m² more over the land and the sea's trap 1.31 W/m² less longwave over the sea, 0.46 and 0.50 W/m² of the global mean; what drives the two cloud changes is not traced (climate/gcm/README.md, 31 October) |
| When the watch pauses the design run with its ground off the target, the run starts again at once, without a further answer, at the corrected sunlight S** = S (1 − N_t / ASR), N_t the mean imbalance over years 1 to the pause plus λ (their mean ground − 301.25 K) and λ = ASR k at 0.73–0.96% of the sunlight a kelvin; the fresh fifteen-year run starts from the bridge's year 5, after the machine's half-hour rest, under the same watch. | Author, 2026-10-31, answering whether a paused run may restart at the corrected sunlight without waiting ("Yes, restart by the rule") | Applied. The run at 0.949 warmed to 302.10 K by its year 4, its refit having brought a cloud longwave effect 4.75 W/m² larger in its first year than the fixed-state reading carried, and its years 1–4 gave S** = 0.934 (0.9336–0.9347); the corrected run is `A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s934` (domain/climate a5de646) |
| The cold cloud's factors stay as computed with the Chapman–Enskog vapour diffusivity, the difference Massman's measured fit makes stated as a limit of the design years, and the corrected run resumes; at the reconciliation the factors are redone with Massman's fit as a sensitivity on the design years. | Author, 2026-11-01, answering how the run should proceed after the recomputation crossed its threshold ("Carry it as a limit, resume") | Applied (domain/climate a5de646). With Massman's diffusivity alone changed the second layer's water factor falls 7.7%, from 36.6 to 33.8, inside its stated range of 12.8–52.8; the other factors move by under 1.6%, and the factors' first-order effect at the ground moves by +0.47 to +0.61 K. The run, stopped during its year 5, just after year 4 was written, by the thresholds set before the recomputation, resumed from its folder. At the reconciliation the factors were recomputed on the design years ([the record](integration/four-branches.md#the-cold-clouds-factors-on-the-design-years)): there the diffusivity moves them by under 2.5% and the change of climate moves the second layer's water 11.5%, and taken together, the recomputed factors would warm the design climate by 0.33 W/m² at first order, 0.21–0.27 K at the ground. Whether the design case takes them is open |
| The design climate is the design run's years 10–19, `A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s934` at sunlight 0.934: 300.84 K at the ground, 0.41 K below the 301.25 K target, with years 15–19 at 300.76 K as their settled end. It replaces the 20 October design years ([above](#the-design-case-and-the-models-earth-limits-1920-october-2026)). | Author, 2026-11-01 ("10 - 19."), of the proposal to take years 10–19 | Set (domain/climate a5de646; climate/gcm/README.md, The design run). The run cooled about 0.25 K in its years 11–16 while the sea's clouds reflected 1.25 W/m² more by year 15, about 0.65 more by years 17–19, then held, so over years 10–19 the ground's trend, −0.31 K a decade, fails the settling criterion while the last five years' imbalance, −0.14 W/m², passes. The longwave refit holds within 1.73 W/m² on years 5, 9, 14 and 19, and the exobase stays inside the middle atmosphere's bracket, the top level holding 0.036–0.039 ppmv of vapour. Against the 20 October design years the cloud cover falls from 0.50 to 0.37 while the clouds' net effect turns from +11.1 to −2.3 W/m², the rain falls 26% to 2.00 mm/day, and the drylands grow from 43% to 58% of the land, the semi-arid land from 12% to 25%, while the hyper-arid land falls from 8% to 4%. The dimming against the 0.970 design sunlight falls from about 7.5% to 3.7%. The design climate's readers wait for the models to agree (next row) |
| Biome maps, local climates, the Jura box and the other studies that read the design climate wait until the GCM and CM1 agree closely enough that the remaining gap is unlikely to move them much. With a significant gap after the design run, the design study does only its minimum, the branches are integrated and aligned, and the work then returns to the gap. | Author, 2026-11-01 ("I don't want to start mapping out local climates only to have things change dramatically again. If there's still a significant gap then we'll do the minimum we need to with the current design study, spend the needed time doing branch integration and alignment, and then when that is done we'd have to get back to resolving that gap. If the gap is now unlikely to have a significant impact on the need to update a biome map, Jura and other studies, etc. then we can start working on those other things. So let's see which it's going to have to be.") | The gap is significant. Over the Near-side Sea site the design years rain 2.52 mm/day in the tenth of the lunar day before midnight, where CM1's boxes rained 0.1–0.2 mm/day from 135° to midnight on every grid and forcing tried, though those boxes were forced by earlier runs; CM1's upper air ran 8–10 K colder than the GCM's; and between the old and new design years the rain changed by more than half on 54% of the land. The design study's records are done (domain/climate a5de646) and the branches merged with main on the integration branch ([the record](integration/four-branches.md)); next, the gap from CM1's sea and land boxes forced by the design years ([plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order), item 5). On 8 November the local climates, the Jura study and the other readers of the design climate took the gap as a range instead ([below](#the-corrected-design-climate-the-gap-carried-as-a-range-and-the-boxes-held-8-november-2026)) |
| Storm water running off dry ground into channels and hollows, which PlaSim's soil cannot shed below its capacity, is planned soon after the reconciliation. | Author, 2026-11-01 ("These regions would be quite different from the typical  dry savanna regions which dry up  completely. There should be considerable collected water that remains, no?"), then ("Something to plan in soon after the merge is complete.") | Planned after the reconciliation ([plan](plan.md#next-work-in-order), item 5). PlaSim's soil is a 0.5 m bucket that runs off only when full, so its drylands carry no runoff, ponds or groundwater. On the design years the semi-arid land, a quarter of the land, receives 243 mm a year, 83% of it by day; its soil water holds at 14.5–15.6 mm through the two-week night, when evaporation falls to 0.14–0.16 mm/day, and dries to about 9.5 mm in the sunny morning before the storms. A pool there would lose about 60 mm per lunar day at Priestley–Taylor's demand, mostly by day, so pools deeper than about 6 cm and fed by runoff could last from one storm period to the next, a hand estimate from Priestley–Taylor's demand that no model run has tested |

## The gap test's rules and the storm water, 1 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The GCM and CM1 agree closely enough for the biome maps, local climates and the Jura box when the rain agrees in amount and in timing and the physics behind the agreement is justified. At each box the lunar-day rain lies within 10% of the GCM's, and at the land boxes so does the aridity, the rain over a Priestley–Taylor demand built from the box's own radiation; at most 15% of the rain falls in a different quarter of the lunar day from the GCM's, and where the GCM rains in the late evening or after midnight CM1 rains two thirds to one and a half times as much; the evaporation, the low and upper air, the column's vapour, the share of falling rain evaporated and the radiation agree as the rules set them. Every change made to either model to close the gap is explained physically, by a test or the literature, on evidence that does not rest on the comparison, before it is adopted. | Author, 2026-11-01 ("10%.  And we have to have enough reason to believe that the physics that closed the gap to 10% is justified. Also, and I'll let you decide the percentage here, we need to try to align rain TIMING as well a bit more. Rain timing correctness and rain amount should both be constrained."), to the proposal of 10% on the land boxes' aridity | Set before the first run (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/rules.txt). A uniform rain bias of 10% moves 8–10% of the land across an aridity class boundary and one of 20% moves 17–21% (session-tools/design_run/class_sensitivity.json). The earlier sea boxes held their lunar-day rain to 0.7–2.8% between days 3 and 4 and moved 2–14% of it between quarters from one day to the next, so two read days resolve both rules; where a box's days differ by more, a fifth lunar day runs. The sea box started on 1 November. |
| The water-closure check counts the vapour and condensate CM1 creates where its numerics reset negative amounts to zero, a known term its statistics record, and still asks the rest to close within 0.1 mm/day; the clipped water is reported beside the rain, and the rain's verdicts must hold with the box's rain lowered by the whole of it. | Author, 2026-11-01, of how the closure should treat the clipped vapour before the sea box's read days ("Count it (Recommended)") | Set before any read day (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/rules.txt, amendment). The sea box's second spin-up day left 0.113 mm/day unaccounted, of which CM1's recorded clipping was 0.066 (0.031 in the earlier fifteen-level sea boxes, 0.059 in the earlier land box); the rest, 0.047, closes. The clipping comes from the day-night table's drying above 40 km, where the box's vapour is spent while the microphysics sublimates ice back into it (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/clip_trace_box_near_side_sea_design_years.json); limiting the table's drying to the vapour present is the physical correction for a later setup. |
| A third box joins the sea and land boxes: a flat site box over the semi-arid inland block of the near side's south-east, centred at 44.3° S, 56.25° E over six GCM land cells, on the land box's build and defaults with its land at the GCM's own wetness there. It tests the GCM's afternoon storms on dry ground, which holds 58% of the land where the other two boxes stand on sea and wet ground, and gives the storm-water study its storms through ten-minute windows rerun from its restarts. It goes on domain/climate with the other two. | Author, 2026-11-01, asking whether Jura would serve ("Would the Jura box be a good one to add then or is that the wrong region?"), then of the semi-arid site ("Very wll, we'll go with this box as the added one."), and of its branch ("Indeed.") | Set up (domain/climate 89747cb): the site takes the six cells, its land at the GCM's wetness there, 0.08, and its smoke test passed. The GCM rains 0.609 mm/day there, with an aridity of 0.306 and 94% of its rain from noon to sunset. The six cells rain 156–280 mm a year against a demand of 670–790, 95–98% of it by day and about three quarters from storms, close to the semi-arid land as a whole in rain, demand and storms (243 mm against 714, two thirds from storms), with more of its rain by day than the class's 83%. Jura is the wrong region for these storms: its two GCM cells are dry sub-humid, with 94–96% of their rain falling after sunset, the evening rain the sea box tests, and the first Jura box made no rain over the island (session-tools/design_run/jura_dryland_cells.json). |
| The storm-water study works out the runoff, the hollows' water and the recharge on several soils and surfaces side by side, a sandy loam, a loam, a sealed loam and a biological crust, under various conditions. When the drylands' soils are chosen, they vary across the drylands to diversify their biomes, each a soil that can be sustained under its storms and water. | Author, 2026-11-01 ("Since we don't need it yet, we'll see what it is under various conditions. The reasonable choice is to try to vary the soil types to diversify biomes while ensuring the soils developed can be sustained.") | Direction for the study; the choice waits for its results. For each surface the study reports what sustains or wears it: its loss to the storms' runoff against its formation, and the salts left where pools dry. |
| The storm-water work has its own domain branch, domain/hydrosphere, made from main in a sibling worktree. | Author, 2026-11-01, of a branch for the study ("Hmm... domain/hydrosphere") | Created on 1 November. The study starts in research/studies/storm_water/, the lane its plan proposed, with the change to geography's drainage that lets it take a runoff field. Its first phase, the runoff from dry ground on the four surfaces, is committed there (65df2d0). |
| The drift check is judged on the last lunar day read, so a fifth lunar day, where one runs, decides a box's drift; R6's exception reads as the plan words it. A wind-check miss reads as a physical difference noted beside the reading, and the box still counts as a test, where the box's own boundary-layer and resolved transport carry the shortfall while the relaxation acts at its design rate and the wind budget closes. | Author, 2026-11-01, after the sea box's days 3 and 4 were read, of the drift check ("Last read day (Recommended)") and of the night wind ("Physics, noted (Recommended)") | Set in rules.txt's second amendment (19:50) before the land and dry boxes' days were read (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/rules.txt). The sea box's wind after midnight reads so (night_wind.json there), and its fifth lunar day settled its drift: over that day the column's vapour changed by −2.9 kg/m² and the 16–60 km band by −0.17 K. The land and dry boxes miss at 1 km in the morning (0.81–0.86 of the target); their wind budgets have not been diagnosed. |
| The site boxes carry the GCM's own weather: their forcing follows the design years' own sequence at the site, and each box lunar day is read against the GCM's same lunar day, under rules fixed before they run. The mean-forced boxes are read as they stand. | Author, 2026-11-01, of the sea box's evaporation gap ("Carry the weather"), where the proposal was to read the evaporation like for like | On the same air CM1's surface transfer evaporates 11% more than PlaSim's. The mean-forced sea box evaporates 2.96 mm/day against the GCM's 3.61. 0.74 mm/day of the GCM's evaporation rests on weather the mean forcing lacks: 0.46 within its 3-day means and 0.28 between them (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/findings_sea.md). The forcing was built and smoke-tested (domain/climate af42e92). Carried at the sea box, the weather narrowed the evaporation's gap to 12% and left the rain's where it was (2 November, below). |
| PlaSim's convection gains a diluted (entraining) parcel, designed and tested offline on both models' columns first, its entrainment rate taken from evidence apart from the comparison: CM1's sampled lunar plumes and the plume literature. A GCM run follows only at the author's word. | Author, 2026-11-01 ("Yes, offline first (Recommended)") | Designed and tested offline (domain/climate a16582f; session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/plan_dilution.md) at 2.0×10⁻⁴ m⁻¹ (range 1.0–3.3×10⁻⁴). The diluted reference lies within 1.2 K of CM1's air at 20–60 km. The closure and the evening timing fail, traced to the scheme's two-hour relaxation and 0.7 reference humidity, both Earth values, and to a trigger that reads buoyancy alone. |

## Dance and the movement arts, 1 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|

## The weather-carrying boxes, the convection and the storm water's corrections, 2–3 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The weather-carrying boxes follow a rerun of the design run's years 15 and 16 from its own year-14 restart, writing its means every 5.5 hours, so they carry the weather within the 3-day means as well as between them. Each box runs a transition lunar day and six read lunar days. A rule holds when its paired difference lies inside its tolerance by two standard errors of the read days' differences, misses when it lies outside by two, and is unresolved otherwise; a rain amount or timing still unresolved after six read days runs six more. The dry box, whose 10% of rain six paired days cannot resolve, runs twelve read days and is judged on them. The sea and land chains keep each lunar day's last restart and the newest two; the dry box keeps every restart. Only the sea box runs now. The land and dry boxes follow once PlaSim's convection has changed, since they would be rerun on the new climate. | Author, 2026-11-02, of the run plan's questions ("Approve, rerun first (Recommended)", "Two standard errors (Recommended)", "12 days, then judge (Recommended)", "Dry all, others at ends (Recommended)"), then "Let already provided answers stand.", and of which boxes run, "Just the sea box." | Rules fixed in session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/rules_weather.txt at 04:00 before any weather-carrying box ran, with their reader read_weather.py; cases on domain/climate (100c754). The rerun's year-end restarts equal the design run's byte for byte, and 75 of its 82 fields rebuild the design years' 3-day records (interval means within 1e-7, snapshots exactly). The other seven, the fields PlaSim forms in its spectral accumulators (surface and sea-level pressure, temperature, the winds, the vertical velocity and the humidity), miss by 2e-5 to 6e-2 of their largest values, since their 3-day means are not the means of the shorter ones; by the rule the rerun is a second realization, and the box is read against its own records, the ones its forcing follows. The year's conversion ran in lunar-day pieces (domain/climate 2d93e2e) after the whole-year conversion held 11.6 GB. The sea chain ran on 2–3 November, one case at a time, on four threads and from the ninth case on three. Lunar day 1's transition test missed (the box's column vapour fell 12.2 kg/m² further behind the GCM's, against 5), so day 2 was a second transition and days 3–8 were read; the rain was unresolved there, and six more lunar days ran. On the twelve read days (weather_box_near_side_sea_design_years.json there; the inflow reckoned against the nudging window CM1 holds, domain/climate 4f63c43) CM1's rain follows the GCM's from one lunar day to the next (r 0.91) and falls 17.5% short, 1.078 against 1.305 mm/day with a standard error of 0.061: unresolved by the rule's margin, and a miss with CM1's clipped water, 0.188 mm/day, taken off its rain. 22% of the rain falls in a different quarter of the lunar day (the mean-forced box 41%) and the late evening and the hours after midnight get about a third of the GCM's rain, both misses; the evaporation runs 12% short (the mean-forced box 18%), unresolved; the low air's vapour runs 0.67 g/kg short, the column's vapour 7%, the air at 40 and 60 km 8.5 and 7.3 K colder, and CM1 evaporates 0.69 of its falling rain against the GCM's 0.51, all misses; the radiation holds. The water budget misses on day 8 alone (−0.110 mm/day against 0.1), and the wind after midnight at 1 km, where its budget leaves 10.9% of the relaxation unexplained against 10% (night_wind_weather.json there), so the box reads as observations only; its drift settled by day 14. The weather the mean forcing lacked leaves the rain's shortfall as it was. |
| Before any GCM run of the diluted scheme, its relaxation time and reference humidity are set from lunar evidence, and a trigger tied to the large-scale ascent is designed and tested offline. Its form and constants rest on physics and on CM1 boxes outside the gap test, and are fixed before they meet the gap boxes' columns. | Author, 2026-11-02 ("Yes, but also explain the trigger more.") | In progress (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/notes_convection.md). The reference humidity comes from Romps's (2014) bulk-plume theory, with CM1's measured detrainment and the lunar water-vapour lapse rate: about 0.83 (0.80–0.88). It reproduces CM1's own humidity within about 0.05 wherever its plumes detrain. The relaxation time rests on CM1's removal of instability, 11–27 h, about five turnover times. CM1's deep convection is mostly elevated, rooted at a median of 8–26 km, above the boundary layer, which a parcel from PlaSim's lowest level cannot represent. The mechanism runs below give the trigger its evidence: the evening storms need the forcing's lifting while it acts. |
| Before the depression floors are chosen, the pools' evaporation is corrected on its physics and the recharge is checked against Earth's dryland measurements of like soils and cover. | Author, 2026-11-02 ("That's a definite yes. We can work on that while we wait for the current work to finish.") | Phase 2c on domain/hydrosphere (08062f6). The pools evaporate by COARE 3.0 at lunar gravity on the drylands' gusty wind, with a free-convection floor; the night check passes (0.88 of the demand by day, 5.6 mm by night) and Lake Mead's fails (0.61, and 0.67 on its half-hourly record, a shortfall in the lake's own transfer coefficient). The floors carry nearly all the recharge and pass 4–14 times what playas pass per unit of floor, the designed seal's consequence; the slopes pass none, as at Walnut Gulch. The three first-run biases are corrected and no leverage verdict moves. The floors wait for the biome work (3 November, below); the water table's 1% floor and stage (b) are the author's to decide. |
| Before the trigger's form is chosen among two routes with memory, a prognostic closure and a multi-source parcel, two CM1 runs on a design-set sea box show whether the large-scale forcing's daily cycle times the evening storms and whether the storms still come once the forcing stops. They run in the evening, after the sea box's weather chain and a rest, since no outcome of theirs bears on the chain. | Author, 2026-11-02 ("If the results don't impact anything that runs at 11, then I'd say just do it in the evening after a rest.") | Rules fixed in session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/rules_mechanism.txt at 07:35 before either run. Cases and the forcing change on domain/climate (2505b3d), on box_near_side_sea_l15_inflow_day3's corrected build: the forcing's cycle held at its lunar-day mean from just after sunrise, and the forcing off from its peak. Smoke-tested 07:39–07:47. The first ran 19:05–20:00 on four threads, the second 20:31–21:09 on three (a departure from the rules' four that changes only CM1's rounding). Read at 21:09 (mechanism.json there), both verdicts beyond twice the noise. With the cycle held at its mean the forcing's cycle times the evening storms: the sunset rain falls to nothing against the control's 1.70 mm/day, the run rains 0.05 against 0.66 mm/day, nothing from sunrise to the late evening, and no deep updraughts form in the afternoon or at sunset. With the forcing off the storms need the forcing's last five days: the sunset rain falls to 0.18 against 1.70 and the day's running mean below half the control's 0.5 days after the switch, while the rain after midnight rises by 3.9 mm/day to 4.7, 2.3 against 0.90 mm/day over the run, from updraughts rooted at a median 16.5 km. One realization of each run, on the October fifteen-level GCM's forcing at the site. |
| The drylands' soils, and with them the hollows' floors, are chosen as the biome design is made, after a follow-up study, run with the biome and mapping work when it starts, of which soil types are easier or harder to maintain where. Until then the storm-water study's surfaces and floor designs stand as conditions worked out side by side. | Author, 2026-11-03, of the proposal to let the floors vary with the biome soils ("I'm pretty sure I said the plan was to study the various conditions and then figure out which soils are easier to maintian and harder to maintain and select which soil types for the biome design as we choose. So we will still need to do a follow up study as we do more biome and mapping work whenever that starts, to see what soil types make sense to work towards maintaining where.") | Direction for the biome and mapping work, which waits until the models agree (1 November); it keeps the soil row of 1 November. The storm-water study's four surfaces and three floor designs are its inputs (domain/hydrosphere 08062f6). The water table's 1% floor stays as the register has it meanwhile. |

## Dance: the salto, render sizes and the costume, 2–3 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The conlang's cultures enter the dance at most as a vague influence, each taken on its own merits; movement sources keep being chosen by mechanics, anatomy and expressive quality, and no style follows the conlang's culture. | Author, 2026-11-02, answering [humanities/dance/questions.md](../humanities/dance/questions.md) question 8 ("At most merit, if that. Vague influence only.") | Stands. The costume brief takes the cultures only as vague notes judged on their own merits ([humanities/dance/garment/costume.md](../humanities/dance/garment/costume.md)) |
| The hanging salto shows the lunar difference well and impresses even in previsualisation renders, but it is not the material of the main flowing dance. | Author, 2026-11-02, of the round's renders ("The hanging salto, even with rendering weakness is impressive. I will say that.  It's not what I had in mind for the main flowing dance but it sure shows off the lunar difference well.") | Recorded. Its variants were studied the same morning, for understanding only (dance-runs/round_2026-11-02/experiments/salto_variants on the research drive): the engine's Euler step in flight gains about 0.4 m/s of vertical speed, so the stored salto's arm sweep and 755 N peak were integration artefacts; with RK4 in flight and the late push the figure needs about 12 lunar body weights at its peak. Changing the engine's flight integration waited for the author, who chose Runge-Kutta on flights on 7 November (section "Sport from the body, 7 November 2026") |
| Dance videos render at 480p until a complete video is near, with a few frames at higher resolution for checks; compute is kept for other projects and for a high-quality final draft. | Author, 2026-11-02 ("I don't need 1080p. Done well it can look good at 480p or even 360p. No?"; then "Until we get closer to a complete video we want to generate, let's keep it at 480p except for a few frames at higher resolution for checks. Save compute for other projects and when we want to render a high quality final draft") | Stands. The renderer's default preset is `draft`, 854x480; check stills render at 720p or 1080p ([visualization/dance/README.md](../visualization/dance/README.md)). Since 3 November renders run on the NVIDIA GPU, which renders a 1080p still in seconds; the integrated GPU used before returned the black and striped frames |
| The feet move as realistically as the work allows (their movement, not the toes' detail), unless the dress covers them most of the time. | Author, 2026-11-02 ("Feet realism a bit too. Not toes or anything I mean realism in foot movement as best as we can, unless the dress will be covering it most of the time.") | Planned: pointed feet in flight, rolling through the foot in take-offs and landings, turns on demi-pointe, and the stored motions' floor sink and ankle overruns corrected ([humanities/dance/plan.md](../humanities/dance/plan.md)) |
| The first costume is elegant: a layered formal gown with layered long flowing sleeves, accepting the compute that finer and more complex meshes cost and the fixes they need; designs are refined from about half a dozen detailed stills compared side by side, and colour is designed later. | Author, 2026-11-02 ("I was thinking something more elegant. Though I know it'll result in more compute because of a more complicated mesh."; then "I really do want layered formal gown and layered long slowing sleeves, or not sure if I mean layered there or pleated.. Maybe I mean petals. I'm not 100% sure what you mean there. I need to work on my design vocabulary. So even if it means fixing things and dedicating some more compute, I want to see what we can get. Hmm does color come in at this point or should we design color for the dress later? I guess we can do a half dozen or so fairly detailed still renders and I can compare and we can refine designs.") | Three design rounds on 2 November, recorded in [humanities/dance/garment/costume.md](../humanities/dance/garment/costume.md): of a layered gown, three sheer layers graded in length and a petal gown, the graded layers, which read as tiers, were preferred; colours stay provisional |
| The costume reads as one outfit, and its sleeves are more elaborate than plain long sleeves, echoing the skirt's tiers rather than belling; a Philippine terno-inspired element is worth seeing. | Author, 2026-11-02, of the first round's stills ("Closer to one of this one, though there is still nothing cohesive that makes it an outfit, and the arms are still just simple long sleeves. They should be a bit more elaborate too. But I know this is more a functional investigation. Still..."); then of the proposals ("Not sure I'm a fan of the bell option and I think we should echo the skirt's tiers.  I'm not sure I'd like it but seeing it with the Philippine terno inspired element might be interesting. Your suggestions may be close enough to what I'm thinking.") | Done in the second round: a matching bodice with a sheer yoke, a satin sash and tiered sleeves; the terno's standing caps were built and held back, since a cap standing above the shoulder swings into the head when the arm rises, as this phrase's arms do |
| True butterfly sleeves wait for a dance with the arms kept low; the sleeve ends carry the elaboration instead, more elegant and with at most a minor flare. | Author, 2026-11-02, of the second round ("Save true butterfly sleeves for a dance with the arms kept low. We won't really worry about that right now. Instead the ends of the sleeps themselves should be more elegant. At most a MINOR flare.") | Done in the third round: three sheer tiers at each wrist graded in length like the skirt, each widening by a fifth toward a satin-bound hem, with level and slanted hems compared |
| The sleeve tiers keep level hems; the skirt's tiers take gold-bound hems like the sleeves'; the fabric model is corrected so that gathered cloth folds; the bodice is worked on next. | Author, 2026-11-03, choosing among the third round's options ("As for decisions... level hems I think." and, of gold edges on the skirt tiers to tie them to the sleeves, "Yes. And yes fix the gathers in the fabric model and then let's work on the bodice.") | Done on 3 November: level hems; the solver's stretch meets compression along warp and weft with 1% of its tensile stiffness, under which the fourth round's sleeve tiers stretch 5-7% at the 99th percentile (8-46% in the third), and the garment study's trials, rerun on it, moved by small amounts ([humanities/dance/README.md](../humanities/dance/README.md)); the skirt's tiers bound in gold. The bodice became a shell over the torso, in three styles and three necklines ([humanities/dance/garment/costume.md](../humanities/dance/garment/costume.md)); how it meets the skirt follows below |
| The longest sleeve tier keeps its 1.40 m, though it trails on the floor while the arms are low. | Author, 2026-11-03, of the fourth round's open question whether to keep or shorten it ("Keep the longest sleeve tier.") | Kept. ## The soils and the land's store, the convection's redesigns, the first biome map and the Moon's turn, 3–4 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The dry box's fifth lunar day, which its rules called, runs while the machine is free, as a third dry lunar day for the trigger's offline test as well as for the box's drift. | Author, 2026-11-03 ("The computer is largely free now so if there's nothing blocking it and it's useful to test the trigger we might as well run the dry box's  fifth day now.") | Set up and trial-tested (the restart, all 37 wind windows, the table, the rain law, the inflow's rates and every field the readings use), ran on four threads 07:57–09:08, resumed from its day-135 restart after the 08:39 logout cut it off (CM1 rewrites outputs and statistics by number, so the cut segment left nothing behind). Read by rules.txt on days 3–5 (rules_box_se_dry_design_years.json; days 3–4 kept beside it): the drift settled (M3 holds on day 5); the rain misses at 0.513 against 0.609 mm/day (16% short), the aridity at 0.273 against 0.306 (10.5% short), the timing at 24% displaced by CM1's morning rain, the evaporation 24% over and the air at 40 and 60 km 7.5–7.7 K colder; the column's vapour now holds (292.0 against 291.6 kg/m²); M2 misses at 1 km in the morning, its wind budget not yet diagnosed. |
| CM1's created water is traced to where it goes. Future readings report it as the forcing's unapplied drying beside the rain and read the rain checks with it removed. A change to the forcing, drying in proportion to CM1's own vapour or a limit with its own record, is designed only if the investigations continue to find a plausible closure of the gap. | Author, 2026-11-03, of the trace and a fix ("CM1's created water, I agree. Trace where the water goes and fix it for future runs."), then, after the trace, of the limit ("Option (a) seems to have no real benefit at this point if what you're saying is true, yes? Or I guess to give it benefit we would also have to make other modifications to the software such as more graduation in layers, etc.?") and of drying in proportion to CM1's vapour ("Let's do that only if current investigations continue to find plausible closure in which case we would move from option b to option a or a new informed derivative of it.") | Traced (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/created_water.md; clip_trace.py, clip_fate.py). On the weather-carrying sea box all of it is vapour, made above 40 km where the forcing's table dries air that has no vapour left; at 60–100 km the table's drying would empty CM1's vapour within a third of a day to a day. CM1 resets the negative vapour at the start of its microphysics step, so a limit on the table would move the same water from one record to another. The layer above 40 km sends 0.14 mm/day of water down across 40 km on net (11 of 12 days), three quarters of the 0.19 created; at the land box the 0.14 mm/day created joins 1.4–1.6 mm/day of falling water. The sea's twelve-day rain check therefore reads as a miss (32% short with the created water removed) and the land's aridity check as one too (11% short against 10%). rules_weather.txt's amendment of 3 November sets the reading for the boxes still to run. |
| The water table's 1% floor for dry land stays for now. A low-compute side study scopes which developed soils can be kept in the world's semi-engineered, semi-natural way and in which climate zones, for the later choice of soils and for the land scheme's floors. | Author, 2026-11-03 ("wrt the 1% floor, that might be good, but maybe we should do at least some additional investigation in a side agent (low compute) to get some ideas of what kind of soils can be maintained in the same semi-engineered/semi-natural way that we've been maintaining the rest of this world, and where (or at least what zones if not physical boundaries since we're still resolving what exact climate is where in this world).") | Ran 08:00–08:34 (session-tools/agents/soils/soils_maintenance.md). By its reading the soils that can be kept are those that hold a living surface over them, and the cover each needs rises with the storms. Under the storm-water study's upper-bound wear, bare developed soils lose 0.05 mm a year in the arid land, 0.22–0.26 in the semi-arid and 0.47–0.57 in the dry sub-humid, against Earth's natural formation of 0.017–0.036 mm a year. A biological crust alone holds 68–100% of the arid land; the semi-arid land holds where crusts fill the gaps in a cover of about a third to three fifths, and the dry sub-humid only under a nearly closed cover of about half to four fifths; in the humid land forest holds the soil, and the lasting work is fertility, since glassy young soils run down in phosphorus and bases with no uplift to renew them. The maintained drylands come closest to the study's sandy loam and crust, which recharge 1.3–3.9% of the rain by class under Walnut Gulch's storms and 7–17% under CM1's, so the 1% floor stays just below them. The author's decisions on its three recommendations, on the crust's strength, the superseded premises and the slope surfaces, are recorded below. |

| PlaSim's convection trigger is developed on the 15-level boxes of the design set: the layer route (route L) with its own elevated closure, a Betts-Miller relaxation toward the diluted parcel launched from the triggering layer; a surface route for the land's afternoons; the Kuo-forced sea box's quiet nights carried as a limit; then pass rules fixed for the design-years boxes before the offline test reads them, and a report before any Fortran or GCM run. | Author, 2026-11-03, as in the land store's row above; the GCM test of the trigger and closure, 2026-11-03 ("For immediate requested decisions, agree with your recommended decisions for this set.") | The elevated closure built (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/trigger_routes.py): on CM1's air it rains 0.9-2.5 mm/day at sea against CM1's 0.6-1.3 (the proxy 2.1-6.5), with 0.14-0.29 of the rain in the wrong quarter on the 15-level boxes. The surface route follows the heated condensation framework (Tawfik and Dirmeyer 2014): CM1's land storms start as its boundary layer's top reaches the buoyant condensation level. Read on CM1's own air, no closure of this kind can rain through CM1's land afternoon, so the schemes were run instead in PlaSim's single column under each box's own forcing (trigger_column.py), which gives the gap's amounts and afternoon bias with the design scheme, by another mechanism than the GCM's (next row). There route L times the four 15-level sea boxes within the gap's 0.15 (0.04-0.15) but rains 11-47% short, the column keeping the vapour saturated at 2.5-6 km; the lunar closure alone times the land within 0.14; the surface route as built worsens the land and is left out; the Kuo-forced sea box's nights stay wet. With the column found not to convect as the GCM does (next row), the trigger and the closure are tested in the GCM itself: route L and closure (c) are written into PlaSim's convection scheme behind switches, tested step for step against the column's Python, and run two years each, closure (c) alone and with route L, from the design run's year-9 restart after the first store run; they are judged at the three sites by the gap test's rules with the convective and the condensation rain kept apart, with the energy balance and the exobase rechecked, and the column stays as a diagnostic. Built and committed (domain/climate fee8a57): both behind switches that default off and leave the scheme's output byte for byte as before, the Fortran matching the Python copy step for step and the copy matching the column bit for bit over 8,028 steps; the moistening counts PlaSim's dynamics and radiation, since in the leapfrog the change between successive physics states counts convection's own drying as moistening. The runs leave out --sbm-rh, which closure (c) replaces. In the harness the scheme costs about 1.3 times the design run's time with closure (c) and 2.7 times with route L (the design years ran 8-9 minutes a year on four threads). Run 20:07-22:17 and read by the rules (rules_branch_c_L.txt; notes_convection.md, the GCM test, read): closure (c) shuts off the GCM's convective rain (convective share 0.01-0.10 at the boxes against 0.72-0.89; with route L 0.12-0.16); neither run closes the gap (R1 misses at the land and dry boxes in both, marginal at the sea with route L; R2a holds only on land, as before), and every verdict is provisional because the boxes' low air moves 1.2-1.4 K warmer and 2.7-3.2 g/kg moister. The climate warms 1.7-1.8 K by year 1 and has not settled (+2.7 W/m2 at the top); the exobase's inputs stay inside the cold trap's range, falling toward its edge. The diagnosis the rules named (4 November) places the stop at the trigger (next row). |
| The lunar closure relaxes the whole convecting column, from the parcel's source to its top, each layer toward the humidity Romps's (2014) relation gives for its own detrainment rate, in place of one reference of 0.83 over the buoyant layer alone. In the single column the buoyant-layer form times the 15-level sea boxes within 0.04-0.15 but rains 11-47% short, and the whole column at 0.83 rains within -2% to +12% but moves their after-midnight rain into the evening (0.20-0.29); CM1's measured detrainment gives its own humidity through Romps's relation within about 0.05 at 12-40 km in every quarter, including the evening's upper cloud near 0.96. The detrainment comes from CM1's measured cloud-body rate and the IFS's organized detrainment from the updraught's deceleration (Cy36r1, Part IV, eqs. 6.9-6.11); in PlaSim it adds a few operations per convecting column. Built and run in the column: on PlaSim's levels the organized detrainment comes out small and the references at 0.81-0.88, rain within -14% to +9% on the 15-level boxes but 0.19-0.27 displaced; a diagnostic relaxing toward CM1's own vapour each hour does not fix the timing either. The column was then found not to convect as the GCM does (85% of the design years' rain at the sea site is convective, 89% on land; in the column the design scheme's rain is mostly the saturation adjustment's, and route L's buoyant-layer timing was that condensation), so its results cannot yet choose the closure. Its test moves to the GCM with route L (row above). In the GCM, as built with the lunar closure's 18 h relaxation and 2e-4 per metre dilution, it almost stops the convective rain and warms the climate (row above). Traced offline on 4 November (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/notes_convection.md, "Why closure (c) stops the GCM's convective rain"; domain/climate's climate/gcm/README.md): the diluted parcel is 1.9-3.7 K colder than the GCM's air through σ 0.44-0.89 on the design years' columns and on the closure run's own, so the closure convects deep on at most 1% of the boxes' columns and route L launches from at most 2%, against 54-83% on CM1's air at the same boxes. CM1's free troposphere is 2.5-8 K colder and 0.2-0.3 higher in relative humidity at σ 0.44-0.69, the same difference R6 measures at 40-60 km; its temperature alone added to the GCM's columns opens none of route L's layers, its humidity alone 1-13%, both 43-84%. The design scheme holds the GCM's free troposphere near its undiluted parcel and CM1's storms hold theirs near the diluted one; with the convection off, the large-scale condensation's heating keeps the GCM's warm. Romps's humidity blocks the rain a second time where a parcel convects, and the relaxation time plays no part. The convection's redesign follows from that trace (the redesign's row below), and closure (c) and route L stay in the code behind their switches. |

| PlaSim's convection is redesigned toward the air CM1's storms make: a scheme that convects in the GCM's present air, relaxes toward the diluted parcel's temperature and a moister reference, and moistens without raining where its reference stands above the air, built and tested in the single column first, then in PlaSim behind a switch with two GCM years. | Author, 2026-11-04, as in the row above, of the option so described | Phase A ran 05:58-06:57 (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/redesign/report.md; notes_convection.md, "The redesign, phase A"), its rules fixed at 06:32 before the test set was read (rules_redesign.txt). The design keeps the design scheme's undiluted trigger, relaxes toward the diluted parcel's temperature but warms a layer only toward a reference above it, takes Romps's humidity in the buoyant cloud and the air's own vapour beneath it, and moistens without raining by Frierson's (2007) qref form. CM1's own spin-up from GCM air supports the energy choice: while its free troposphere stood 3.3-3.4 K above the diluted parcel, CM1's convective heating there stayed within -0.05 to +0.15 K/day and the radiation cooled it to the parcel in 15-22 days. On the test set the design convects in the GCM's present air and moistens its middle levels (O2, O3a, O3b hold), but it forms its rain above the level of free convection, where 79-100% of it evaporates in the single column, while CM1's convection dries its air mostly at σ 0.69-0.92; the gate misses (O1 by the reader's normalisation, the water kept to 2e-18 kg/m2/s; T2a at the dry box, where neither scheme fires for a lunar day; T2b at sea, within the column's known limit), so the Fortran and the GCM run are not recommended. The author then chose to check the rain's evaporation first and let its result pick between another design step and a GCM test (2026-11-04, "Agreed"), under a rule fixed before the check ran (redesign/rules_drops_check.txt). Phase A's column had evaporated falling rain by PlaSim's original rule, a share of each layer's saturation deficit, which used up the redesign's gentle rain at any height; with the design run's own drop-based evaporation 0.30, 0.53 and 0.37 of the redesign's formed rain reaches the ground at the sea, land and dry boxes, against CM1's own 0.30, 0.47 and 0.31, so the rule chose the GCM test. Phase B (the Python copy, the Fortran behind a switch, its harness tests and the runner's switch) was built 07:25-07:57 and committed (domain/climate 84d94ba5). The two GCM years (08:14-08:58, read by rules_branch_redesign.txt, fixed before the run; notes_convection.md, "The redesign's GCM test") do not close the gap: the sea box's cells rain 0.35 mm/day against CM1's 1.17, with 0.61 of it displaced, the dry box misses R1 and R2a in both years, and the land box holds R1 over the two years only as the average of a wet and a dry year. The convective share of the boxes' rain is 0.14-0.36 (design years 0.72-0.89), the large-scale condensation taking the vapour the scheme carries into σ 0.43-0.69. The free troposphere moved from the undiluted parcel's temperature to 2.2-5.0 K below it and 1.8-1.9 K above the diluted reference, its relative humidity from 0.63-0.67 to 0.74-0.75. The ground warmed to 303.8 K in year 1 with the top of the air still taking in 4.8 W/m², the outgoing longwave 9.1 W/m² lower, 5.5-5.8 W/m² of it in clear sky; the exobase's inputs stay inside their bracket. Not adopted: the literature check and the dilution study that followed (next two rows) set a further design round, and the redesign stays in the code behind its switch (nclos_bm 2). |
| Whether the redesigned closure is adopted depends on an analysis of its physical plausibility, beginning with a very bounded literature check of whether observations concur with its physics. | Author, 2026-11-04 ("Well... adoption will depend on physical plausibility analysis. Maybe have a subagent do a VERY bounded (but still intelligent) search in literature to see if the redesign gets physical concurrence from observations.") | The check runs from 08:58 (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/redesign/brief_literature.md): thirty minutes of reading and ten of writing on six questions (the trigger, the reference temperature and humidity, the one-signed heating, the moistening without rain and the relaxation time), judged on observations and reanalyses translated to the Moon's gravity, column mass and rotation. It ran 08:59-09:30 (report in redesign/literature_check.md; notes_convection.md, "The literature check"). Observations support the redesign's direction: the convecting free troposphere stands colder than the undiluted pseudo-adiabatic parcel, where the design climate's air stands. They conflict with its dilution, Earth's thermal structure fitting plumes three to eight times less diluted per e-folding of pressure than the redesign's 2.0×10⁻⁴ per metre, CM1's own rate, and they partly concur with its trigger, heating, moistening without rain and relaxation time; the reference humidity was not checked. Three options were weighed: keep the design climate with the divergence recorded as uncertainty, a further design round built on the observations, or adoption as built (not recommended). The first step of a further round follows in the next row. |

Keeping the design climate unchanged would not spare the shield a new setting, since a free troposphere standing where observations put it would warm the ground beyond 301 K at today's sunlight. | Author, 2026-11-04 ("The problem with A I think is also it's already running hotter than our design target so we're going to have to adjust the shield, etc. again anyway. A finer CM1 grid raises the sampled rate (one nudged pair), and CM1's grid-wide cores cannot test the gravity translation. The proposed target puts the free troposphere about 3.5 K (2-5 K) below a finely integrated undiluted parcel from the lowest level at σ 0.44-0.69 and about 4 K (3-5.5 K) at 40-60 km, where both CM1's raining-quarter air and Earth's translated rates at the GCM's humidity lie. The design years stand 4.6-7.0 K warmer than its centre. The redesign's air reached it at sea and on land, while its reference, keeping 2-4% of the boundary layer's air, followed the air toward saturation. | The water's effect on the Moon's mass and rotation is checked and written up as a report on main. | Author, 2026-11-04 ("Double check findings then put in a report on main.") | Done: [moon orientation](studies/moon_orientation/README.md). The seas, lakes and pore water are 0.02% of the Moon's mass at first and about 0.1% after a million years. Their uneven weight turns the Moon 8.05° within years (pole toward 167° W, the point beneath Earth to 8.0° N, 6.1° E), stiffens the lock and tilts the spin axis 1.6–2.5° from the ecliptic; nothing threatens the orbit or the Moon's stability. An independent re-derivation reproduced every turn and corrected the hydrostatic part (Darwin–Radau k_f 1.436). The long-term turn, 1.5–8°, depends on today's lithosphere, which no measurement gives. |
| A new domain, domain/geology, holds the solid Moon's response to the water. Its first study builds that response from the Moon's present thermal state, to narrow the long-term turn, and a subagent works it. | Author, 2026-11-04 ("domain/geology makes more snse even though we have to open it. Put it in there and have a subagent work on it.") | Opened 4 November from main at 2cea6b89 in ../terluna-geology, with a new top-level lane geology/ registered on the branch. The study is done (domain/geology 45c3d817; geology/README.md, with the products geology/results/interior_response.json and moon_orientation.json). Three published present-day selenotherms with Hirth and Kohlstedt's olivine flow laws give the Moon's viscoelastic response and through it the turn: in the central case 8.02° at once, 7.62° at 1,000 years, 7.24° at 100,000 years, 6.59° at 10 million and 5.62° at a billion, always toward 167° W. The stiff bound keeps 7.97° at 1,000 years and 7.62° at 10 million. The soft bound, on the warm profile, falls to 1.89° by 1,000 years, and the measured heat flow makes it the least likely of the three. The 8° state is therefore the one people live with unless the shallow mantle is as warm as the warm profile. |
| The second set of full simulations runs on the turned Moon at the elastic state of the seas and lakes, which with the current studies at today's orientation brackets the range the Moon can take. The world developed so far is later carried across to the new orientation. | Author, 2026-11-04 ("We are goingto need to do a second set of full simulations on the final stable orientation though and then at some point we'll have to figure out how to transition everything we've developed in the world adjusted for its new orientation, changing biomes, etc."; then, of the orientation, "8 degrees since that's the other bound from current studied no orientation shift that we've studied.") | Set. In today's coordinates the north pole goes to 81.9° N, 167° W, the south pole to 81.9° S, 13° E and the point beneath Earth to 8.0° N, 6.1° E ([moon orientation](studies/moon_orientation/README.md)); it is the reoriented geography the design run waits for. The frame for the simulations is domain/geology's orientation product (geology/results/moon_orientation.json, 45c3d817), and the orientation is recomputed once with the turned climate's lakes. In the geology study's central case the pole stays within 0.4° of this state for 1,000 years (row above). |

The land box's rain fell because R2 began from the design years' warm free troposphere, in which the scheme convects on almost none of the land's columns: the land's moisture convergence collapsed, the box's soil water fell from 0.37 to 0.04 m within six lunar days and the land's rain moved to the tropical seas (+2.0 mm/day), while the dry box rains more because the cooler free troposphere lets its columns convect on their own evaporation. The land's soil water fell by about 60% in two years, and the land dried under closure (c) and the first redesign too. Whether that dry land is the transition's hysteresis or the scheme's equilibrium is open; a two-year GCM test with the land's soil water restored (about 40 minutes) is proposed before the design run. The 0.70 reference humidity, the shallow scheme's 10 m²/s and the 200 hPa depth test are kept on CM1's evidence; CM1's moister band (0.90-0.93) comes from ice re-evaporating within CM1, which translated observations and the plume model do not join. For the convective cloud the agent proposes a lunar intercept of 0.43 in place of Slingo's 0.245, fitted to CM1's thick cloud, a comparison of CM1's whole cloud with PlaSim's convective part that needs its own GCM test. For the turned design run it was replaced on 5 November by the design years' convection (section "The convection of the turned design run", 5 November). |

The soil-memory test (A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s934_redesign2_soilmem, two years from R2's year-1 state with the design run's soil water put back on the land) dried the land again at R2's own rate, the land box from 0.334 to 0.039 m by lunar day 6 and all land to 0.060 m by the end of year 1, as in R2, so the dry land is the second redesign's own state and not a hysteresis of the transition (next row). The cloud check compared PlaSim's whole cloud with CM1's at the boxes: in raining windows PlaSim's anvils at σ 0.12-0.21 cover 0.16-0.29 of the sky against CM1's 0.57-0.75 and its shortwave cloud effect falls 123 W/m² short at sea and 66 W/m² on land, so by its rules the convective formula was the gap there, while over the lunar day the two models' net cloud effects agree within 4-14 W/m². The GCM test of a lunar intercept (A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s934_redesign2_convday, convective_day_s 388,800 s, 18:25-18:51; report_convday.md) closed the anvil gap on land, but at sea it narrowed the raining-window shortfall only from 135 to 108 W/m² while its lunar-day shortwave effect overshot CM1's by 22.5 W/m², and at the dry box the lunar-day net effect overshot by 10.4 W/m². By its rules Slingo's values stay, with the limit stated: the GCM's sea rains mostly at night, so its anvils' missing reflection belongs to the sea's rain timing, which no cloud formula closes. |
| The second redesign's heat limit is revisited before the design run: one offline design step on how much a convecting column may heat when its free troposphere stands near its reference, then a two-year GCM test, while no design run is launched. | Author, 2026-11-04 (choosing "(b) Revisit the heat limit first": "It's a clear issue and very much impacts things, and we already decided probably no major climate run tonight anyway so it's an effective use of free time.") | The offline step and the build ran 18:36-19:07 (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/followups/report_heat_limit.md). The evidence behind the rule came from CM1's spin-up days, when CM1 did not rain. Raining, CM1 puts 25-66% of its gross convective heating into layers at or above the scheme's reference, where only the imposed circulation's cooling keeps its net tendency small, so the rule followed the net tendency, which a GCM's dynamics supply, and not the gross heating its scheme must supply. The variant chosen, 'spread', keeps every decision and, where the limit would bind, rains the vapour's relaxation unheld, warms the layers below their reference at the limit's rate and spreads the rest of the heat over the cloud layers by one increment; Frierson's form was set aside for putting 23-63% of its heat below the condensation level. In the single column it rains 1.75 and 1.16 times CM1 at the sea and land boxes and ends the band within 1 K of its parcel, warmer than the target, and in the last round the column ran the band 0.9-1.7 K colder than the GCM did at sea and on land. Built behind --sbm-heat spread (domain/climate 6fe1e2d7, after make check). Its two-year GCM test (A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s934_redesign2_heat, 19:24-19:50, under rules fixed before it in rules_heat_limit_gcm.txt; report_heat_gcm.md) gave outcome (D): the land dried as under the scheme's own rule, all land to 0.039 m of soil water against 0.058-0.060 m under that rule and the design years' 0.155 m, with the land box's moisture convergence near nil (+0.05 against the design years' +2.87 mm/day). Freed of the limit, convection carried 0.96-0.97 of the rain within 30° of the equator, but its heat spread over the whole tropics: the tropical free troposphere stood 0.92 K warmer than under the scheme's own rule and its band 1.09 K above the fine parcel, about two thirds of the way back to the design years' state, and at the sea and land boxes the afternoon band stood nearer its parcel than the 1 K the rules name as undoing the target. The switch stays off (climate/gcm/README.md on domain/climate, 0fb79181). |

The rule chosen leaves the sub-cloud layers to the boundary layer and the surface (--sbm-subcloud cloud, domain/climate e6b09e7a).
