The switch stays off (climate/gcm/README.md, 0fb79181). CM1's sub-cloud budget in report_subcloud.md summed CM1's vturb terms with its boundary layer's tendency, which they nearly repeat (in the free troposphere's band on the land box's third day vturbs gives +0.068 against the boundary layer's +0.065, at sea +0.085 against +0.081; session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/refinement/turb_terms.out), and the refinement's band budget closes only without them; that report's turbulence column and its residuals of -1.0 to -5.6 mm/day carry the double count, and its convective net, on which the rule rests, does not. |

## The seas' models and the waters' studies, 3–4 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The seas' circulation is set up for modelling on domain/hydrosphere, from a Codex proposal, and simulation beyond the setup waits on more of the base climate work. | Author, 2026-11-03 ("I'd still wait on more of our base climate work before we do too much in terms of simulation but we can at least start setting things up." "Work in domain/hydrosphere") | Set up on domain/hydrosphere (60e5387): two ocean models built from pinned sources with the Moon's gravity, radius and rotation. The seas' climate-forced circulation waits on the base climate (climate/ocean/README.md, Next work). |
| The ocean models are MITgcm and SCHISM, SCHISM through a lunar patch kept as the project's own fork, each checked against geography's linear tide solver before use. | Author, 2026-11-03 ("MITgcm plus SCHISM, as Codex proposes: an hour or two more setup, its own checks, and a fork to maintain.") | Done (60e5387). MITgcm matches the solver's seiches to 0.0013% and its forced response to 0.21% with a hundred times the solver's friction; SCHISM's forced response matches to 0.02%. |
| The ocean models live in climate/ocean. | Author, 2026-11-03 ("Use climate/ocean as placement.") | Done (60e5387). |
| The first study on them, run now ahead of the climate-forced work, is Mare Fecunditatis's monthly tide through its straits. | Author, 2026-11-03 ("And run the Fecunditatis strait. It would be nice to know at least a bit more about the oceans/seas.") | Done on domain/hydrosphere (c15d3130). Both models ran the 222.9 days and agree on Fecunditatis's tide within 5%. The straits halve its monthly tide, 0.58–0.60 of the equilibrium's amplitude and 2.4–2.6 days late, its western channels carry tidal races of 2.2–2.3 m/s, and the open sea follows its equilibrium tide within 6% (climate/ocean/README.md, What the runs find). |
| Where the fresh waters run coldest is estimated on the design years: the highland lakes, with the air carried up its lapse rate, and shallow ponds through the lunar night. | Author, 2026-11-04 ("I'm also curious if we have any "relatively cold" waters where fish that prefer colder temps reside." then, of the offer to estimate both, "Please do.") | Done on domain/hydrosphere (1ac9357d; research/studies/cold_waters). The fresh water stays above about 15 °C everywhere: the deep highland lakes hold about 21 °C at depth, and ponds of 0.5–1 m on the highest ground fall to about 17 °C late in the night. |
| Two studies that need little computing run side by side at equal priority, each by its own subagent: the monthly tide in the shores' groundwater, with the seas' load at the roots, and the crust's sag under the seas. | Author, 2026-11-04 ("Alright.. (1) and (7). Have two subagents work on those and let's see what they yield.  They're the same priority as far as I'm concerned.") | Done on domain/hydrosphere: the crust's response (c2c8624a; geography/README.md, The crust's response to the water) and the shores' tide (a81d79c2; research/studies/coast_water). Holding 28% takes 0.89 million km³ more water within years, mostly for the water's own gravity, and 1.4–2.6 million km³ if the mantle flows. The tide widens the desert belts by about 2%; the held sea cements about a centimetre of soil a century at their roots, and the delivered water's chloride would salt them within two years unless flushed. |

Counted as a lunar-day dose, photosynthetic light reaches 71 m in the open sea at the equator and 42 m at 75°; no UV-B reaches the water; fish see by day to about 690 m. |

## The convection of the turned design run, 5 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The convection's changes are model refinements, and the records call them so: they change how the GCM represents storms and move no design condition of the world. | Author, 2026-11-05 ("Also please be careful: 'redesign' implies we're intentionally changing a design condition. (c) I THINK should be referred to more as continued 'morel refinement' no?") | Applied from 5 November: the first and second redesigns of the rows of 3-4 November are the convection's first and second refinements in research/plan.md, research/status.json and domain/climate's GCM README (0fb79181). The rows above keep their wording, and the code keeps its names (--sbm-closure redesign and redesign2, nclos_bm 2 and 3, the _redesign runs), on which run keys and the patches' ledger depend. |
| The turned design run keeps the design years' convection, so that its differences from the design run come from the turn alone, and the convection's refinement continues as its own line meanwhile, read against the turned run's results as they come. | Author, 2026-11-05 (choosing (a), the turned design run with the design years' scheme, and (c), the refinement continuing as its own line: "Alright... Let's do (a) but also work on (c). The turned design will give us some more info anyway which we can then use to make inferences and extrapolations about how things will be as we continue refining the model.") | Replaces the second refinement's adoption of 4 November for that run. The run takes the design run's configuration (A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s934: SBM at 0.7 with the 200 hPa depth test) on the geography turned into domain/geology's frame, keeping the dry Moon's geoid until the atlas work (row of 4 November on the water's own gravity), and it starts as the next row sets. The second refinement and its heat and sub-cloud switches stay in the code, off. The refinement's first step ran offline 06:35-07:18 (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/refinement/report_free_troposphere.md) and found no rule worth building. Across fourteen state-years of the GCM runs the tropical band's temperature varies little (272.1-273.5 K) while the boundary layer's parcel under it moves 3.6 K: where a scheme convects it holds the band at its own equilibrium, the undiluted trigger parcel when its heat is free and the second refinement's reference when its heat is held, so the second refinement meets the target in its convecting columns mainly through a warmer boundary layer. Its large-scale condensation rains in the equatorial seas' afternoon ascent, in columns whose surface parcels stand 4.2-4.5 K colder than the convecting columns'. A trigger on the reference's own plume rains less there, and condensate sublimating in the band would not cool it: CM1's band is a net condensing layer, kept cool by radiation at sea and the dry box and by the imposed ascent on land, and it still cools 0.25-0.35 K a lunar day at days 3-4. What follows is open. |
| The turned design run starts from the design run's last state carried with the ground into the turned frame: PlaSim's own fresh start on the turned surface gives everything it derives from a surface, and the design run's climate gives the state, the surface's fields moved with the ground. Of the three starts weighed it is the nearest to a climate turning with its ground, and its tool is the first piece of the machinery the eventual build-years simulation, which follows the world as it is built, will need. | Author, 2026-11-05 (asking "Which one would be closest to the physical reality of the Moon rotating over a number of years as we add water? Which one would give us the most insight into building the eventually needed 'build years simulation' which tracks how the world actually evolves as we build it out?", then, of carrying the climate with the ground, "Proceed.") | A PlaSim restart carries its land-sea mask and orography, so the run could not start from the design run's state as it stood; the two fresh starts weighed, from ExoPlaSim's own cold state (about 45 years) or from a warm one (untried), begin from states the Moon never passes through. The turned surface (domain/climate 55c31e9e; climate/gcm/turned.py, climate/results/gcm/turned_surface_8d71fd350ca4c6cd.json) keeps the water's share to 0.00001 and puts geology's named places within 0.004° of its own turned coordinates. A year of the design run rerun on today's shared PlaSim source, every refinement patch off, is identical to the design run's year 10 in all 91 variables of its year file and all 185 records of its restart (session-tools/turned_design/compare_source_check.out). The carry (climate/gcm/carry.py, 2ec61a79) takes the masks, the orography and the runoff's velocities from a lunar day of PlaSim's fresh start on the turned surface and the clock, the atmosphere and the surface's state from the design run's year-19 restart. The run, A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s934_turned, takes 20 years at the design run's sunlight from 07:10 on 5 November (session-tools/turned_design/run_turned.sh), its years 10-19 the turned design years; its first lunar day's zonal tables are finite, with temperatures within 2-3 K of the design run's. It ran 07:10-10:13. The turned design years settle near 301.7 K, 0.80 K above the design years and 0.39 K above the 301.25 K target, mostly through the clouds, whose net cooling weakens by 1.6 W/m² as the air holds 7% more vapour; the rain stays at 2.02 mm/day against 2.00. Land below an aridity of 0.65 keeps its share (57.5% against 57.8%) while arid and semi-arid land grows from 44.2% to 47.1%: the near side's north wets as its seas and coasts move toward the equator, and the southern highlands dry as they move poleward, Tycho's ground turning from humid to dry sub-humid and the dry south-east block from semi-arid to arid. Jura's two land cells rain 481 and 741 mm a year against 517 and 488, the lower turning humid, nine tenths of it at night. The exobase stays inside its bracket. The longwave correction strays past its 2.5 W/m² rule at sea, to 3.56 W/m² in the fourth layer from the ground by year 19, so the turned climate takes a longwave refit of its own before a design run on the turned geography. One fitted offline on year 19 holds within 1.11 W/m² on columns of every kind and lowers the outgoing longwave by 1.03 W/m² at that state, which with the ground's own offset puts that run's sunlight near 0.926 at first order (climate/gcm/README.md on domain/climate, cf9e75e9; session-tools/turned_design/). |
| No run is made at the turn's ten-million-year state, 6.6°, for now. The runs at today's orientation and at the elastic 8° bracket every state domain/geology gives, and the turn of the first thousand years, 7.1-8.0° outside the least likely soft case, lies within a sixth of a GCM cell of the 8° run. | Author, 2026-11-05, proposing ("I think the final tilt will be closer to 6.6 degrees so we might want to do a run around that orientation (maybe actually off the 8 degree orientation which is the closest thing to simulating a shift back in settling state - much the way we're using this build off the old as a sort of estimate on transition)."), then, told that the geology puts 6.6° about ten million years after the water arrives ("Yeah alright we can hold off on the 6.6 degree middle.") | Held. In domain/geology's central case the pole moves 8.02° at once, 7.62° by 1,000 years, 6.96° by a million and 6.59° by ten million (geology/README.md, 45c3d817). The climate settles within decades while the turn eases over millions of years, so a run at 6.6° shows the Moon of that age, its start setting only its spin-up; the turned run's end, carried with the ground, is the cheapest start. Its climate can meanwhile be read about 82% of the way from the design run to the turned run. Geology's time series holds the pole and the point beneath Earth for every state, from which a frame can be built. |
| The convection's refinement first rebuilds its yardstick: CM1 boxes that make their own large-scale vertical motion by the damped gravity wave, so that they can say how much rain the state should give and how much moisture the storms should draw in. But maybe another model that's simply a different take might give us insight. We used mitgcm for sea work, but it's a general model right?  Could we use that to investigate if we keep hitting dead ends with refinements?") | Built on domain/climate (c3898a7c, after make check; climate/crm/README.md): the damped gravity wave over the inflow build (moon_omp_rain_law_pbl_wind_gust_aloft_inflow_dgw, var9), its rate set by the lunar first baroclinic mode's speed, 40.5 m/s, and the site's footprint, about 2.9 hours at the sea and land boxes and 2.4 at the dry box (0.65-18.7 across the range carried), its lid the GCM's convection top near 73 km, its source cut below the boundary layer's top and its reference the site's GCM column by local time. The three boxes, box_0e_dgw, box_near_side_sea_dgw and box_se_dry_dgw, each run to its fourth lunar day under rules fixed before any of them ran (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/yardstick/rules_wtg.txt, rules_wtg_boxes.txt). The offline step found that the second refinement lost the land box's afternoon convergence in its horizontal part: +3.09 mm/day in the design years against -9.37 under the second refinement, while the vertical part stands at +11.25 against +9.64 (report_wtg.md). Smoke-tested 10:20-10:44; the boxes run from 10:44 (run_wtg_boxes.sh: land, sea, then dry; about 11 hours at the smoke tests' speed). MITgcm's own atmospheric packages, atm_phys (a Betts-Miller convection with grey radiation, PlaSim's family), aim_v23 (SPEEDY's mass flux) and fizhi (relaxed Arakawa-Schubert), are the candidates for such a check. Read after the chain (10:44-21:23) by the rules, with one change appended at 18:44 for the wave record's missing segment-end intervals, which the budget now estimates from their neighbours (domain/climate 3d42c797, after make check; the estimate moves the import by at most 0.05 mm/day). The land box sides with neither GCM state: its own afternoon import is +17.1 mm/day against the design years' +11.25 and the second refinement's +9.64, with its band within 0.15 K of the reference, so its own convection draws in more than either state; it rains 6.66 against 6.01 mm/day (+10.8%, within 10% with CM1's clipped water out, so unresolved) with its rain in the right quarters. The sea box rains twice the GCM's, 2.85 against 1.42 mm/day, and misses the wind check narrowly, so it reads as observations; the dry box dries almost out, 0.01 against 0.61 mm/day under a mean descent, the dry equilibrium the rules anticipated, and misses three validity checks (session-tools/agents/gap_boxes/yardstick/reading_wtg.md). Proposals, none started: the land import's dependence on the wave's time, the dry box's moist start, and boxes forced by the second refinement's state. |

## The Moon's energy supply, the loads' placement and the fault across the ring, 5–6 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The Moon's energy supply is reviewed on domain/provisioning: what the project has analysed of beamed power's costs and benefits against its alternatives, local production and local solar included, with a broader study where that analysis is limited. | Author, 2026-11-05, after the beams' heat and losses were read back (the deep air takes 6.3-7.8% of the beams; each delivered watt leaves 1.47-1.49 W of heat) ("If this were a dryer thinner world it would work more. I'm not sure if I feel like this approach makes sense, though it could still actually be more effective than local energy production including local solar to get close to the equivalent. Is that a provisoining or resources question? Whichever it is, have a subagent work in the appropriate branch and have it review what analysis we've done on cost/benefits of energy transfer, alternatives, etc. and if it's limited have it do a broader study.") | Done on domain/provisioning (fc6be517, after make check; provisioning/energy_options.md; agent 16:55-17:41, session-tools/agents/energy/report_energy.md). The analysis was limited: the beams had been weighed against fusion and sized in depth, never against local solar under the design climate, wind, fission, raised receivers, other frequencies, optical links or loads made in orbit on one basis. On that basis the beams stand as the bulk supply: 1.47-1.50 W of heat a delivered watt, the dense air 0.10-0.12 W of it and nearly all the oxygen's (water vapour 3-5% at 2.45 GHz), against 2.0-3.0 W for fission or fusion; local solar leaves 0.19-0.79 W but the deep, cloudy air passes 34% of the sunlight to the ground, so a delivered TW takes 39,000-94,000 km² of panels and a grid round the Moon or 300-330 TWh of storage each lunar night, 3.8-16 million km² of ground for each point of dimming saved; wind, rivers, tides, the heat flow and the seas' thermal gradients reach about 1 TW together. Making the food the beams power at the ring radius would be the largest lever (0.12-0.97 points), if its carbon and 2.3-3.4 Mt a year of phosphorus could return (not taken up; next row); local supplies belong in the reserve for a fault across the ring. |
| Computing runs in the ring fleet's array as far as latency allows, with only the work that needs low latency on the Moon; the food the beams power is made on the Moon, in the blend as set. | Author, 2026-11-05, answering the decision ask on making that food, and most close computing, at the ring radius, which would take the beams' heat from 0.39-1.84 points of dimming to 0.20-0.81 at the cost of return paths for the food's carbon and phosphorus ("Compute makes sense to have as much as possible at the array with only local compute as needed for low latancy. Food production... that requires getting more resources back TO the array as well, so there's that to consider. I think food production should remain local.") | Adopted. On the computing screen's division (provisioning/computing.md: the Moon's interactive services work within the 0.13 s round trip to the ring radius; millisecond control such as tile keeping, sky-boat separation, grid protection and robots stays on the Moon), three-quarters of close computing moves to the ring radius, which takes 0.06 points off the beams' heat: 28-152 TW stay on the Moon, 0.32-1.78 points, 3.0-22% of the resort's dimming (domain/provisioning afe38eda, after make check, energy options mix A2). The food at the ring radius would have saved 0.12-0.97 points more, but its carbon (0.47-0.71 Gt a year, 1.5-6.2 times the managed import) and 2.3-3.4 Mt of phosphorus a year would arrive on the Moon and need freight back to the array or sinks on the Moon, and the food supply would depend on the array and its arrivals. This answers the heat levers' question on food made in orbit. The power screen, the heat levers and the domain synthesis's array case keep close computing on the Moon until their next refresh. |
| The fault across the ring is studied on research/array-industry: what can stop every beam at once, which of those faults can also reach the tiles' attitude, station-keeping and the film's filter and shade, the time a restart takes and the stores it needs, and a separation of the beams' control from the tiles' attitude and station-keeping, so that a fault in the power cannot become a fault in the shade. | Author, 2026-11-05, after the beams' one common point of failure was set against the shade's, whose opening would tip the upper air into its swollen state ("A fault across the ring would also be catostrophic for the atmosphere so... not sure that's as big an issue as it seems but yeah. No?"), then ("Yeah alright. Let's have a subagent do that study on the array-industry branch") | Done on research/array-industry (1b2358a1, after make check: 4,654 passed; research/studies/solar_shield_array/ring_fault.md, ring_fault.py; agent 18:34-19:18, session-tools/agents/ring_fault/report_ring_fault.md). Ten classes of fault reach the whole ring. The film filters the ultraviolet without control, but the shade's position rests on photon keeping: a film tile without attitude control touches its neighbour in 20 min-2.5 h (seconds to minutes if its momentum store dumps into it), free rings close within 2.5-6 h, and losing 28,000-254,000 tiles at once carries the UV transmission past the latch, which does not undo itself; the upper air's response time is open (a bracket of 15 days-5 years). With the separation (own hardware, dissimilar software, separate command authority, relative navigation, staged updates, actuators and beams that fail safe; 0.28-2.8 Mt over the fleet) the faults of the beams' reference, pilots, scheduling, updates, intrusion and operations reach the beams only; space weather, shared navigation and timing, and a collision cascade at the rings' common line of nodes can still open the shade, and over long horizons must be excluded by physical margin. Beam recoil (21-31 N a whole-tile transmitter in use) makes a ring-wide stop a force step on every transmitter, and a whole-tile transmitter shares the rings' one sail loading only near 0.21 kg/m², 12-19 times lighter than the 1978 reference, a conflict for the power screen's whole-tile transmitters. Proposals: transmitters as vehicles of their own outside the nested rings; 8 days in the ground's store (an extreme proton event needs 8, an intrusion up to 15); spares of order 28,000-62,000 tiles in the formation; a time-dependent thermal column for the upper air's response. The power screen's next work 5 is answered in part. |
| A time-dependent study of how fast the upper air would swell past the latch, the ring-fault study's first open question, follows in the atmosphere domain soon, not yet. | Author, 2026-11-06 ("I do still want to do that study at some point soon but not yet.") | Noted; not started (research/studies/solar_shield_array/ring_fault.md, open question 1, research/array-industry 1b2358a1). |

Have a subagent do a quick study on that."), then ("Wait wait wait. No... the idea was to have a migration. We would change the places of course because... DIFFERENT WORLD. That's why I was asking if there was a place where there would be a one way flow to contain the salt while still allowing migration away from the saltwater to the much more massive freshwater locations.") | Done on domain/hydrosphere (f6fe4026, after make check: 4,822 passed; research/studies/seawater_lakes/README.md, run.py; agent 10:29-10:58, session-tools/agents/seawater_lakes/report_seawater_lakes.md). Easiest held as a terminal lake in a dry belt, a metre or more below a held fresh sea or large rain-fed lake on a fine-grained floor, fed by one downhill channel, its bleed and floods to pans in a separate hollow: every flow of water and salt runs inward and only evaporation leaves; the atlas's common-level desert hollows are the candidates, 10-30 m deep (0.36-1.08 Mt of salt per km²). A 20 m lake needs 718 kt of salt per km² and 18-28 L/s of fresh inflow per km²; upkeep from nothing on the held sea's water to about 3 kt of salt a year on magnesium-bearing feeds, so the fresh seas' magnesium (the 19 October row) sets it; wet belts shed their salt in 13-36 years. One-way isolation with fish passage is viable: the whole inflow falls down a pool fishway into the lower lake, its jets carry no salt upstream, and lunar gravity gives 0.31 m drops for 1 m/s jets (0.051 m on Earth). Barramundi are realistic in lakes of about 10 km² and 5-10 m or more; mullet need 100 km² and 30 m or more at 35-37 g/kg; Australian bass need colder brackish water than the Moon has; eels would rest on hatchery spawning and larval rearing (pressure is one maturation cue, and hatcheries spawn them at surface pressure). The dry belts, desert hollows and water tables come from products on today's orientation, so the sites are left to the atlas round on the turned Moon. Level connections and fish locks leak salt; a perched lake leaks through any practical liner. Follow-up on the migration (agent 14:59-15:15, research/studies/seawater_lakes/sites.py, results/sites.json): of 428 atlas hollows below the common level within 50 km of the large waters, 104 cannot be relied on to hold seawater on the turned design years and 38 meet the four criteria (a nursery of 100 km² or more and 20 m deep, 1 m³/s of inflow or more, 20 km or less to a named sea, 10 Gt of salt or less); hollow 547 beside the Near-side Sea (turned 37.5° N, 43.6° E) is recommended: 789 km², 40 m mean depth, 1.15 Gt of salt, 13 km to the sea over a 45 m pass the channel is cut through, 0.98-1.15 m a year net evaporation, 25-29 m³/s of inflow. The eel dossier on domain/food-systems (cb85374c, remote) sets the nursery: seawater for the eggs, a brackish reach of 13-17 psu at the fishway's mouth for the larvae, and an eel ramp for the glass eels; the lake serves its local spawning route and its saline fallback beside its freshwater endpoint. |

## The food-systems branch's evaluation, wild lines from Earth stock, the eel's seawater spawning and organisms crossing between worlds, 6 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The food-systems branch, with the aquatic foods and the eel and sturgeon dossiers of 4–5 November (`cb85374c` on the remote), is evaluated against the register, the standing directions and the older food work, with the ideas that come from setting the older and newer work side by side. The review and everything worth keeping from it stay on the research drive. | Author, 2026-11-06 ("Have a subagent run a proper evaluation on the food-systems branch. I'm sure codex messed up a lot esp. since it does not have access to memories. Also have it consider the current work against old work. Any interesting ideas pop out?"), then, of the review ("I don't want this report getting lost either so it should be recorded and any information that should be preserved should be on the project space drive.") | Done. On the research drive, `session-tools/agents/food_systems_review/` holds the brief, the report with the lead's checks, the source checks, the agent's transcript, a request to the branch's Codex for the author's words, never sent because that session can no longer be reached, an unsent draft of fixes and the rework plan. Sound and kept: the trial design, the split of raw, cooked and material products, complete life cycles from captive-bred founders, and the eel's salinity evidence stage by stage, which the seawater lakes already use; the eel calculator regenerates byte for byte, its 49 tests pass and the branch merges cleanly with main. To fix on the branch: fish have no place in the diet or the waters' phosphorus, and fed eel at 1 kg cooked a person a year for 8 billion people would release 167 kt of phosphorus a year, 3–8 times the 21–56 kt the waters' life can spare, so fed fish belong in closed systems; no fresh water falls below about 15 °C, so no sturgeon spawns in the open waters, though adults could live deep in the coldest highland lakes; the lines meant to recruit on their own are untested against the rules for what lives wild; the author's directions sit in a register of the branch's own; and the prose needs the editorial pass. Ideas, none started: soda lakes as phosphate traps with a spirulina food web; fish runs that carry phosphorus back uphill; the waters' phosphorus as the measure of the wild fish in the heritage meat; the lunar night as the sturgeon's winter in the highest lakes; tropical eels for the warm seas; captive lines of threatened eels and sturgeon as insurance populations; and a urea-excreting fish for soda water. |
| Every line that lives wild meets the rules for what lives wild of 12 October, the fish bred from Earth stock among them: selective breeding comes first, and a minimal engineering step then gives each wild line the shared signals, reporting traits and DNA watermark. | Author, 2026-11-06 ("I think I understand and agree. Just give me one paragraph explanation. If I change my mind later I'll ammend the decision."), to the recommendation to apply those rules to the eel and sturgeon lines bred from Earth stock: breed first, then add the watermark and reporting traits as the minimal engineering every wild line carries | Set. The food-systems branch's lines meant to recruit on their own are tested against every one of the rules, returning what they take, phosphorus above all, among them ([machines](../biosphere/ecology/machines.md)). |
| Held seawater lakes for the eel's spawning, the migration of 6 November, lessen the need to engineer an all-freshwater life cycle, so far as the lakes can be held and the eggs develop in their warmth; the all-freshwater cycle stays a longer-term engineered option. | Author, 2026-11-06 ("If we can get solid saltwater environments for eel spawning (and they can spawn in warmer water - I don't know if that's true or not) then it reduces how much we have to rely on forcing an all freshwater lifecycle."), to the recommendation to lead with the seawater-lake migration and keep the all-freshwater cycle as a longer-term engineered option | Leaning, conditional on the lake's warmth. Japanese eel eggs have been caught near the top of the thermocline, most at 150 m, under an upper 100 m at 26–30 °C (Aoyama et al. 2014); in hatcheries they hatch at 19–28 °C and none hatch at 31 °C (search summaries of the incubation studies, whose papers were unreachable); and the tropical Celebes eel spawns in Tomini Bay and the Celebes Sea close to its rivers (Arai 2014). The seawater-lakes screen set every lake at the design years' sea surface of 29.5 °C without modelling the lake, so the first check is the temperature of the spawning layer, with tropical eels or a line selected for eggs 1–2 K warmer if it runs warm. The larger hurdle is maturation: eels in captivity are matured with hormones (recalled), and the deep water's pressure, one of their cues, would take 0.9–1.2 km of water at lunar gravity, so the line is selected or engineered to mature in the lake. |
| Organisms will cross between Earth and the Moon over time, as they cross between continents. The design accepts the crossing, plans for it over long terms and mitigates its risks, the parasite risk of raw fish from the Moon's warm waters among them. | Author, 2026-11-06 ("I'm trying to remember what that was about. Ah I think I know. The fact that just like with trying to keep continents isolated, the reality is that we're going to get crossover between organisms. We'll have to figure out how to deal with that rather than pretend that we can totally eliminate all transfer. We can however mitigate and plan over long terms. It was in part related to the parasite problem. Cold salt water is great for lower parasite risk, but we can still probably mitigate risk in these environments while admitting that we will get organisms moving between worlds."), confirming the direction the food-systems branch recorded in its own register (`aquatic_decisions.md`, line 12, at `cb85374c`) | Direction. The DNA watermark of the rules for what lives wild tells the planned lines from those that crossed. |

Done 16:36 (runner and watch exit 0, the watch never paused). Years 10–49, the forty years the remade map reads: ground 301.20 K, rain 1.97 mm/day, cloud cover 37.7%. The run cooled by about 0.008 K a year and settled near 301.1 K, years 40–49 at 301.09 K in balance at the top (+0.06 W/m²), against 301.33 K and -0.20 W/m² in years 10–19; rain and cloud barely moved (1.98 to 1.96 mm/day). |

Aridity alone is too narrow an axis, since the temperature varies little, so the biome analysis looks for a further axis among the conditions the world's builders will not set, topography among them, and tries the author's Codex mockups with the exact data where the data exist. | Author, 2026-11-07 ("Hmm... I think the split is fine. What's not really fine is the singular axis I think. Temperature doesn't really vary much so that's not going to be a major driver of biome. I'm not sure what might be. We'll keep the biome map as well.") | Tried on 8 November on domain/ecology (7472f0a8; the next section); how the moisture and the terrain combine into the biome map stays open. Proposed on 7 November: Walter's frame, the climate's zonal biomes divided by altitude belts on the 16 px/deg terrain and by a topographic wetness index from the drainage routing, with the rain's timing within the lunar day as a further climate axis. The author's Codex chat (Explore terrain as a biome axis, 7 November) made four mockups and a cycle screen, copied to session-tools/codex_terrain_axis_2026-11-07/ on the research drive; humidity × terrain can be made properly on the remade map, the water regimes need the groundwater rerun on the turned climate and the lake distribution, and the ecological strategies stay ideation without a vegetation model. |
| No dimming graded by latitude for now; cooler climates come from height. | Author, 2026-11-07 ("Let's avoid conditional dimming for now. We'll go up for cooler climates.") | Set. Under lunar gravity the air cools by at most about 1.6 K per km (the dry adiabatic rate, a sixth of Earth's), so the natural uplands stay near 20 °C, and the cold comes from built height: the winter parks at 13.5–15 km sit just below freezing. |
| The lakes get an approximate distribution among fresh, soda and seawater lakes, by the area that works best for each, balanced against biological and economic value. It is set beside the water-balance screen of the 1.12 million km² of closed basins below sea level in dry cells, which serves the soda lakes and the seawater lakes alike. | Author, 2026-11-07, to the proposal to run that screen with the soils study ("Reasonable. | The biome map's remake compares the two windows of the turned run at the target. It builds the map on the full forty years (10–49) and on years 10–19, with the climate averages and the rivers and lakes on both. If the classes and the lakes barely move, the 10–19 products stand and the forty-year versions confirm them; if they move, the forty-year versions replace them and the globe is redrawn. The difference between the windows, both drawn from one run, also measures how far a cell's class moves by chance, against which the 45% of ground that changed class after the turn is read. | Author, 2026-11-07 ("Hmm... is there a middle option? Can we just compare both cases? Or is that going to take a lot more compute?"), then, to the proposal to run the comparison as part of the remake ("Sounds good.") | Done (40e28efa). Years 10–19 keep the forty years' class on 75.8% of the land, with drylands of 59.5% against 57.9% and rain-fed lakes on land of 2.458 against 2.457 million km²; the forty-year drainage holds 33,686 lakes over 10.30% of the Moon against 33,262 over 10.28%. The four decades classed alone differ in class on 30.5% of the land, 25.8% in UNEP's five classes, so the 45% of ground that changed after the turn exceeds chance by about 19 points, an upper bound on what the turn and the new climate changed. The turned design years' products, the globe and its rivers and lakes among them, therefore stand, and the forty-year versions confirm them. The forty-year climatology took seconds, the reroute about a minute and the map seconds. |

## Main brought up to date, the mockups on the exact data and the weather cycles' rescreen, 8 November 2026

Hold.") | Held. With the gap carried as a range (the last row) the hold on the local climates lifts without them; their restarts stay on the research drive (`session-tools/gap_turned/`). |
| The hold on the local climates and the Jura box continues after the turned sea box: its gap moves 12–34% of the land's moisture classes against the 10% bar, and even the direction of the rain's error is unsettled, the boxes driven by the climate model's motion raining 0.75–0.83 of its amount and the boxes making their own motion 1.1 times it on land and twice at sea. | Author, 2026-11-08, after the moisture map's sensitivity to the gap ("Then hold the boxes... But give me a full paragraph description of what is fairly clear about Jura Island under the reorientation.") | Lifted the same evening for the local climates and the Jura study, which carry the gap as a range (the last row); the boxes stay paused (the first row). |
| The corrected run's years 10–19 are at the target: at 301.04 K, 0.21 K below the 301.25 K warm edge, they are the design climate on the turned geography, without a second step at the rule's 0.940. | Author, 2026-11-08, answering whether 301.04 K counts as at the target or the rule's second step at 0.940 should run ("I think the temperature is at its target.") | Set (climate/gcm/README.md on domain/climate, The corrected design climate on the turned Moon, 93854f06). The run, `A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s938_turned_refit_precession` (the twilight counted once and the equinox regressing, at sunlight 0.938 from the target run's year 49), ran its twenty years on 8 November, years 1–19 from 14:46 to 17:37 under the watch, which never paused it (`session-tools/precession/` on the research drive). Years 10–19 stand at 301.04 K with the top of the air at −0.23 W/m² and settle near 301.0 K. The rule of 31 October gives S** = 0.940 (0.9399–0.9402), an imbalance of −0.39 W/m² at 301.25 K, so removing the double count cooled the climate about 0.3 K more than the step carried. The twilight is counted once to the last year (slope 1.003 and rms 0.36 W/m², against 7.0 for the double count), the longwave correction holds (largest layer 1.82 W/m²) and so does the exobase (the top level at 166.8–167.0 K with 0.042–0.044 ppmv of vapour, inside the target run's range). Against the target run the air poleward of 70° cools 0.7 K, the clouds' shortwave effect strengthens 3.5 W/m² at unchanged cover as the double count leaves the all-sky pass, and the land dries slightly: drylands 60.7% against 59.1–59.9% in the target's ten-year blocks, the north 63.2% against 58.3–61.6%, land rain 2.15 against 2.16–2.19 mm/day. The night rain where the gap lies does not drop: over the Near-side Sea box's site it rises 12%, and Jura's cells rain 598 and 848 mm a year against 503 and 782. The run changes the twilight, the equinox and the sunlight together, so none of these differences is the fix's alone. The products still read the turned design run's forty years; rebuilding them on the corrected climate on the same footing needs its run extended to year 49, about 4½ hours on four threads, done on 9 November ([next section](#the-products-on-the-corrected-climate-and-the-gaps-closure-moved-to-world-crafting-9-november-2026)). |
| The gap between the climate model and CM1 is carried as a range for now: the local climates, the Jura study and the other studies that read the design climate go ahead on the corrected climate with the climate model's reading and the dry end of the boxes driven by its motion, and the places that rain at night are designed to work at the dry end. For them this replaces the wait of 1 November for the models to agree ([above](#the-design-run-31-october-2026)). | Author, 2026-11-08, choosing among carrying the range, a third model's check with a different convection scheme and further refinement toward closure ("Carry the range for now I suppose.") | Set (climate/gcm/README.md on domain/climate, 93854f06). The two ways of tying a CM1 box to the large scale bracket the climate model. Under its imposed motion the turned sea box rains 0.75 of its rain, 0.09 mm/day of it at night against the model's 0.70; making its own motion by the damped gravity wave, the sea box forced by the design years rains twice its rain, its night rain 0.66 against 0.72 mm/day and the excess in the afternoon. Both read as observations by their rules, and the spread between the methods exceeds the gap. On the moisture map's reading of the target run 78% of the land keeps its moisture class under the gap's scaling, and 23% takes a quarter or more of its rain at night, mostly beside the sea (16% of the land) and poleward of 60° (9%), Jura's cells included. The land and dry boxes stay paused (the first row), and a third model stays the author's check (register, 5 November). Confirmed on 9 November, with the work of closing the gap moved to the world-crafting project ([next section](#the-products-on-the-corrected-climate-and-the-gaps-closure-moved-to-world-crafting-9-november-2026)). |

## The freshwater littoral community: the shore's water, the founders' review and the simulator shelved, 9 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The self-sustaining freshwater littoral community is studied on domain/ecology: a community of producers, decomposers, grazers and predators that feed one another and reproduce in the fresh shallows without restocking. | Author, 2026-11-09 ("Let's do this self-sustaining freshwater littoral community study"), with their ChatGPT conversation of that day on founder species, kept on the research drive (`conversation-exports/chatgpt-founders-2026-11-09/`) | Done on domain/ecology (20719673, 843b3727, 3c9e9918; research/studies/littoral_community). The shore's water on the corrected design climate (forcing.py, results/forcing.json; five sites, closed pans in the lake model and slabs trading water with the open water): the sea shores' open water averages 28.9–30.4 °C; a closed 1–2 m pan at 8° swings from 23–24 °C late in the night to 38–41 °C late in the day; a 2 m shore trading three tenths of its water a day with the sea holds 27–36 °C at 8° and 30°, 28–33 °C at 47° and 28–31 °C at 64°; a sheltered lake margin at 14° spends 95 hours of each lunar day above 36 °C; photosynthetic light just below the surface stays under 1 µmol m⁻² s⁻¹ for 261 hours of the lunar day at 8°, 244 at 30°, 210 at 47° and 109 at 64°; and at lunar gravity the floor of a 2 m column bubbles its oxygen away at 115% of saturation. The run's clear-sky fields are snapshots at each output time, as the climate lane records (climate/gcm/exoplasim_run.py, CLEAR_SKY), so the forcing reads the run's clear sky by the Sun's elevation from them and averages it over each window before forming a cloud factor; provisioning's energy extract bins the same snapshots by each window's mean solar hour for its clear-sky day profile, a half-window offset to check there, its totals resting on long means. The founders' review (founders.md, sources.json; 44 searches, most sources known through a search summary, its placements and first community proposals): upper heat tolerance barely answers selection in a fish, so founders are chosen for the heat they already stand and bred for timing, dormancy and growth; the gentlest shores lie between 45° and 65°; the hard, clear, phosphorus-poor water favours stonewort meadows and a food web fed from the floor after a rich first decade; Earth's lakes confirm plants first, crayfish next and predators after, with the meadows fenced until they hold and predators that take crayfish small and moulting; the leads are warm-water stoneworts, greater duckweed and heat-tolerant azolla, water fleas and bladder snails, redclaw crayfish on open shores, spangled perch, jade perch and tilapia of the GIFT lines, stocked eels, and mixed cichlid stocks for the crater lakes. Open: stoneworts at 30–35 °C and through 110–260 warm dark hours, for which no measurement was found; the seas' chloride and magnesium for animals; breeding in the dark; and the companion review of the land's founders. The discussion's working direction for founders, aggressive colonisers with microbes, algae and water plants first, crayfish next and their predators after, is the review's premise and waits for the author's word to enter this register as a direction. |
| The community simulator built first for the study is shelved and preserved, tested before its commit, and the study turns to a bounded review of founder species: which species, what makes each easy to select, where each goes and in what order. | Author, 2026-11-09 ("Alright shelve the simulator but preserve it. It could be a useful then. So test it and make sure it works before you commit it. Then focus on the bounded review.") | Done on domain/ecology (20719673; biosphere/ecology/littoral.py). Sixteen test cases show carbon, phosphorus, nitrogen and oxygen closing through every exchange, the stage-structured maturation rate meeting its analytic limits and each mechanism acting in its direction, and a further test drives it with a shore's series from the forcing; make check passed on the worktree (5,267 passed, 31 skipped). Its species traits are placeholders, so it gives no result about the Moon, and it returns when founders have measured rates. The review is the row above (843b3727). |

## The waters' warmth mapped at the design climate, and the calm-air exchange at lunar gravity, 10 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The waters' temperature is mapped at the current lock, the corrected design climate, in the manner of the moisture map. | Author, 2026-11-10 ("can you give me a waters temperature map similar to the moisture map?"; "At current lock."), after asking how shallow water reaches 38–44 °C at the 301 K lock | Done on domain/ecology (910f09c8; research/studies/littoral_community: water_map.py, results/water_map.json, schema terluna.research.littoral-water-map/1; the sheet drawn by visualization/water-temperature-map/render.py and kept on the research drive, session-tools/water_temperature_map/). The littoral forcing is carried to every T21 cell of the corrected design climate's years 10–19 as mixed layers of water. On average every water stays under 34 °C; the seas' and large lakes' upper 50 m averages 29.8 °C (23.7–32.6 °C by cell). In the hottest hundredth of hours, about seven hours of each lunar day, the seas' upper 50 m stays under 34 °C everywhere and a lake's upper 10 m as one mixed layer under 36 °C, while still layers 4 m and 2 m deep reach 36 °C or more over 18% and 50% of the seas' area, 23% and 51% of the 44,580 km of sea coast and 38% and 89% of the rain-fed lakes' 2.31 million km². Still 2 m water's band means in those hours are 36.9 °C over the seas and 39.1 °C over the land at 0–15°, 35.7 and 36.8 °C at 30–45° and 33.9 and 34.2 °C at 45–60°, and it stays under 36 °C in every cell from the row at 58° poleward. A mixed layer gives a water's body: the lake model's own column at the forcing's five sites puts the surface of a calm 20 m water 1–6 K above the body in those hours (36.95 °C at the 8° sea cell, where the climate model's sea shows 30.91 °C), between the still 4 m and 2 m layers, as the day's mixed layer of 3–6 m in the seas' oxygen study implies. The forcing's shore figures of 9 November trade water with the climate model's 50 m mixed layer, so they are the cool end for a shore beside a calm sea; the study's README now says so. Limits: T21 on 3-day windows; the climate model's light winds (2.1 m/s over the water cells, 0.6–0.9 m/s over the land cells) with no sea breeze and no gust of the boundary layer's eddies; still layers that trade no water; the calm column at five sites only. The map takes the speed of each 3-day mean wind vector as it stands, where the waters' oxygen study raises it by about a fifth to the mean speed: with that raise still 2 m water's hottest hours fall 0.4–0.8 K at three sites (a working calculation on the research drive, littoral-community/gravity_bracket/wind_raise.py), and folding the raise into the forcing and the map is proposed to the author. make check passed on the worktree (5,280 passed, 31 skipped). Whether the waters run too warm at this lock is open with the author, who raised it on 10 November. |
| The lake model's column is not run for every cell now, and the map as delivered stands for now. | Author, 2026-11-10 ("Yeah not worth running right now. This is fine  for now."), of the offer to turn the five sites' calm-surface check into a map | Held. The run is about three hours on three threads, a column of some fifteen seconds for each of the 2,048 cells. |
| The evidence for carrying the calm-air exchange laws over water to lunar gravity is examined in a narrow, bounded study by one subagent. | Author, 2026-11-10 ("do we have any indirect studies, physics we can rely on to determine whether lunar gravity is likely to impact that? Have a subagent do a targeted narrow bounded study."), of the statement that those laws are measured on Earth and scaled by gravity | Done 07:25–07:45 by one agent within its bounds (12 searches, 6 page reads, 34 tool calls); its brief, report, notes, sources with reading levels and transcript are on the research drive (session-tools/agents/lunar_free_convection/). It starts from the climate lane's lunar forms of 26–28 October. Gravity enters the laws only through buoyancy, so with Earth's gas a lunar pond is the same similarity problem as an Earth pond at a sixth of the water–air contrast. Where the Rayleigh number or gravity itself was varied the transfer followed the law: effective exponents of 0.28–0.333 in water (Funfschilling and others 2005, abstract read), 0.27 rising to a third and beyond in a centrifuge at 5–60 g (Jiang and others 2020, read), and a heated plate on parabolic flights at 0.05 g matching the standard correlation, in laminar flow (Lotto and others 2017, abstract read). Mars at 0.38 g: similarity applied to Viking's data (search summaries), and large-eddy simulations at Mars's gravity reproducing the boundary-layer depths radio occultation measures (Spiga and others 2010, read). Chamber measurements of evaporation for Mars and Titan were made at Earth's gravity and corrected by the cube root (search summaries). No experiment on a turbulent horizontal or water surface at reduced gravity was found. The bracket: the lunar transfer by gravity alone is 0.41–0.61 of the Earth law against the cube root's 0.55, the low end from the gust law under a boundary layer no deeper than Earth's and the high end from the lowest exponent; in that law gravity and the layer's depth enter as a product, so the Moon's deeper boundary layer offsets its gravity (the agent's inference). In the lake model's still 2 m water the bracket moves the hottest hundredth of hours by +1.2 to −0.5 K at the 8° sea cell and +0.9 to −0.3 K at the 14° land cell, and gravity's whole effect by the cube root is about 2.5 K there, 38.5 °C against 35.9 °C with Earth's constant unscaled (a working calculation by the lead, littoral-community/gravity_bracket/ on the drive). Open: the boundary layer's gust is absent from the lake model and the map; Ingersoll (1970) and the Ahlers–Grossmann–Lohse review were not opened. |
| The wind inside a 3-day mean is folded into the littoral forcing and the map as the waters' oxygen study treats it. | Author, 2026-11-10 ("Alright. Fold it in."), of the proposal to raise the wind as that study does | Done on domain/ecology (85746366). Folded in as that study's whole treatment since its correction of 18 October: the exchange paired at the climate model's lowest level, 313 m up, with that level's wind floored at 1 m/s and raised by 1.21. The raise alone on the first version's 10 m pairing fails the check against the climate model's own sea: a 50 m layer at three coastal cells stands 1.3–1.5 K below the run's sea with it, 0.7–0.8 K below on the first version and 0.2–0.5 K below on the paired method, and over all water cells the map's 10 m layer now stands 0.3 K below the run's sea. The rebuilt map supersedes the figures of the first row of this section: in the hottest hundredth of hours still layers 4 m and 2 m deep reach 36 °C or more over 26% and 54% of the seas' area, 29% and 54% of the sea coast and 16% and 71% of the rain-fed lakes' area; still 2 m water's band means are 37.3 °C over the seas and 38.1 °C over the land at 0–15°, 36.0 and 35.7 °C at 30–45° and 34.0 and 33.1 °C at 45–60°, and it stays under 36 °C in every cell from the row at 53° poleward; the calm column's surface at the 8° sea cell is 37.13 °C against the body's 30.91 °C. The seas' still water thus runs up to 0.4 K hotter by band than first mapped and the land's about a kelvin cooler, the lakes meeting the lowest level's wind over water (1.6–1.7 m/s at 10 m) where they met the run's 10 m wind over rough ground (0.6–0.9 m/s). In the forcing of 9 November, 2 m of sheltered lake margin trading three tenths of its water a day spends 36 hours of each lunar day above 36 °C in place of 95; the sea shores' figures hold to the degree. The wind record (wind_record.py, results/wind_record.json) puts the raise at 1.19 over the whole grid of the 6.4-hourly record, beside the 1.21 taken, and shows the windows holding a nearly constant 1.74–1.78 m/s beyond their mean vector, so one factor raises calm windows too little. On the paired method the gravity bracket moves still 2 m water's hottest hours by +1.2 to −0.5 K at the 8° sea cell, lunar gravity accounting for 1.2–2.7 K of those hours at three sites. make check passed on the worktree (5,285 passed, 31 skipped). Open, the author's: whether the lake model takes the climate lane's gust law for light wind over water, where it keeps the free-convection term; and a year of the corrected climate written every 6.4 hours, about a quarter of an hour of climate-model time, to measure the wind inside its own windows. |
| The cold-waters study's forcing, which takes the older 10 m pairing, is planned for redoing when the ocean studies are redone. | Author, 2026-11-10 ("Maybe something to plan to redo when we redo the ocean studies (the most recent study predates the reorientation analysis)."), of the flag that the cold-waters study of 4 November pairs its exchange at 10 m as the littoral forcing first did | Planned, not started: to be scoped with the ocean studies' redo on the turned Moon. The littoral forcing's check gives the size to expect in warm water, a 50 m layer 0.2–0.6 K further below the climate model's own sea on the 10 m pairing than on the lowest level's; the cold-waters study's nights are not rechecked. |

## The corrected climate's year written every 6.5 hours, the wind inside its 3-day means and the year files' first humidity records, 10 November 2026

| Decision | Source | Status |
|---|---|---|
| A year of the corrected design climate is written every 6.5 hours, to measure the wind inside its 3-day means. | Author, 2026-11-10 ("Let's do that now. I'll think about the others."), of the proposal of that year, there called a year written every 6.4 hours | Done on domain/climate (43b14b38, the runner's fix; 39c5ee04, the year's record) and domain/ecology (d4b4a37b; research/studies/littoral_community: wind_record.py, inside_windows.py and their products). The run, A28R_301k_design_l15_snow_s938_turned_refit_precession_eddies, is the corrected run's year 10 again from its own year-9 restart, written every 13 half-hour steps, eleven records to each 3-day mean: 17 minutes on four ranks, 2.4 GB. The interval is 6.5 hours; 6.4 was the littoral study's wording for it, since corrected. The year ends on the corrected run's year-10 restart byte for byte, and its surface fields aggregate to that year's 3-day records to rounding, so it holds the weather inside one design year's own 3-day means. The audit before its launch found that a branch from a precessing run would have started the equinox's regression again at zero, 189° back for this branch; the runner now hands on the parent's count. The wind: at 313 m, where the exchange is paired, the mean of the 6.5-hour speeds stands 1.13 times the mean speed of the 3-day vectors over the water cells and 1.16 times over the land, and the forcing's operation, floored at 1 m/s, would need 1.12–1.13 where it takes the 1.21 measured about 0.9 km up on the earlier design case; the speed a window holds beyond its vector grows from 0.95 to 1.57 m/s with the vector's speed. The waters, stepped through that year under the 6.5-hour records and under its 3-day means: left out, the wind inside the windows would warm still 2 m water by 0.25–0.33 K on average and 0.35–0.47 K in its hottest hundredth of hours, and add 19–28 hours of each lunar day above 36 °C within 30° of the equator. The forcing's raise brings the water within 0.07 K in the mean and 0.14 K in the hottest hundredth, a little cool in both, with two more hours above 36 °C (7–8 more inside 15° of the equator); by latitude band the hottest hundredth stands within 0.06 K inside 15° and 0.1–0.4 K low poleward, and single cells scatter by 0.4–0.5 K in the one year. Each window's true mean speed, each cell's own factor and each cell's own excess in quadrature follow the 6.5-hour forcing no more closely, so the forcing and the map keep the raise of 1.21, with this measured error. make check passed on both worktrees (climate: 5,232 passed, 31 skipped; ecology: 5,299 passed, 31 skipped). |
| The littoral forcing and the water map fill each year file's nearly empty first humidity record from its neighbours before taking the windows' humidity. | Found in the audit before that year's launch, 10 November; the climate lane's README ("The humidity in the year files") documents the records | Done on domain/ecology (d4b4a37b), both products rebuilt and the sheet redrawn. The run writes its humidity as the value opening each 3-day window, and each year file's first record holds 2% of the vapour, because the held value is not carried in the restart. The forcing and the map had averaged every record with the next, so two windows in every 120 met air about half as humid as it was. The correction moves the fold-in row's figures of 10 November in their last digit: in the hottest hundredth of hours still layers 4 m and 2 m deep reach 36 °C or more over 26% and 55% of the seas' area, 29% and 55% of the sea coast and 17% and 73% of the rain-fed lakes' area; still 2 m water's band means move by 0.03 K at most, and the rows from which it stays under 38, 36 and 34 °C are unchanged; the calm column's surface at the 8° sea cell is 37.14 °C; the sheltered lake margin's 2 m of water spends 38 hours of each lunar day above 36 °C. The coldest hours move most: the coldest hundredth rises by up to 0.36 K by cell and the five sites' ten-year minima by 0.2–1.1 K. The same reading stands, unchanged, in the waters' oxygen study, whose stored humidity series holds 1.3–2.6 g/kg at each year's first record beside 7.6–17.9 g/kg, and by their code in the cold-waters, coast-water and climate-targets studies; correcting them is open with the author. |
